Measured on a Lightroom Classic 15.6 catalog of 40 RAWs with 46 smart collections: 25 import (19 before), 23 with exactly the photographs Lightroom lists (compared by master file; the two that differ only by a virtual copy's own rating stay out of that count's difference), 2 differ. **Known differences:** Lightroom's word search matches whole words and names lenses from its own table ("TAMRON SP 17-50mm …" where the file says "17-50mm"), so a lens or camera collection can list other photographs than Lightroom did, and the import warns about each such album (`lens words 18`: Lightroom 0, here 7; `lens words TAMRON`: 2 in Lightroom, 0 here). **Still left out and named in the report:** edited, aspect ratio, GPS, flash, virtual-copy status, last modified, video; 9 of the 46 collections of this test used criteria Lightroom itself does not evaluate. ISO ranges ("is in range") import too. Camera, lens, ISO and capture-date albums fill as the background pass reads the files' capture data after the import; the import says so for each of them. Virtual copies keep following their photograph in smart albums.
- **A Lightroom HDR edit keeps its HDR settings.** Importing a Lightroom Classic catalog now keeps, for a photo edited in HDR mode, every `crs:HDR…` and `crs:SDR…` setting (HDREditMode, HDRMaxValue, SDRBrightness, SDRContrast, SDRClarity, SDRHighlights, SDRShadows, SDRWhites, SDRBlend and any other of the family) with the photo's recipe, and the XMP sidecar Rawtonic writes carries them back unchanged; the HDR settings of a Lightroom sidecar already beside a photo are never changed. Rawtonic does not render or export HDR yet: such a photo shows "HDR edit from Lightroom — shown and exported as SDR" in Develop. Photos without these settings render, export and write their sidecars exactly as before.
- **New Landscape mask: select the sky, mountains, water, vegetation, natural ground, artificial ground or architecture of a photo.** Add a layer or a component of kind *Landscape*, then tick the parts it should select; they form one mask, and the parts the photo has are marked (the others stay selectable). One run of the network serves every part of a photo, so changing the parts answers without running it again. Along the sky the parts end where the sky's fitted edge (the Sky mask's) finds the sky: on two full-size RAWs checked at 100 %, this removed the 30–60-pixel halo the network's coarse edge left in the sky above a ridge and a tree line; where the fitted edge is unsure, the parts keep the network's softer edge. Pasted, synced or preset Landscape layers are detected again on each photo, when it opens (shown layers only) or at export. Opening a Landscape mask's settings the first time downloads the Landscape model (119 MB, the Sky mask's network with another head; model pack `pack-2026-11-21`); until then pasted or preset Landscape layers wait without their mask, and their exports say so.
  - **Against Lightroom Classic 15's Landscape mask, on 12 public scenes with human labels:** mean IoU per part, Rawtonic vs Lightroom: mountains 0.57 vs 0.42, water 0.82 vs 0.75, vegetation 0.63 vs 0.53, natural ground 0.55 vs 0.31, artificial ground 0.59 vs 0.58, architecture 0.59 vs 0.57, sky 0.93 vs 0.93; boundary accuracy higher for five of the six ground parts (architecture 0.56 vs 0.58). Measured on 640-pixel photos; on full-size RAWs the edges against the sky were checked by eye only.
  - **Landscape mask: the sky part follows the horizon more closely, and snowfields and glaciers beside the sky stay ground.** Before, sunlit snow, a glacier or a pale roof next to the sky could be taken for sky, and then went missing from Mountains and the other parts. New Landscape masks (also those imported from Lightroom) keep them as ground, while the edge against the sky stays where it was. Landscape masks you saved, pasted or kept in a preset keep working as before.
  - **Known limits:** snow on a mountain top in cloud can count as sky (it is left out of Mountains), and Lightroom's separate Snow part has no equivalent yet. Lightroom shows only the parts it found; Rawtonic shows all and marks those not found.
  - **Speed, against Lightroom Classic 15 on the same four RAWs (Mac mini M4 Pro, ONNX Runtime on the CPU, other work running on the host):** from the click on *Landscape* to the list of parts, Rawtonic took 1.3 s on the first Landscape mask after starting on a quiet host (model loaded with it; 36 MP Pentax DNG; up to 4.7 s under heavy load from other work) and about 0.6 s on the next photos; Lightroom 3.5 s on its first (5.2–7.4 s under heavy load) and 3.0–3.2 s on the next. Ticking another part is answered from the same run of the network: the mask is now kept at the network's grid (at most 1024 pixels long, all the mask holds) and read at each pixel while it is placed, instead of a full-size bitmap made, sent and decoded for every part (on the 36 MP DNG that took ~50 ms to make and ~80 ms to decode); from the click on a part to the frame with the new mask (app log, quiet host), 58–71 ms on the 36 MP DNG instead of 169–431 ms, 66–88 ms instead of 90–148 ms on a 20 MP drone DNG, 58–69 ms instead of 252–278 ms on a 40 MP Fujifilm RAF; under heavy load from other work single ticks took up to 226 ms. The mask is the same within one code of 255 (the grid stretched over the photo with a triangle filter, as stored before). Exports of photos never opened compute their Landscape masks the same way. On photos with a lens profile (a 40 MP Fujifilm RAF) a new mask also skips mapping it through the correction it was made in: every AI mask's recorded correction was read without the render version's way of computing a warp, so it looked made in another one and was mapped pixel by pixel again (~160 ms per new mask on the RAF); Landscape masks now compare the correction's geometry only. Sky, Subject, People and depth masks keep the old comparison, so their exports stay byte for byte as they were.
  - **Lightroom and Camera Raw Landscape masks import** from Lightroom catalogs and XMP presets: each part (Lightroom writes one `Mask/Image` component per part, `MaskSubType` 0 with the part in `MaskSubCategoryID`, as read from Lightroom Classic 15's own saved masks) becomes a Landscape component with that part, computed again on the photo by Rawtonic's Landscape mask, as imported Sky and Subject masks are; add, subtract, intersect, invert and opacity carry over. Lightroom's Snow part has no equivalent: a mask with it is left out, as other masks the import cannot read, and the catalog import's report now names every Lightroom mask it left out. The edge settings Lightroom offers for a Landscape mask are not read yet.
- **Face retouch: Teeth no longer lighten the dark of the mouth; Iris and Sclera keep to the eye; the model downloads say what happens.** These changes apply to edits whose eyes and teeth come from the face-parsing model, which is new in this release (next entry). Edits with Face Mesh's outlines export byte for byte as before.
  - **Teeth.** The parser's teeth outline also takes in the gaps between the teeth, the gums and the black of the mouth, and Brightness and Whiten lightened and reddened them. Each pixel is now weighed by how much it looks like a tooth: its lightness against the mouth's own teeth, and its redness. The outline is pulled in by 0.5 % of the eye distance, and the lift is 1.4× so the teeth themselves stay as light as before.
  - **Iris.** Iris lightens towards the eye white with a roll-off instead of clipping at it. Tones that the contrast step pushes towards the white are eased into the room left below it, so a pale iris keeps its texture instead of turning flat. At 100 it now lightens about as much as Capture One 16.9.1 does at its maximum (before: half as much).
  - **Iris and Sclera masks.** Both are pulled in from the parser's outlines by 5 % and 7 % of the iris radius, so less spills onto the lids.
  - **How it was measured.** 20 public portraits (36 eyes, 20 mouths) with hand-marked eyes and teeth, at each editor's maximum. The Teeth and Sclera settings were chosen on the 10 tuning portraits only and then checked once on the 10 held out. Iris's strength was also set on the tuning portraits (to match Capture One there), but its roll-off was chosen after looking at one held-out portrait, so its held-out numbers are not a blind check. Medians, Rawtonic before → now vs Capture One:
    - Teeth: before, 11 of 20 mouths had more than 5 % of the dark pixels next to the teeth lifted; now none do (Capture One: 1 of 20). In the worst mouth, 3 % of those pixels are still lifted. Of the changed pixels, 0.15 → 0.07 lie outside the teeth (0.09). The glow on the lips, as a share of the lift inside, went from 0.37 → 0.12 (0.15). Coverage 0.96 → 0.90 (0.88). Lightening ΔL* 9.4 → 8.8 (8.8).
    - Iris: lightening ΔL* 2.5 → 5.6 (5.0). Outside share 0.15 → 0.12 (0.17). Glow 0.53 → 0.26 (0.22).
    - Sclera: outside share 0.24 → 0.16 (0.16). Glow 0.28 → 0.19 (0.14). Lightening ΔL* 7.0 → 5.5 (2.9).
  - **Still open.** The glow around Iris and Sclera is larger than Capture One's: an iris circle fitted beside a narrowed eye, and the lids. Deeply shadowed teeth are still missed in places.
  - **Downloads.** The Activity title names the model in the app's language (it said "Eye and teeth model wird geladen" in German). While the face or eye and teeth models download, the Retouch tab shows the percentage of all their files together in place of the button. Each model's download is its own Activity entry, so a Sky download running at the same time does not show its percentage there. A failed download now says that the model could not be downloaded and to check the internet connection, instead of the raw network error. The button stays in place to try again (for the face models it disappeared until the tab was opened again). Report in the notes repository, `benchmark-results/eye7-2026-11-21/REPORT.md`.
- **Face retouch: Eyes and Teeth find the eye whites and teeth with a face-parsing model.** New Iris, Sclera and Teeth edits take their masks from EasyPortrait's FPN face parser (SberDevices; converted by Rawtonic and published under CC BY-SA 4.0 in model pack `pack-2026-11-21b`) instead of Face Mesh's lid and lip outlines with colour models: Sclera is the parser's eye opening outside the iris, Iris the iris circle within it, Teeth the parser's teeth. Where the parser misses an eye or finds no teeth, that eye or mouth keeps the Face Mesh masks. The model (57 MB) downloads only when you click *Download eye and teeth model* under Eyes, or apply a retouch with Eyes or Teeth to other selected photos; until then those sliders wait. Measured through the command line's export path on 10 public portraits held out from all tuning (19 eyes, 10 mouths, marked by hand): the masks cover 0.61 of the eye white with 0.13 of their pixels outside it (Face Mesh masks: 0.38–0.52), 0.95 of the teeth with 0.07 outside (0.60–0.77), and 0.93 of the iris with 0.09 outside; Capture One 16.9.1's and Lightroom's masks covered 0.61–0.64 of the eye white and 0.90–0.92 of the teeth on the tuning portraits. Once the model is installed, opening Retouch on a photo whose retouch has no Eyes or Teeth yet runs the parser on its faces in the background; the skin sliders do not wait for it, the Eyes and Teeth sliders do (37 ms per face with Core ML on an M5 Pro, about 3.7 s per face on ONNX Runtime's CPU provider). Edits whose Eyes or Teeth were set before keep their Face Mesh masks and export byte for byte as before. Misses that remain: a black-and-white mouth (no teeth found), deeply shadowed teeth, and narrowed, older or hair-covered eyes. Report in the notes repository, `benchmark-results/eye4-2026-11-21/REPORT.md`.
- **Library: one Culling panel suggests misfires, documents and closed eyes, and Auto-Stack compares how photos look.** The Library's new Culling panel counts, per criterion, the photos it suggests: misfires (lens cap, ground, pocket, motion smear) and documents (pages, receipts, screenshots), read by the search model from each photo's thumbnail, and closed eyes from the eye check. Each count filters the grid (also as a smart-album rule, *Culling suggestion*, beside *In a stack*), each suggested photo carries a tag, and **Reject** flags the checked suggestions among the photos shown (filters and search apply) as rejected in one undo step; nothing is rejected, hidden or moved by itself, closed eyes are left out of Reject unless checked, and **Not a problem** dismisses a suggestion for the selected photos for good (**Show suggestions again** brings it back). The scores are made by content search's background pass from the same reading of each new photo (no new model; the search model is downloaded only on a click) and kept in the library (schema 30); a library indexed for content search before reads its photos once more for them. Auto-Stack now starts at 2 seconds and, with **Only photos that look alike** (on by default), also needs two consecutive photos the search model has read to look alike (cosine of their readings at least 0.78, adjustable); photos not read yet are grouped by time alone (the dialog says how many), and camera bursts and brackets are grouped by the camera's data as before. Measured through the app on the held-out half of our 368-frame culling set (`benchmark-results/cul2-2026-11-21/REPORT.md` in the notes repository; misfire and document positives are synthetic): misfires precision 0.75, recall 0.64 (14 positives; Lightroom Classic 0.64 / 0.50), documents 1.00 / 0.75 (8 positives; Lightroom 0.80 / 1.00), closed eyes 0.23 / 0.63 (Lightroom 0.25 / 0.25), stacks (pairs of consecutive frames) 0.90 / 0.91 (Lightroom's defaults 1.00 / 0.08); 2 of 154 real frames were suggested as misfires, none as documents. On an M4 Pro Mac mini under heavy load, misfire and document scores took 27 s for 184 photos with thumbnails already made (6.8 photos/s; Lightroom's whole panel about 2.3 photos/s), the eye check 107 s (1.7 photos/s). The inherited Cull Images dialog (blur score and image-hash grouping, which could delete photos) is gone.
- **Export ▸ Provenance (C2PA): a signed provenance manifest in each JPEG, PNG, TIFF, WebP, AVIF or JPEG XL, off by default.** It records the photograph the file was developed from (file name and type, no thumbnail), the kinds of edits (opened, tone and colour, cropped, rotated, local adjustments, retouching, resized, border or watermark) and one entry per area a generative model made, marked `compositeWithTrainedAlgorithmicMedia` like the file's IPTC Digital Source Type; the photo's creator (the library's, else the file's EXIF Artist) is named as author only if you tick it. Sign with your own certificate (a PKCS#12 `.p12`/`.pfx` file with ECDSA P-256/P-384, RSA or Ed25519 keys; its password opens the file once and is not kept) or with one Rawtonic makes on this computer, which verifiers show as an unknown signer, as the panel says. The panel shows what goes into the manifest for the first photo before you export, for every recipe that signs (the author as `dc:creator` in a CAWG metadata assertion); a certificate is checked against the C2PA certificate profile when it is imported (also its RSA-PSS and Ed25519 parameters, the curve its public key names, digital-signature key usage, validity and the order of its chain; the panel shows its names without control or text-direction characters); and an export that cannot be signed (no certificate or an expired one) does not start, from the panel or the File menu (which then says why). A queued export signs only with the certificate it was queued with. Nothing is sent anywhere: no account, no upload, no time-stamp service; the private key stays in Rawtonic's data folder, readable only by your user account, until you remove it. The C2PA reference validator (c2patool 0.27.23) reads JPEG, PNG, TIFF, WebP and AVIF exports as valid (AVIF with the BMFF hash binding, which also binds each box's position) with the expected actions and AI marking, the unknown signer being its only finding for a local certificate (and for test certificates of an authority it does not trust). Signing adds 0.7–3.2 ms to a 24 MP JPEG export (three real exports, Mac mini M4 Pro), 17–50 ms to a 24 MP PNG. A TIFF is now hashed while its strips are written instead of read back: encoding and signing 24 MP of already rendered pixels takes 2–4 ms longer than encoding alone in 8 bit and 37 ms longer in 16 bit (p50; p90 39 ms) when written to memory, 7 ms longer (p90 22 ms) for 16 bit when written to an external SSD (the read-back took 50 and 65 ms longer in the same runs); measured without the render and before the file is synced to disk. Exports with the option off are unchanged byte for byte (280 CLI exports of the preservation matrix on Metal). JPEG XL files carry the manifest in a `jumb` box before the codestream, bound box by box (`c2pa.hash.boxes`); c2patool 0.27.23 reports them valid and a changed byte as a mismatch. Only the main file is signed: mask layers exported to their own files carry no manifest, in any format. Reports in the notes repository, `benchmark-results/c2p1-2026-11-21/REPORT.md` and `benchmark-results/c2p2-2026-11-21/REPORT.md`.
- **Adaptive presets: AI layers in presets and styles work on photos you never open.** Apply a preset or style with Sky, Subject, People or depth range layers to many photos in the Library (or paste or sync them, or give a tethered or hot-folder capture a preset): each photo's selections are now computed in the background, one photo at a time, as opening it in Develop would, and its thumbnail is made again; the Activity panel shows the progress and names every photo whose selection could not be computed, with the reason (a model not installed, a person the photo does not have). Exports compute People and depth range selections a photo still waits for (from a preset, paste, sync or capture), as they already did for Sky and Subject, instead of refusing the photo; a People or depth range component saved without its mask otherwise exports as before. Originals not on this computer (cloud placeholders, a drive not connected) are not fetched for a preset: such photos are named, and their selections are computed when they are opened or exported. An undo or redo of a batch apply computes the selections again. Object selections still wait for your click on each photo. A preset's or style's layers now join the photo's own layers instead of replacing them, as in Lightroom; applying the same one again updates its layers instead of adding them twice. Five built-in adaptive presets with our own values: Sky: deeper, Subject: lift, Background: darker, Portrait: smoother skin, Portrait: brighter eyes. They apply as presets, not styles: their Amount scales their layers' settings from none to full (as Lightroom's Amount does for adaptive presets), and their layers stay yours to edit or delete. Measured with the command line on an M5 Pro (48 decodable RAWs of a batch of 50, never opened, with the layers of Sky: deeper, Subject: lift and Portrait: smoother skin): all 48 exported with their selections, 1.76 s per photo against 0.50 s without the layers (the selections add about 1.25 s per photo); the build before refused all 50 ("open the photograph in Develop"). On the export path, a never-opened photo exports byte for byte as the recipe holding the masks that export computed (Sky, Subject and People, GPU test on a photo with people); Develop and the background queue (which decodes without AI Denoise) were not compared. Exports of existing recipes are unchanged byte for byte: 48 plain exports and 48 with Sky and Subject layers against the build before. The background queue in the app was not run end to end here (tests only); depth ranges are computed by the app only. Report in the notes repository, `benchmark-results/apr1-2026-11-21/REPORT.md`.
- **Develop tool groups and readouts.** Alt/Option-click a group header to open just that group. The header menu offers Collapse All Tools, Expand All Tools and a remembered Solo Mode, with keyboard access via Shift+F10. The white balance loupe shows RGB percentages and the temperature and tint a pick would apply. Hover over the tone curve to see input → output and the histogram level. With layers in use, the tool column shows which layer or Background you are editing.
- **Color editor targets: the readout states the measured result at the export's resolution, the solve corrects itself on it, and the photo follows while you type.**
  - The target's readout no longer states the model's prediction. With the range picked in the photo, it states what the export gives at the picked point: the eyedropper's block is rendered at full resolution with every tool (the zoomed-region path, as an export develops it), in sRGB.
  - When the target is out of reach or outside sRGB, the readout says so with that measured distance. It shows "Measuring…" until the measurement is back. When it cannot measure (with AI Denoise on: a region's source is not the export's), it says so and gives the prediction.
  - The range's own pick reads the colour entering the color editor in that block too. The range's colour is the whole block's mean.
  - After the model's solve, the sliders are corrected on the measurement up to three times, while the distance keeps shrinking. This covers texture at the pick and tools after the color editor.
  - A typed value redraws the photo 50 ms after the last valid keystroke (a hex code once all six digits are in). Return renders it at full quality at once; the measured correction joins the same undo step. Escape restores the photo and the target.
  - **Measured (QA app, full-resolution exports of the saved recipes, five public RAWs, laptop M5 Pro):** distance to the target on the eyedropper's block: sky 0.05, skin 0.13, product L*C*h 0.35, feathered layer 0.18. Before, the product read "reached 0.1" in the preview and exported 2.0 off. The readout was within 0.25 of the export on every task.
  - **Not reached:** a blue toy robot whose colour lies beyond sRGB. The colour its export feeds the color editor is so far from the target that hue (±50°) and lightness stop at their limits. It exports 3.3 off, and the readout says "out of reach, 3.3". Capture One lands 1.4 on the same pick.
  - **Latency (same laptop under load from other jobs; screen polled every ~60–90 ms):** typed hex + Return → settled preview p50 68 / p90 98 ms (before: 116 / 154). A value typed digit by digit settles p50 104 / p90 130 ms after the completing key. Target pick → settled p50 156 / p90 518 ms, where the slow ones are a visible measured correction. A range pick takes p50 388 ms on the first pick in a photo (1.8 s on a photo opened for the first time: the full-resolution source) and p50 187 / p90 222 ms after that.
- **Color editor targets: the renderer now tells the solve which color the color editor receives at the pick.** A range picked in the photo, and its target solve, read the color entering the color editor at that point straight from the renderer: unclipped (a product blue beyond sRGB is its own color, not the display's clipped one), before the ranges' own change (picking a range again through Saturation −90 keeps its color exactly instead of undoing the ranges from an 8-bit sample), and with the mask layer's strength there (a feathered layer is solved at its real influence instead of full strength). The solve now works in the renderer's working colors and moves the colors around the pick, not one mean color; a target beyond sRGB that lies inside the working colors and within the sliders' reach is reached there, and an sRGB export maps it into sRGB (that mapping is not part of the prediction). Targets beyond the working colors (ProPhoto) are flagged. Measured with CLI exports of 12 picks in 9 public RAW photos, the probe stood in for by a ProPhoto export (`docs/benchmark-results/tgt2-colour-probe-2026-11-21`): the readout's prediction matches the export within ΔE00 0.5 (median 0.00) on 60 targets; all 11 in-reach product targets inside sRGB land within 1.6 in the sRGB export (TGT1: 8 of 9, worst 2.4); saturated product colors beyond sRGB land within 2 for 13 of 15 in-reach targets (median 0.15; were 4–13), and the other 2 have targets beyond the working colors, which the readout flags and predicts exactly; on feathered layers (influence 0.5–0.75) 17 of 20 targets land within 2 (median 0.10; every in-reach miss is flagged as beyond the working colors), against 6 of 20 when solved at full strength. In a macOS QA build, picking the blue toy robot again through Saturation −90 kept the range's color bit for bit, and its typed target #0057B8 exported within ΔE00 0.7 (TGT1: 11.0). Targets at the edge of sRGB that the previous solve counted as reached by clipping each channel now show their real distance, since the renderer maps colors beyond sRGB rather than clipping them. Exports unchanged byte for byte on Metal: 280 CLI exports of the preservation matrix (10 RAWs, 28 recipes) and 40 more with color-range and mask-layer-range recipes.
- **Soft masks combine as in Camera Raw and Capture One** (new layers, `maskVersion` 5). Within a layer, an added component now combines with the coverage so far as a + b − a·b instead of the larger of the two, and an intersected one as a·b instead of the smaller; Subtract stays a − b, as in Capture One, so a mask subtracted from itself still cancels exactly. Where two soft edges meet, the dip of an added pair is much shallower: two soft radials side by side covered 0.64 at their crossing, 0.36 below their full centres; now 0.87, 0.13 below (Camera Raw 0.90, 0.10 below, with its own slightly wider radials). Intersecting the same pair now gives 0.41 at the crossing instead of 0.62 (Camera Raw 0.48). Hard edges and single components give exactly the same mask as before. Measured on six RAWs (radial with linear, brush with luminance range, two radials, a radial with itself): the new layers follow a·b and a + b − a·b within 0.001–0.007 coverage on average where both inputs are soft, as close as Camera Raw (0.001–0.019; 0.054 for its radial with an inverted linear) and Capture One (0.002–0.006) follow those laws; before they were 0.08–0.11 away. Layers made before keep max and min and render byte for byte as before, also when Refine edge is set on them later. Copies, pastes and presets carry each layer's version. Report in the notes repository, `benchmark-results/msk1-2026-11-21/REPORT.md`.
- **Color editor: move a color onto a target color.** Pick a color range as before, then set its target: pick the target in the photo, sample it in another photo and use it here, or type it as sRGB hex, R/G/B (0–255) or L\*C\*h° (CIELAB D50). The editor solves the range's own Hue, Saturation and Lightness sliders, so the result stays editable with them and Undo takes it back in one step; nothing new is stored in the edit, and existing edits export as before. A readout says how close the range's color got (ΔE00) and, when the target is beyond the sliders' reach (hue ±50°, lightness ×0.6–1.4, saturation 0–200 %), which slider is at its limit; it also flags targets outside sRGB and picked colors at the edge of sRGB, on a mask layer it gives the full-strength prediction instead of claiming the target reached, and after picking a range again through its own strong change (for example Saturation −90) it says the prediction is uncertain. Picking a range again on a color the ranges already move now selects the color before them, as the renderer does. Measured with CLI exports of 12 picks in 9 public RAW photos (60 targets, `docs/benchmark-results/tgt1-target-colour-2026-11-21`): within ΔE00 0.2 for sky (14 targets) and skin (3), within 2 for 8 of 9 product targets in sRGB (worst 2.4). Saturated product colors beyond sRGB, which the picker reads clipped, missed by ΔE00 4–13 (5 targets); the readout warns for them, but cannot predict the result.
- **Library: photo info and Canon CR2 thumbnails read far less of a cold RAW, which matters most on a network share.** The Library's photo info (exposure, lens, ISO) copied the whole RAW first, so a cold folder read every byte of every photo. Photo info of a mapped TIFF-based RAW or a RAF now reads only the file's tags: on six cold mapped RAWs (Fujifilm RAF, Canon CR2, Nikon NEF, Sony ARW, 12–56 MB) it read 2 MB instead of 184 MB from an SMB share and took 0.3 s instead of 8.8 s over a link shaped like a wired LAN (about 1 ms round trip; 0.6–0.8 s instead of 22.9 s shaped like Wi-Fi), 0.14 s instead of 0.21 s from a local NVMe disk. A never-edited RAW's thumbnail no longer copies or reads ahead whole files where it only needs metadata and the embedded preview. Before, the camera crop read all of the raw data of Fujifilm, Nikon, Olympus, Panasonic, Samsung and Sony RAWs (copied for a decode that only reads sizes), the embedded preview of Canon CR3 and Panasonic RW2 files was copied out of the whole file, and the check whether a stored preview can be shown (Canon CR3, Sony ARW and Fujifilm RAF, with a graphics card) and finding the photo's camera (when develop defaults are set) read whole files ahead. For the same six files plus an RW2 and a CR3, these steps in the Library's order read 31 MB instead of 536 MB from the share and took 3.0 s instead of 25.8 s LAN-shaped (6.1 s instead of 63.7 s Wi-Fi-shaped, 1.06 s instead of 1.46 s from the local disk); on 362 of 363 RAWs they brought 1.8 GB of the files into memory instead of 11.1 GB (pages read, the system's read-ahead included). Still read in full: a file that cannot be mapped, a malformed TIFF and a RAW without a usable embedded preview (its thumbnail is developed from the raw data). Photo info, embedded previews and camera crops were identical before and after on the 362 of 363 test RAWs (12 formats) the tools could open, 22 of them without an embedded preview; thumbnails developed from the raw data were not compared. Measured on Linux with a user-space Samba share and KDE's SMB client, not on macOS or Windows; reports in the notes repository, `benchmark-results/crd1-2026-11-20/REPORT.md` and `benchmark-results/crd2-2026-11-21/REPORT.md`.
- **The import dialog and never-edited thumbnails read only what they show, also from a network share.** For each photograph it showed, the import dialog read the whole RAW: the large view's 100 % size and its capture info copied the file first, the grid copied Canon CR3 and Panasonic RW2 files to find their previews, and the preview probes handed a Canon CR2's raw data to the JPEG reader, which copies all of it before it gives up. They now read the file where they need it; from a memory card the import still reads each file into memory, so pulling the card cannot crash the app. Measured with a tool that makes the dialog's reads with the same functions, one opening of the file per request as the app makes them, on eleven cold RAWs with a usable embedded preview (Fujifilm RAF, Canon CR2 and CR3, Nikon NEF, Sony ARW, Panasonic RW2; 3–56 MB) from an SMB share: the grid read 24 MB instead of 74 MB and took 2.2 s instead of 5.1 s over a link shaped like a wired LAN (4.3 s instead of 12.2 s shaped like Wi-Fi); the large view's preview, capture info and 100 % size read 55 MB instead of 507 MB and took 4.6 s instead of 23.2 s (10.1 s instead of 58.8 s). A RAW without a usable embedded preview is still developed for the grid and read in full, as before. In the same tool on 362 RAWs (12 formats), these steps brought 0.93 GB of the files into memory instead of 11.1 GB (pages read on the local disk, the tool's first mapping read without read-ahead; for the RAWs without a usable preview the tool stops where the app develops them). A never-edited thumbnail now reads its camera crop through the mapping it read the preview from: opening the file again let the share client drop the pages just read, so the crop read them once more (for a Canon CR2 around the header of its raw data). For twelve cold RAWs (the nine and three of 3–8 MB) the Library's steps took 3.8 s instead of 4.4 s LAN-shaped (7.2 s instead of 8.6 s Wi-Fi-shaped) and read 37 MB instead of 45 MB from the share. On those 362 RAWs the tool compared the import's preview pixels before the app's resize and JPEG encoding, the large view's camera JPEG, the capture info and the 100 % size, and the Library's frames, camera, embedded preview and camera crops: identical before and after. Measured on Linux with a user-space Samba share and KDE's SMB client, not on macOS or Windows; report in the notes repository, `benchmark-results/crd3-2026-11-22/REPORT.md`.
- **Film grain costs about half as much per preview frame, and drags with grain on render fewer pixels.** The grain tile is now laid out so that a pixel and its neighbours read one block of it: on an M5 Pro (Metal), film grain added 0.38 instead of 0.71 ms per megapixel of a preview frame (in colour 0.86 instead of 1.91 ms), measured on synthetic frames at 1024 × 682 to 2048 × 1366; Glow and Halation are unchanged. Exports and settled previews are unchanged byte for byte (Metal: 28 grain recipes, 6144 × 4096 exports in 8 and 16 bit, and preview frames). While you drag a control other than grain's, a photo with grain now renders at the size it would have without grain (on a 2× display about 0.4× the pixels), as Lightroom's and Capture One's drag previews are coarser; the grain stays visible but softer during the drag, and the settled frame shows it one rendered pixel per screen pixel as before. Grain Amount keeps a fixed grain-sized target from press to release, including at zero. Grain sliders use the active Live Preview Quality without the extra reduction for other controls. Full Resolution skips live-quality downsampling. Performance still reduces the preview size, and Linux keeps its existing High and Performance size limits. On the Radeon 680M grain had cost about 11.5 ms per drag frame and 43 of 120 frames per two-second drag (measured before this change); that GPU has not been measured with it yet.
- **Glow and Halation cost less while you drag in large previews.** When the preview's short side is at least 1080 pixels (for example with Render at native DPI on), a drag frame now computes Glow's and Halation's contribution once per 4 × 4 pixels instead of 2 × 2, taken at the pixel nearest each block's centre, and applies it to each pixel's own colour. Smaller previews keep 2 × 2, and so do edits with a mask that changes Exposure, Brightness, Whites, Glow or Halation, whose hard edges would otherwise show a fringe while dragging. Film grain stays at 2 × 2. The effects come from blurs that grow with the preview, so a 4 × 4 block is no coarser than halation's blur there. The settled frame after you release is computed per pixel as before. Exports and settled previews are unchanged byte for byte: on Metal, 326 real-RAW exports from the command line and 156 hashes of synthetic exports and settled preview frames from 1024 × 682 to 2048 × 1366 were checked.
  - **Fidelity (rendered synthetic test frames, 2048 × 1366, Glow 60 + Halation 60):** on a gradient with a bright disk a drag frame differs from the settled frame by 0.05 codes on average (99.9th percentile: 3 codes). On a harder scene of thin bars and dots brighter than white it differs by 0.62 codes on average (99.9th percentile: 16 codes), against 0.32 and 10 codes with 2 × 2 blocks at the same size. With a hard-edged Exposure mask on that scene, 4 × 4 would differ in a 32-pixel strip across the mask edge by 4.0 codes on average (99.9th percentile: 96 codes); such frames keep 2 × 2, which differs there by 0.17 codes (99.9th percentile: 9 codes). Over the whole masked frame the 99.9th percentile is 15 codes for 4 × 4 and 5 for 2 × 2.
  - **Speed (synthetic frames on an M5 Pro, Metal):** Glow + Halation drag frames at 2048 × 1366 add +0.04 ms instead of +0.34 ms over a frame without effects.
  - **Radeon 680M, estimated and not yet measured:** per-pass GPU timestamps on that GPU (Vulkan, before this change) put Glow's drag-frame cost at 2048 × 1366 at +2.1 ms, of which the 2 × 2 terms pass took 1.56 ms. Scaling the terms pass as it scaled from 1 × 1 to 2 × 2 gives about +0.8–1.0 ms for Glow or Halation alone; WEFX5 measured +1.96 and +1.89 ms in the app.
- **Film grain costs less while you drag other controls in the default preview.** When the preview's short side is below 1080 pixels (the fit view on most laptops, and Render at native DPI with grain on, whose drags render at the size without grain), a drag of any control other than a grain slider now computes the film grain once per 4 × 4 pixels instead of 2 × 2, and applies it to each pixel's own colour. The grain looks coarser and softer while such a drag lasts.
  - **What stays exact:** a drag of Grain Amount, Size or Roughness shows every pixel's grain, as before. The settled frame after you release is computed per pixel, and exports and settled previews are unchanged byte for byte. Larger previews keep 2 × 2.
  - **Fidelity (rendered synthetic test frames, film grain 40, Size 25, Roughness 50, each frame less the same render without grain):** at 1024 × 682 the drag's grain keeps 93 % of the settled grain's strength at 4 × 4 resolution, and its pattern correlates 0.62 with the settled grain's there (0.79 with 2 × 2 blocks). At full pixel resolution it has 37 % of the settled grain's RMS, against 59 % with 2 × 2 blocks.
  - **Speed (synthetic frames on an M5 Pro, Metal, median of three runs):** film grain drag frames add +0.11 ms instead of +0.14 ms at 1024 × 682 and +0.16 ms instead of +0.21 ms at 1608 × 1072; Glow + Halation + film grain +0.24 instead of +0.27 ms and +0.40 instead of +0.47 ms.
  - **Radeon 680M (Windows, Vulkan, the same synthetic frames, median of three runs):** film grain drag frames add +0.51 ms instead of +0.85 ms at 1024 × 682 and +1.26 instead of +2.43 ms at 1608 × 1072. With Glow, Halation and film grain together they add +1.27 instead of +1.60 ms and +3.22 instead of +4.18 ms, still over the +1 ms goal: Glow and Halation alone add +0.78 and +2.00 ms there.
- **Glow, Halation and film grain cost less while you drag.**
  - **What changes:** while you drag, the preview computes Glow's and Halation's contribution once per 2 × 2 pixels and applies it to each pixel's own colour, as Lightroom's and Capture One's drag previews are coarser. Film grain (the grain of new edits) is computed the same way, from the press of the slider, while you drag any control other than a grain slider: each 2 × 2 block gets the grain field rendered at half resolution. A drag of a grain slider still shows the grain exactly, and so does every drag at the Full Resolution live quality.
  - **What stays exact:** the settled frame after you release is computed per pixel as before. Exports and settled previews are unchanged byte for byte. On Metal we checked 156 hashes of synthetic 6144 × 4096 exports in 8 and 16 bit (including the compile-limited path) and of preview frames from 1024 × 682 to 2048 × 1366, plus 326 real-RAW exports from the command line.
  - **Fidelity, measured on rendered synthetic test frames:**
    - With Glow 60 and Halation 60, a drag frame differs from the settled frame by 0.04 codes on average (99.9th percentile: 2 codes).
    - Film grain, measured as each frame minus the same render without grain, keeps 93 % of its strength at half resolution while dragging. Its pattern correlates 0.83 with the settled grain's. At full pixel resolution it has 58 % of the settled grain's RMS, because the detail within each 2 × 2 block is not rendered while dragging.
  - **Speed (synthetic frames on an M5 Pro, Metal):** wall-clock time per frame from submission until the GPU finishes, CPU work included, added over a frame without effects:
    - Glow + Halation at 2048 × 1366: +0.33 ms instead of +1.62 ms.
    - Glow + Halation + monochrome film grain at 1608 × 1072: +0.44 ms instead of +1.64 ms.
    - Film grain at 1024 × 682: +0.11 ms instead of +0.27 ms.
  - **Radeon 680M, estimated and not yet measured:** with Render at native DPI on, Glow or Halation added +3.0 ms per drag frame and all three +2.5 ms, and 11–15 of 120 previews per drag were missed. Scaling those measurements by the Metal ratios gives about +0.6 ms for Glow or Halation and +0.7 ms for all three. At default settings, grain's GPU work would drop from about +1.6–2.0 ms to about +0.7–0.8 ms.
- **Tone curve: dragging a point no longer shakes the tool column** when the curve is partly scrolled out of view (the press no longer starts a text selection, whose autoscroll moved the column; checked by hand in a macOS QA build — a Linux or Windows web view not checked), and **a double-click on a region split handle resets it** (to 25/50/75), as a slider's double-click does; Enter on a selected handle types an exact value.
- **Linux: at 100 % and closer the settled photo is the visible part, lossless.** Instead of a JPEG of the whole photograph, the editor now receives a lossless PNG of the part on screen once a change settles; drags stay as before. Before/After (split and side by side) shows the same part the same way, and a pan or a larger window that uncovers more asks for the new part. Against the export, fine colour noise at 100 % now differs by more than 15 % in 11 of 80 measurements on 16 test edits (4 noisy RAWs × 4 colour noise settings), down from 45 of 80. The export itself rounded to the nearest 8-bit value misses the same 11 measurements: there the export's finest colour noise is smaller than one 8-bit step. On an RTX 3080 PC with a 1920 × 1200 screen, a settled frame took 96–102 ms from the change to the decoded image (median), down from 853–936 ms for the whole 24 MP photograph. Slider drags started 0.6 s after the previous release were unchanged on a calm machine (median p95 19.0 ms in both versions, 64 drags each; measured before the scope render below was added). The histogram, scopes and clipping warnings still come from the whole photograph at full resolution: rendered once no new change came for 0.15 s, in about a quarter of a second (median, 0.5 s at p95), during which a new change or drag waits. This now holds while the second window is open too, and the second window's own 100 % view is lossless as well: it develops its own frames for its size instead of showing a JPEG of the whole photograph. With the second window open (both windows at 100 %, same test edits), fine colour noise differs by more than 15 % from the export in 7 of 80 measurements in the main window, the same 7 as the export rounded to 8 bits (was 45 of 80), and in 7 of 80 in the second window, whose 8-bit floor is 6 (was 45–46 of 80). The main window's settled frame took 82–87 ms to the decoded image (median), down from 848–928 ms. The second window still shows its own frame later, 858–874 ms after the change (median; was 685–698 ms, when it showed the main window's JPEG), or 659–670 ms with the main window at fit (was 812–833 ms). With colour noise reduction on, the second window's own region still differs from the main window's pixels (by up to 2–9 levels at the 99th percentile, at most 210 on single pixels) and has slightly less colour noise than the export on one test RAW (3 measurements, 0.83–0.84): it was developed from a cut-out of the photograph; fixed for colour noise reduction (see Fixes: zoomed-in regions). Slider drags with the second window open were unchanged (mean p95 20.0 against 20.2 ms, 96 drags each). Measured on an RTX 3080 PC: `docs/benchmark-results/swc2-second-window-regions-2026-11-20/REPORT.md`.
- **The tone curve grows with the tool column.** It is square and as wide as the column instead of at most 210 px high with empty bands beside it, and the column can now be widened to 480 px (was 390) in windows at least three times as wide, so the curve reaches about 450 px; in a smaller window the column shows narrower again and keeps the chosen width for a wider one. Curve points are grabbed within the same screen distance at any curve size. On the RGB curve the photo's red, green and blue histograms are drawn as coloured outlines over the luminance histogram; the Red, Green and Blue curves show their own channel's histogram in its colour.
- **The histogram's channels share one scale**, in the histogram panel and behind the curve: a colour with few pixels in a tone now stays low beside one with many, instead of each channel filling the plot on its own. Clipped ends (a channel blown out or black in most pixels) still reach the top without flattening the rest. Levels' **Auto** is unchanged: it reads each channel's plain values.
- **DNG lens corrections with separate values per colour now act on the camera's colours** (`renderVersion` 14, new edits and never-edited photos). Some DNGs carry a lens distortion correction with separate values per colour, which also corrects lateral chromatic aberration. Rawtonic applied those values to the red, green and blue of the developed image, but the DNG specification defines them for the camera's own colour planes. Each camera colour now moves by its own values. Measured against an independent implementation of the specification:
  - DJI Osmo Action DNG (raw.pixls.us), whose red plane moves 1 px at the corners: the camera colours' edges were up to 0.41 px off before and are now within 0.001 px. The red edge difference was up to 0.28 of full scale before (p99 0.020).
  - DJI FC6310: the edges were up to 0.025 px off before and are now within 0.003 px.
  - DNGs whose correction uses one set of values for every colour (other DJI drones) and all other formats render exactly as before.
  - Edited photos keep their look and export byte for byte as before.
- **The photograph keeps at least 320 px of width** (35 % of a smaller window) beside the tool column and a docked filmstrip: in a small window the filmstrip narrows first, then the tool column, down to their minimum widths, and each comes back to the width you chose when the window grows. Their dividers move from the width they show; widening the tool column takes room from the filmstrip, not from the photograph.
- **AI Denoise 50 is much closer to Camera Raw Denoise 50's strength** (new edits; AI Denoise version 5). Version 4's 50 left about twice Camera Raw 19.0's flat-area noise on ten RawNIND frames (ISO 6400–51200, five bodies). Version 5 tells the network 0.955 × the photo's measured noise at 50 instead of 0.8 × — one constant for every camera, fitted on five of the frames. On the five held-out frames rendered at version 5's 50, against Camera Raw 19.0 Denoise 50 (sharpening 0 / 40): flat-area noise 1.43× / 1.20× Camera Raw's (version 4: 2.42× / 2.02×), so 43 % / 20 % more noise remain; lightness error 1.30× / 1.14× (1.71× / 1.68×); finest detail kept 0.97× / 1.00×. 100 stays at 1.25 ×, about Capture One 16.9 Enhanced Denoise 50's strength; below 50 the slider blends in the result of 50. A photo whose saved edit has AI Denoise at 0 gets version 5 when you turn it on. Not yet at the bar: at equal flat-area noise (interpolated between renders at several strengths) the lightness error stays 6 % / 4 % above Camera Raw's (the network's limit); on an M4 Pro the network takes 1.15 s per tile on the GPU, an estimated 28 s per 24 MP photo for the network alone on first use, against Camera Raw's ≈ 10 s (measured on the same Mac beside other work). Saved edits that use AI Denoise keep their version and export byte for byte as before (versions 4 at 25 / 50 / 75 on two RawNIND frames, before and after, Metal: 6 of 6 identical); the Library does not list version 4 edits under "Older AI Denoise version" (same model, only what 50 means changed).
- **Eyes and Teeth in the face retouch.** The Retouch tab has **Eyes** (Iris and Sclera, for both eyes or, with the Left/Right tabs, the eye on the left or right of the photo; a flipped or turned photo keeps each value on its eye) and **Teeth** (Brightness, Whiten), for all faces or one, applied to other selected photos with the rest of the face retouch. Iris adds texture, light and a little colour without lifting the pupil or rising above the eye white; Sclera lightens the eye white and takes out redness and yellow; Whiten takes yellow out of the teeth towards neutral and never past it, so teeth do not turn blue. The masks come from the face models already used (no new model): Face Mesh's lid and iris outlines checked against each eye's own colours (eye white, iris and lid skin sampled from that eye), the teeth against the mouth's dark and the lips around it. They still reach beyond the eye and teeth in places: on the 10 public portraits the tool was tuned on (CC0, CC BY, public domain; glasses, a black-and-white photo, a group), against hand-marked eyes and teeth, of the pixels a slider at 100 changes, 17 % (Iris), 13 % (Sclera) and 2 % (Teeth) lie outside the marked part (medians; worst photos 40 %, 59 % and 40 %: narrowed or shadowed eyes, where the lower-lid skin is as light as the eye white, and the lips of a black-and-white and a flash-lit photo), mostly a few levels at the rims. At 100, yellow in the teeth falls by about 40 %; no photo's teeth turned bluer. Not yet checked on photos it was not tuned on, nor side by side with Capture One 16.9 or Lightroom. Faces found before this change show a "Find eyes and teeth" button; the sliders work once their eyes and mouth are found. Saved edits render as before.

### Fixes

- **Integrated GPUs (Windows/Linux): Develop's fit view can now show the export's own colour noise reduction.** Before, laptops with an integrated graphics chip (e.g. Radeon 680M) always showed a lighter filter in the fit view: its colour noise left 50–80 % of the measured cells more than 15 % away from the export's at Color 25–100. The fit view now builds the export's filter there when the app's memory has room for the build, and keeps the uploaded photo (up to about 26 MP) between typed Color, Detail and Smoothness changes. **Measured on a Radeon 680M with 24 MP RAWs (app log, the filter's build only, without the 300 ms pause before it and the frame after it):** 0.73–0.80 s per typed change. The app's Private Bytes still rose by 0.88–1.51 GB per change, including other work in that window; the aim is 0.5 GB. Opening a photo or changing its geometry uploads it again; at opening that build took 1.4–3.3 s. On discrete GPUs every build still uploads; in a headless probe on an RTX 3080 it peaked 0.18 GB lower on the GPU than before (0.72 GB instead of 0.90 GB) and took 0.39–0.71 s (before: 0.41–0.43 s). Exports are byte for byte unchanged.
- **Windows: Restart now installs the update.** After **Restart now** (or **Restart Anyway** during an export) in the update notice, Rawtonic closed and the update setup stopped with "Failed to kill Rawtonic … Please close it first then try again" and installed nothing: it checked for the old Rawtonic while that was still ending. The setup now waits up to 20 s until the old Rawtonic has ended. Tested on a Windows 11 laptop (Radeon 680M, standard user) with two test builds: an idle Restart now and two Restart Anyway during a 16-bit TIFF export each installed and started the new version by itself, left only complete TIFFs and offered to resume the export. The wait is in the setup of the release being installed, so it also applies when 0.1.0-alpha.18 updates to this release; that pair itself was not run. A Rawtonic that does not end within 20 s meets the setup's old check, as before.
- When the Library rebuilds the thumbnail of an unedited RAW after reconnecting, it uses the camera crop from the original file.
- **A photo whose LUT file is missing no longer renders black.** When the LUT an edit names could not be loaded (moved, deleted or on a disconnected drive), the preview, thumbnails and exports read an empty placeholder table instead and came out black at full LUT intensity. Such a photo now renders without the LUT, as RapidRAW 1.7.1 does (ported from RapidRAW); it comes back once the file is there again, and Library thumbnails made while it was missing (or black before this fix) are made again. Exports of photos whose LUT loads are unchanged byte for byte (6 of 6 16-bit TIFFs with display- and scene-referred LUTs compared); Library thumbnails of photos with a LUT are made once more after updating.
- The first edit and batch edits use the camera's framing when a RAW reconnects. With the original still in the cloud, they use the latest cached crop for that photo. Batch commands report when a photo must be downloaded to apply its Develop defaults before saving a first edit.

- Tone curve arrow edits still settle into Undo history if a key release is missed. When you drag a region split handle back to its starting position, the photo follows the handle.

- Color Editor targets keep small corrections that reach the target and take a fresh sample after RAW processing preferences change. The Levels pickers retry if the graphics processor changes during sampling. When an older preview lacks camera-crop information, Develop reads it from the local RAW.

- **A queued batch with an invalid record no longer blocks later exports.** The queue lists the failed photos with the reason and continues with the next batch. If the queue database itself cannot complete the update, the affected batches stay queued for the next start or resume.
- **Raised Shadows keeps deep shadows crisper, lowered Highlights keeps more cloud texture** (new edits; saved ones keep their look). Shadows +100 lifted the darkest tones about as much as their surroundings, so dark detail inside the shadows looked flat; Highlights −100 flattened texture in bright clouds and lit walls a little more than Capture One. Both now add a term that follows each pixel's own brightness: the deepest tones keep a little more of their contrast against what is lifted around them, the brightest keep more against the tones just below them. Halos and noise stay within the atlas's limits, and the overall lift or pull is about as before. Measured on the slider atlas (24 held-out photos, matched by effect): shadow detail at Shadows +100 went from 0.13 to 0.07 below the better reference editor (now within the atlas's limit of Camera Raw's), bright detail at Highlights −100 from 0.10 to 0.04 below Capture One's.
- **Lowered Whites leaves less of a light rim beside strong edges** (new edits; saved ones keep their look). Whites −100 kept the bright side of a strong edge (a sky against branches or a roofline) a little lighter than the rest of the sky. The bright side now darkens nearly as the open sky does; fine texture in bright areas keeps nearly all of its detail. Measured on the slider atlas (24 held-out photos, matched by effect): the light rim at Whites −100 fell from 0.69 to 0.39 L* above Camera Raw's (Capture One's rim is larger), bright detail stays between Camera Raw's and Capture One's. At the same slider value Whites −100 darkens the brightest tenth about 0.7 L* more than before (still less than Camera Raw).
- **Raised Shadows now move at Camera Raw's pace** (new edits; saved ones keep their look). Shadows +50 lifted the darkest quarter of a photo by 4.0 L* where Camera Raw's Shadows +50 lifts it by 5.7 (medians over our 48 test photographs), so the first three quarters of the slider did too little, while +100 already matched (14.4 against 14.3). The slider now gives what the old one gave at +35 for +25, at +61 for +50 and at +83 for +75; +100 and lowered Shadows stay as they were, and so does the look of the lifted shadows at a given lift. The new pace was set on half of the photographs and checked on the other half: there +25, +50 and +75 lift the darkest quarter by 2.4, 5.2 and 9.6 L* against Camera Raw's 2.2, 5.3 and 9.3 (were 1.6, 3.8 and 7.8). Shadows copied or pasted, presets and Edit all selected render the same lift between edits made by this version and by development builds since 0.1.0-alpha.18 (tone versions 20 and 21, both ways); between those and edits saved by 0.1.0-alpha.18 or earlier, the Shadows amount is kept and each edit renders it with its own Shadows response. Develop defaults saved before keep their look. Resetting the photo's Exposure group keeps its layers' Shadows as they render (a photo whose layers use the older tone sliders keeps its older response). Lightroom catalog imports keep their Shadows value, and a Lightroom preset's Shadows now lifts like that value on new edits (before, its +50 lifted as the old +50; Camera Raw's pace is matched on average, not photo by photo).
- **Capture One imports bring Shadow over close to the lift it had in Capture One (an approximation).** Capture One's Shadow was multiplied by 2.5, which lifted the darkest quarter less than Capture One does up to its +30 (its +20 by 4.0 L* instead of 5.3) and far more above it (its +40 became Rawtonic's +100: 14.4 L* instead of 9.8). It now takes the Shadows value whose lift is closest to Capture One's on average (medians over 48 photographs: Capture One's +10 is about Rawtonic's +26, +20 about +48, +40 about +75). This is an approximation measured on the whole photo's Shadow (layers take the same table, their rendered lift was not compared): single photos differ, and with a table made from half of the photographs, the other half's median lift is 90–102 % of Capture One's measured lift from +20 to +60 and 87 % at +10; lowered values are estimates, since Capture One was measured only at −50 and −100 there (81–94 % of a Capture One lift interpolated between 0 and −50). Capture One's Shadow reaches further than Rawtonic's at both ends (beyond about +62 and −43), and such a value comes over as +100 or −100 with a line in the import report. Applies to catalog and session imports and their layers, and to styles.
- Reopening a photograph while its queued AI selections finish keeps its existing Undo steps. Reusing an AI selection from the saved edit requires the same source decode. A first edit of a cloud-only photograph reports when the camera crop was unavailable to the editor.
- The Levels black and white point pickers keep both parts of a precise color sample on the same graphics pipeline while preview variants compile.

- **Exports with Super Resolution no longer wait while the app retries the optional fp16 model download.** Opening Export again during a batch, after that 1.2 MB download had failed, held the model until the download finished or timed out, so the running export paused on a slow link; exports now keep using the loaded model meanwhile. Photos switch to the fp16 model only after its GPU check has passed, as before. The enlarged pixels of each graph are unchanged (measured byte-identical on CUDA for the fp32 and the fp16 graph).
- **RAW and JPEG sidecars stay with their own photos when names overlap.** On the same drive, moving a RAW with XMP beside a JPEG with its own XMP keeps both packets, including through Undo and Redo. Import uses a numbered copy name when a RAW would take an existing stem sidecar. Ambiguous dotted names leave the shared XMP untouched, and camera RAWs saved as TIFF keep the RAW sidecar naming rule.

- **Develop updates open JPEG and TIFF photos when their preprocessing preferences change.** The photo refreshes in place with its current edits and the same preprocessing as the export. Small changes from a preset's Amount control also refresh AI Denoise when they change its stored strength.
- **Develop: the Levels black and white point pickers read what Levels receive with 16-bit precision.** The pickers sampled 8-bit display values, rounded to whole levels, so a patch at 128.5 set the black point to 128 and left a cast that a steep channel gamma made visible. They now read the value before Levels, undithered, in 16-bit steps from the preview frame the photo is shown with (including a frame whose colour noise reduction was applied at full resolution), and round as before (black up, white down). Where a graphics variant without this read stands in, the picker uses the 8-bit value as before.
- **Library: adding a folder of RAWs needs half the memory again, and its thumbnails come twice as fast.** Since 2026-11-12 every grid thumbnail converted the camera's whole embedded JPEG to floating point before reducing it (290 MB for a 24 MP photo, on each of the four thumbnail threads), and reduced it twice, once per tile size. The tiles are now reduced straight from the 8-bit JPEG through a table of its linear values (the same pixels, bit for bit), and, at the default tile sizes, the small tile from the medium one (in a stripe test 1.00–1.01 of the direct reduction's contrast; a small tile set to over 40 % of the medium one is still reduced from the photo). Measured adding 1,000 RAWs (29 cameras, MacBook Pro M5 Pro, 3 runs each): peak memory footprint 2.04–2.28 → 1.01–1.15 GB, peak RSS 2.34–2.68 → 1.23–1.30 GB, first grid ready 1.6–2.7 → 1.6 s; scrolling through all 1,000 thumbnails 75.3–75.6 → 36.6–37.5 s (peak RSS 3.1–3.4 → 1.65–1.72 GB). Lightroom Classic builds its 1,000 Minimal previews in 24–29 s; that gap stays open. Exports are unchanged.
- **New Sky, Subject, People and Depth Range masks on photos with a lens profile are placed in a fraction of the time.** A mask made after the photo's lens profile correction was mapped through that correction and back again every time it was placed, because the stored record of the correction left out how the current version computes it. New layers (`maskVersion` 6, also Lightroom and Camera Raw imports) now see that it is the same correction and place the mask as it was made. (New Landscape masks, stored on their network's grid, already skipped it.) Measured on a 40 MP Fujifilm X-T5 RAF with its camera's lens profile, with the app's own Sky mask and two Landscape masks stored at full size, which are placed the same way (Mac mini M4 Pro, CPU, 15 runs each, decoding the stored mask included): one mask at full resolution, as an export places it, p50 3.17–3.22 s → 0.15–0.19 s, and at a 2560 px preview 372–413 ms → 38–79 ms. The remap had moved those masks' edges by a fraction of a pixel: at full resolution 0.22–0.66 % of the pixels differ, 0.03–0.18 % by more than one code (up to ~100 codes on hard edges inside the photo, up to 255 within 16 px of its border), IoU at 50 % ≥ 0.9997. Saved layers keep the old placement, so their exports stay byte-identical: full-resolution CLI exports of that RAF with a saved layer (no version and version 5) of each kind — Sky, Subject, Foreground, People, Depth Range, Landscape at full size — are byte for byte those of the previous build, and the whole export takes 2.1–2.4 s instead of 5.1 s with a new layer (the preservation matrix without AI masks: 280/280 CLI and app); a mask you add as a component to an older layer still uses the old placement, a new layer the new one. Changing the lens or transform correction afterwards still moves every mask along.
- **Library: an adaptive preset, paste or sync also computes the AI masks of the active photo.** The photo shown in the Navigator was written by its editor and left out of the background queue, so its Sky, Subject, People or depth masks waited until it was opened in Develop or exported. It is now queued after its save, and when its masks are computed the open photo takes them into its current settings: a slider moved meanwhile stays, nothing is undone, and a later preset no longer writes the mask back as waiting (also when you moved on to another photo meanwhile: its undo steps stay as they were). Photos developed with AI Denoise are left to Develop and the export, which compute their masks on the denoised photo; the Activity panel names them. A first edit made in the editor of a never-edited RAW whose original is only in the cloud, with no camera crop cached by its previews (a RAF previewed before the camera's aspect ratio was read), now shows the same Activity warning as a batch apply: "Camera crop not applied …".
- **Library: right-click ▸ Apply Preset lists Rawtonic's built-in styles and adaptive presets.** The menu listed only your own presets, so an adaptive preset (Sky: deeper, Subject: lift, Background: darker, Portrait: smoother skin, Portrait: brighter eyes) could be applied to many photos only after opening one in Develop. It now has a Built-in folder (hidden with the setting that hides them in Develop), and the photos' AI selections are computed in the background as before. Measured in the QA app on 30 never-opened RAWs: each of the five presets applied from the Library computed its masks for 29 photos (the Library's active photo, which the editor writes itself, is not queued: its masks are computed when it is opened in Develop or exported); the masks of two photos equal those of a per-photo Develop apply (IoU 1.0 on all five layers).
- **Applying, pasting or syncing settings to many photos no longer makes macOS download originals that are only in the cloud.** A batch apply read each photo's file for its EXIF (and, for imported Lightroom gradients and brushes, to place them), and saving it fingerprinted the original and made its thumbnail again, which downloaded cloud-evicted RAWs. Cached EXIF is used when there is any; the file of an evicted original is never opened. A never-edited photo takes its first edit without the download (unless you have set Develop defaults, which need the camera's name from the file): the camera's crop comes from what the photo's previews cached; when nothing is cached the edit is written without that crop (only a camera set to another aspect ratio has one) and the Activity panel names the photo, "Camera crop not applied … download the original and set the crop in Develop". A photo whose settings need the file (a preset's gradients and brushes, a camera TIFF, a changed lens choice the cached EXIF cannot resolve) is left untouched and named in the failure report: "original is in the cloud — download it to apply …". Photos on this computer behave as before; exports are unchanged. Covered by tests with a simulated placeholder; not run against a real iCloud or OneDrive placeholder.
- **Capture One import keeps layers that were never painted, and says what of its lens correction it does not carry.** A Capture One layer that was made but never painted (an empty adjustment layer with its settings, e.g. −3 EV waiting for a brush) was left out; it now arrives with its name, on/off and settings and an empty mask, so it does nothing until you paint it, as in Capture One. A filled layer covers the photo; a layer whose mask Capture One did not write is kept the same way and named in the import report. Layers whose mask Rawtonic cannot read (e.g. combined masks) are still left out and named in the report. Capture One's *Generic* lens profile (no profile of the lens) no longer turns into a lens profile Rawtonic picked: the photo keeps Rawtonic's default lens correction, and the report says so and names Generic Distortion or Light Falloff amounts, which have no measured equivalent. Chromatic Aberration off in Capture One turns Rawtonic's CA correction off (automatic and lens data), also when that is a photo's only edit; on keeps it on. The layers and the Generic lens were checked on C1Q1's 4-photo Capture One 16.9.1 session (notes: benchmark-results/c1i2-2026-11-21/); the CA switch only by recipe tests (no photo of that session has CA off).
- **Undoing "Move contents" in Session Folders gives the folder role back.** After Capture → a new folder with *Move contents*, Edit ▸ Undo moved the photos back but left the Capture role on the new folder, so new imports went to the empty one; the role now returns to the old folder with the photos, and Redo gives it to the new folder again. A folder chosen for the role in between keeps it; when the old folder has another role by then, the photos come back, the role stays, and a notice says so.
- **A JPEG or HEIF beside its RAW no longer takes over or overwrites the RAW's XMP sidecar.** `IMG_1234.xmp` belongs to the RAW, as in Lightroom Classic; a JPEG, HEIF or TIFF that shares its name now has a sidecar of its own, `IMG_1234.JPG.xmp`. Before, a JPEG added without its RAW took the RAW's rating and label from `IMG_1234.xmp` and wrote its own edits back into it. With both in a library, neither could write its XMP at all. Moving or renaming the RAW alone left its sidecar behind with the JPEG. Now each photo of a pair reads, writes, moves and renames only its own sidecar, also in folders opened without a library. A JPEG moved with its `IMG_1234.xmp` into a folder that holds its RAW takes `IMG_1234.JPG.xmp` there. A JPEG with no other file of its name keeps `IMG_1234.xmp`. Two RAWs of one name (a CR3 and a DNG) still do not write their shared sidecar. Existing libraries need no step and no file is touched: a JPEG that took values from its RAW's sidecar keeps them, and its next edit goes to its own sidecar. If a JPEG already overwrote its RAW's sidecar, the RAW offers the sidecar for review (Use XMP / Keep library) as for any change made elsewhere.
- **Undo after a recomputed AI selection or an Object prompt shows the right mask, also with an export waiting in the queue.** The preview keeps each AI mask bitmap it sent once and refers to it afterwards; that reference named only the mask's component, and a queued export carrying another version of the same mask replaced it. Recompute a migrated Sky selection (or add an Object prompt), queue an export behind another job and Undo before it runs: Develop then showed the newer mask for the undone state. The reference now names the bitmap's content, and a few versions are kept per mask (those queued exports carry are dropped first), so the preview and every queued export each get their own. A queued export renders the mask it carries, as before (tested on the recipe the renderer receives, not by comparing exported files).
- **A Library edit saved in the library keeps its Undo when the photo's folder is write-protected.** Reset, paste, sync, series match and the other batch edits in a catalog or session whose photo folder cannot be written saved the edit in the library, then failed to write the sidecar file next to the photo, reported the whole edit as failed and dropped its Undo step. They now say "Saved in the library; the sidecar file next to the photo could not be written: …" as a warning and keep the Undo step; the sidecar follows on the next save. Originals are never touched.
- **Culling suggestions and content search read the same pixels every time.** A photo whose thumbnail was being made while content search's pass read it was read from the thumbnail in memory, any later reading from the cached thumbnail file (1280 px, compressed), so its misfire and document scores depended on timing: re-reading 92 photos changed 67 scores, and one held-out misfire moved across the threshold between two runs. Every reading now uses the thumbnail as it is cached (also when the cache could not be written); first reading and re-reading are identical (368 of 368 photos). Libraries already scored are read once more in the background for their scores (dismissed suggestions stay dismissed). Smart Albums drop suggestions from older scores as soon as the library opens, including when background indexing is paused. The misfire threshold, re-tuned on the tuning half of our culling set through the app's own scores, is 0.01175 (was 0.01153 from the camera JPEGs; both flag the same photos of the set); held out through the app: misfires precision 0.75, recall 0.64 (Lightroom Classic 0.64 / 0.50), documents unchanged (`benchmark-results/cul3-2026-11-21/REPORT.md` in the notes repository).
- **The eye check reads about a quarter faster, with identical results.** It decodes the next photo's preview while it reads the faces of the current one, and makes the face finder's two small copies of the photo at the same time. Warm, on 184 photos of our culling set on a Mac mini M4 Pro shared with other work: 2.24 → 2.85 photos per second (median of six runs each; Lightroom Classic's whole Assisted Culling about 2.3), every eye call the same in all 42 compared runs. It holds one more decoded preview meanwhile (peak memory during the check about 0.35 GB higher).
- **Develop: the preview keeps colors beyond sRGB (deep blues, saturated greens and reds) as the export does.** The fit view, the drag preview, Standard previews and thumbnails are made from a reduced copy of the photo. That reduction set every negative channel (how the working space holds colors beyond sRGB) to 0, while the full-resolution export keeps it. Such colors therefore looked different in Develop than in the export, and a color range picked on them could miss the export's pixels: a blue toy on a Sony A100 photo showed as #0044DC in Develop and exported as #004EA4, and a range picked on it did not change the export. The reduction now keeps those channels; photos without such colors are reduced exactly as before. Exports are unchanged byte for byte (7 of 7 16-bit TIFFs compared). Standard previews and thumbnails cached earlier are made again with the new colors the next time you view the photo while its original is connected (a Library tile once its preview is renewed or when it is developed from the RAW); no rebuild is needed. While an original is offline, its earlier preview and tile stay until it is back.
- Zoomed multi-view and second-window previews keep Light flare consistent when zoom changes slightly and on tall panoramas. Oversized preview regions fit the GPU texture limit while keeping the whole displayed area.
- **Face retouch survives a later flip, turn, straightening, lens or perspective correction.** After retouching faces (skin, Eyes or Teeth) and then flipping, turning, straightening or correcting the photo's lens or perspective in Crop, the preview and the export left out the whole face retouch until the Retouch tab was opened again. The faces are now placed through the geometry they were found in, so their retouch stays on the same pixels of the photo; edits without such a change render exactly as before (see Changes to existing edits).
- **Face retouch in Library thumbnails and on other photos.** Thumbnails cached while a face retouch was skipped after a later flip, turn, straightening or lens change, and RAW thumbnails with a face retouch (drawn on a reduced base: a preview proxy or a fast decode), are made again and show the retouch (a photo whose original is disconnected and has no preview proxy keeps its old tile until the original is back). A thumbnail built from a reduced preview now places the face retouch where the full render does (it used full-size positions). Apply the face retouch to other photos no longer drops a target photo's own protection strokes without saying so: a photo whose strokes were painted before its geometry changed is reported as not written (clear its strokes in its Retouch tab and paint them again if needed, then apply again). Protection strokes are one photo's face geometry and are never copied to other photos (copy and paste of settings already leaves the face retouch out).
- Newly imported Lightroom and Camera Raw masks use the composition laws measured in Camera Raw: screen for Add, multiplication for Intersect, and `a × (1 − b)` for a Subtract component at full strength (imported as an Intersect with the inverted component), including Camera Raw's residual coverage where a soft mask is subtracted from itself. This is measured in Camera Raw; Lightroom's own renders were not compared. Camera Raw ignores a gradient's or range's strength value when it adds or subtracts (also for Lightroom's Intersect, written as an inverted Subtract), so a Subtract with a fractional value imports as the full `a × (1 − b)`; a brush eraser imports as `a × (1 − e)`, and an eraser aggregate after other additive components keeps the layer instead of being left out. Already imported layers keep their look. Only a brush stroke subtracted with a value between 0 and 1, or a computed mask (subject, sky, colour range) subtracted below full strength, still uses `a − b` and is flagged in the import report.

- **Raised Shadows no longer greys the light background between thin dark lines** (new edits; saved ones keep their look). Between dark strokes on a light ground (text, a fine grating, leaves against the sky) Shadows lifted the light gaps as if they were shadow: +9.8 L* between 5 px strokes 5 px apart at Shadows +100 on a 7000 px test chart (under 0.1 L* in Camera Raw and Capture One), and +2.2–2.6 L* around digits (p99, up to +0.03 L* in Camera Raw and +0.14 L* in Capture One). On that chart, whose ground is L* 90, they now move by at most 0.04 L*. The limits: only light tones are protected. What is bounded is the lift a light pixel takes from its dark surroundings: it loses 0.4 EV of that lift for each EV it lies above about L* 57, and that lift takes it no further than about L* 87 (the lift of its own brightness, as on a flat area, it always keeps, also beyond L* 87; Blacks' lift is not bounded). So darker gaps keep part of the surroundings' lift (gaps at L* 84, the same pattern at Exposure −0.7: 2.4 L* of 11.8), and gaps below L* 57 all of it. On two real photographs nine in ten of the light pixels next to dark detail now move at most 0.3–0.6 L* (were 2.1–3.3), the top hundredth 0.7 and 4.6 L* (were 4.2 and 10.1). Fine detail keeps its tonal order: in our test (lines 1 to 8 px wide at 1080 and 2160 rows on five grounds, Shadows +4 to +100 with Exposure, Blacks and Whites varied) a brighter line is not rendered darker than a darker one beyond the 0.01 EV the tone sliders are held to. The strokes themselves and broad shadows are lifted as before. Across 48 test photographs every Shadows measurement keeps its rating; fine shadow detail at +100 is 0.13 below Camera Raw's in our measure (as before). With Blacks raised as well, raising Shadows no longer takes Blacks' lift back from fine light detail inside dark areas: Blacks' lift is now read the same way at every Shadows value, and with Blacks set the lift that Shadows and Blacks give a pixel together is never less than the one it gets at Shadows 0 (a rule of this tone step alone: tools that act after it, such as a tone curve with a falling segment, can still turn a lighter tone into a darker one). Before, both lifts were read at one brightness level of the surroundings that widened over Shadows 0 to +12, and where the wider surroundings are lighter Blacks' lift fell on the way: on a 4000 px test frame with Blacks +50, a 1 px line at −3.5 EV inside a 27 px dark stroke on a light ground rendered 2.8 L* darker at Shadows +12 than at +2 (saved edits keep that), and with the new bounds a line at −2.5 EV 0.65 L* darker at +6 than at 0. Now no line of that test (1 to 5 px wide at −3.5, −2.5 and −1.5 EV in strokes 15, 27 and 51 px wide, Blacks 0, +25 and +50, Shadows 0 to +20 in single steps; at Blacks +50 also with the brightness statistics of a dim low-contrast photo and of a night scene, which the sliders' response adapts to) renders below its Shadows 0 value, and between two raised Shadows values the largest fall is 0.004 L* (with those statistics 0.03 L*: a 4 px line that rises 0.06 L* by Shadows +8 and gives 0.03 back by +20). The falls between raised values are results of this test, not a rule for every photo; only the comparison with Shadows 0 is built in.
