With Blacks at 0, Shadows +12 and above render as before this change to within rounding (16-bit exports of the test chart and four photographs at +12 to +100: at most 0.23 % of the values differ, by 1 or 2 of 65535 on the photographs and up to 5 on the chart); Shadows below +12, and any Shadows value with Blacks raised, render differently from the first build of this fix (at Shadows +50 with Blacks +30, 66–96 % of the values of those exports differ): that build's response (tone version 20) was never in a release — 0.1.0-alpha.18 writes version 19 — and is changed in place, so only edits saved by development builds since it was merged render differently.
- **Develop now matches the export for old fractional AI Denoise amounts, and for changed decode preferences.** An edit saved with a fractional AI Denoise amount (a fine Option drag, for example 50.4) was shown in Develop at 50 but exported at 50.4; Develop now renders the stored amount, the slider shows it rounded and the edit is rewritten only when you move the slider (paste copies the stored amount). Changing the linear RAW mode, the preprocessing noise reduction or sharpening, or Apple RAW 9 in Preferences now makes the decodes Develop and the exports hold under the old setting count as another decode, so neither reuses them. With Apple RAW 9 on, a photo whose sensor AI Denoise cannot handle (X-Trans, Foveon, linear DNG) is developed again with Apple's engine when you turn AI Denoise off, as its export is.
- **An export queued before you changed AI Denoise is denoised as it was queued.** An export of the photo open in Develop reused the open photo's developed pixels whenever the file and its decode settings matched, but those pixels did not record their AI Denoise. An export queued at AI Denoise 50 that started after you set the open photo to 0, another amount or another AI Denoise version (or after its model could not be loaded when the photo opened) could therefore come out with the wrong denoising. The open photo's pixels now record the AI Denoise they were developed with (amount and version, which decides the model), and an export reuses them only when that matches its own recipe; otherwise it develops the RAW afresh, as an export of a photo that is not open does. An export therefore no longer depends on which photo is open. A RAW whose sensor AI Denoise cannot work on, opened with AI Denoise in its recipe, no longer leaves a decode that later stands in for one without AI Denoise (with Apple's RAW engine on, those differ). The AI Denoise slider now stores whole steps, also from fine (Option) drags and typed values, the amount Develop develops. Limit: an older edit that holds a fraction (such as 50.4 from a fine drag) keeps it and exports with it, as it always did when its photo was not open, while Develop shows it developed at the nearest whole step. Pasting copies a stored amount as it is.
- **Zoomed-in multi-view cells and the second window show the same pixels as the whole photograph, also with Luminance noise reduction, sharpening, Clarity, Vignette, grain and the other tools whose strength or reach follows the photo's size or position.** A zoomed region was developed as a small photo of its own. Tools sized by the photo's short side (Luminance noise reduction, Clarity, Structure, Dehaze, Glow, Halation and others) then used other radii, and Vignette, Centré and film grain were placed by the region instead of the photograph. In a synthetic test the region's pixels differed from the same pixels of the whole photograph by up to 9 of 255 codes with Luminance noise reduction alone, 26 with Colour 60 and Luminance 30, 36 with Vignette and 32 with grain. A region is now developed as a window of the whole photograph, with the photograph's size and the region's place in it. In the test (15 recipes, 5 regions including the photo's edges and corners), every one of these tools now matches the whole photograph at least 160 pixels inside the region's cut edges: byte for byte in the export's rendering, and within 1 code in the cells' display rendering, whose 8-bit dither pattern can differ by 1. Closer to a cut edge (with noise reduction off a region is developed with less room around the visible part, so a pan can show that strip until the next region arrives), the tools that read neighbouring pixels still see the region's edge repeated, as before. Light flare keeps the photograph's size and place too, but its glow is made from the region's pixels only: lights outside the region add no flare (the rest of the photograph is not loaded). A region with Luminance noise reduction and Clarity takes about a quarter longer to render (about 190 instead of 150 ms for a 1167 × 894 region of a 24 MP photo on an M5 Pro, synthetic), because it uses the whole photograph's larger radii, as the export does. Exports, the Develop view and whole cells are unchanged.
- **Zoomed-in multi-view cells and the second window now match the whole photograph up to the region's edges, and Light flare there sees every light of the photograph.** A zoomed region was cut close to what is shown (with noise reduction off), so Glow, Halation, Clarity, Structure, Dehaze and the other tools that read neighbouring pixels saw the region's edge repeated near the cut, also after the region settled: in a synthetic test with small bright lights, up to 2–26 of 255 codes off the whole photograph there. Light flare's glow was made from the region alone, so lights outside it added no flare (up to 14 codes). Every region is now cut with the reach these tools read, from real pixels of the photograph, and only the visible part is developed; Light flare reads samples of the whole photograph, taken once per photo and geometry and kept. In the test (11 recipes with noise reduction on and off, 4 regions including the photo's edges and a corner) the shown pixels match the whole photograph byte for byte in the export's rendering and within 1 code in the cells' display rendering (its 8-bit dither), up to their edges. A region too large for the graphics card's texture limit is developed in pieces instead of as a photo of its own. Cost on an M5 Pro (Metal, synthetic, a 1167 × 894 region): with Glow and Clarity 17 ms instead of 18 ms at 24 MP and 29 instead of 24 ms at 61 MP; with Colour and Luminance noise reduction, Clarity and sharpening 44 instead of 74 ms (24 MP) and 55 instead of 78 ms (61 MP), since only the visible part is developed now. Exports, the Develop view and whole cells are unchanged.
- **Super Resolution ran 1.4× faster on an RTX 3080 under Linux (CUDA and WebGPU)** with a version of its model that computes in half precision (fp16), added to the model pack (`pack-2026-11-20`) and downloaded with the model when Super Resolution is ticked in the Export dialog. Rawtonic uses it only on a graphics card where its own check (same result as the CPU within the check's tolerance, faster) passed for this file; until that check has run once while the app is idle, and with older model packs, the full-precision model runs as before (the next photo after the check passed switches). The CPU and Macs (Core ML) are unchanged. Measured on one Linux PC with an RTX 3080, a 45 MP Nikon Z7 photo, three runs each: the enlargement took 15.4 s instead of 22.2 s with CUDA and 29.0 s instead of 40.4 s with WebGPU (Windows not measured; the version is used on Windows and Linux graphics cards that pass the check). Measurements: `docs/benchmark-results/srf16-superres-fp16-2026-11-20/REPORT.md`. Exports with Super Resolution on a graphics card change slightly; in 16-bit exports of that photo and a 24 MP Nikon photo, against the full-precision model on the same card: at most 1 code in 8 bits with CUDA and 2 codes with WebGPU (16-bit: at most 157 and 417 of 65535; PSNR 75 and 73 dB on the Z7, 75 and 74 dB on the 24 MP photo), with no visible difference at 200 %. For comparison, the full-precision model's 16-bit exports of the Z7 with CUDA and with WebGPU differ by up to 271 of 65535 (1 code in 8 bits). If the graphics card fails during a photo, that photo is enlarged again on the CPU, so its tiles do not mix two paths.
- **Zoomed-in multi-view cells and the second window filter colour noise as the whole photograph does.** A region shown at 100 % or closer was filtered with a noise measurement of that region alone and cut on its own pixel grid, so with Colour noise reduction on its pixels differed from the same pixels of the whole photograph and of the export (in a synthetic test up to 5–10 of 255 codes). A region now takes the whole photograph's measurement (made once per photograph, geometry and scale, about 140–200 ms on an M5 Pro for 24–61 MP plus building the whole base, then kept) and starts on the filter's pixel grid with enough pixels around it: in the test its pixels are within 1 code of the whole photograph's at Colour 25–100, as without the filter. Exports, the Develop view and whole cells are unchanged. With Luminance noise reduction on, a region still differed from the whole photograph (fixed in the next entry).
- **Clipped highlights of DNGs with a linearization table no longer turn black in places.** The decoder's dithered table lookup wrapped around at the table's top entry when that entry is at or near the maximum, so some samples at the brightest code came out near zero instead of white. Affected are JPEGs converted to DNG (8-bit samples with a linearization table ending at 65535) and, in rare highlight samples, iPhone ProRAW. Previews and thumbnails of such photos are rebuilt. Lossy-JPEG DNGs from Adobe DNG Converter (tiled, one polynomial per colour) were checked as well; their decoding was already correct and is unchanged.
- **An open scope takes less from slider drags.** While you drag, the waveform, parade and vectorscope now sample every second row of the frame and count each sampled value twice. This is an approximation: while dragging, a trace can look somewhat denser or fainter than in the settled view. In the waveforms and the parade, values at 0 and 255 (clipped highlights and blacks) are still counted exactly, from every row, so a clipped detail one row high stays on the scope. On Windows their two threads now run below the editor's priority instead of beside it. Once the frame settles, the scopes are computed from every pixel exactly as before. Headless median scope computation on an M5 Pro (2 threads, 2400 × 1600 frames of three public RAW samples): a drag update 2.3–3.8 ms instead of 3.6–4.4 ms (waveforms, parade) and 2.9–3.4 ms instead of 7.3 ms (vectorscope); a settled one 2.9–4.2 ms instead of 3.6–4.4 ms and 4.2–4.5 ms instead of 7.3 ms. On the Radeon 680M (Windows) a scope update took up to 20 ms at p95 and the preview's p95 rose +1.2…+2.9 ms with a scope open; that has not been measured again with this change yet. Exports are unchanged.
- **A scope update no longer carries its image.** The waveform, RGB waveform, parade and vectorscope images now load into the scope panel from the app by their own address; before, each update carried its image inline (100–280 KB for the RGB waveform), which the web view's main thread had to read beside the drag's preview replies. The images are the same lossless PNGs, pixel for pixel. Measured in headless Chrome 158 (Blink, the engine of Windows' WebView2) on an M5 Pro, replaying the update payloads of three public RAW samples in turn, handling one update on the main thread took for the RGB waveform 0.4 instead of 1.4 ms (median; p95 0.5 instead of 2.7 ms), for the luma waveform and parade 0.4 instead of 0.8–0.9 ms (p95 0.6 instead of 1.4–1.6 ms), for the vectorscope 0.4 instead of 0.5 ms, against 0.3 ms with the scopes closed; the timer resolution there is 0.1 ms. Not included: the delivery of the event from the app to the web view, which also grew with its size, and loading the image through the app's own address, which was simulated with a local web server. The preview p95 with a scope open on the Radeon 680M has not been measured with this change yet.
- **Large AI models stay on the CPU on Windows and Linux systems with integrated graphics.** Subject and Background masks, Relight, and the tagging and content search models skip the WebGPU check on systems where the inference backend sees only integrated GPUs. Large-hole Remove uses its existing fallback there. Smaller models can still qualify for GPU acceleration. In a standalone Subject benchmark on a 14 GB Radeon 680M laptop, WebGPU peaked at 8.4 GB of private process memory (7.4 GB of shared GPU memory), leaving 1.2 GB available. The CPU peaked at 3.5 GB and took about 14 seconds on the first run, compared with 14.7–17.1 seconds on WebGPU. The 680M was already using the CPU in the app with its compile-limited driver.
- **Windows clears empty folders left by completed or interrupted update cleanup.** At startup, Rawtonic removes empty update staging folders for the running version and older versions without logging a warning.
- **Tone curve: repeated arrow-key adjustments undo in one step.** A quarter-second pause after key release or moving to another control starts a new step for curve points and region split handles. Focus identifies the selected point, and keyboard nudges announce its changed coordinates. Disabled points are marked for assistive technology.
- **GPU AI: the check in the background no longer loads a second copy of a model on the CPU.** With GPU AI on, a model first runs on the CPU while Rawtonic checks, when the app is idle, whether the GPU gives the same result faster. That check used to make its own CPU copy of the model beside the one already loaded; while that model is still loaded, it now uses it (a model unloaded meanwhile, for example after switching to the Library, is loaded for the check as before). On a MacBook Pro (M5 Pro, ONNX Runtime 1.30 on the CPU, the Subject model), the check's CPU part peaked at 7.53–7.55 GB instead of 8.63–8.69 GB (−1.1 GB) and took 1.8–2.4 s less (3 runs each). The GPU part is unchanged and was not measured here. The check before first use (`RAWTONIC_AI_PROVIDER`, the CLI) already freed its CPU copy before building the GPU one.
- **AI GPU plugins in a relative folder are used.** A plugin folder given as a relative path (`RAWTONIC_ORT_PLUGIN_DIR` in the app or the CLI, the CLI's `--plugins`) was found but failed to register, because ONNX Runtime reads a relative plugin path from its own library's folder; the model then moved on silently to the next provider (WebGPU instead of CUDA, about 2× slower for Super Resolution on an RTX 3080). The plugin is now registered by its absolute path. The app's default plugin folders are absolute and were not affected. In a setup with such a relative folder, the plugin it holds now registers and, like any installed plugin, is chosen once it passes the existing GPU check (until then, or if the check fails, AI steps stay on the provider they used before): re-exporting an edit with Super Resolution on CUDA instead of WebGPU differs by at most 1 code in 8-bit output (16-bit: at most 138 of 65535 codes; measured on a 24 MP photo, RTX 3080).
- **Copy Settings respects the Library selection.** It copies the focused selected photo. Clearing the selection leaves the clipboard alone.

### Changes to existing edits

- **Saved edits whose face retouch was left out now export with it.** An edit whose faces were found in Retouch and whose photo was then flipped, turned, straightened or given another lens or perspective correction in Crop, without opening Retouch again, rendered and exported without any of its face retouch (skin, Eyes, Teeth); for faces found before 2026-10-20 a later vignetting or chromatic aberration change did the same. Such edits now preview and export with the face retouch the user set, on the same pixels of the photo; this was a defect, so it changes without a version key. Edits without such a later geometry change render exactly as before.
- **Camera RAW TIFF edits from before 0.1.0-alpha.17.1 keep their crop, spots and AI masks on the photograph.** Raw `.tif` files of Phase One backs, Kodak DCS and Canon EOS-1D/1Ds bodies were developed from their small embedded thumbnail until 0.1.0-alpha.17.1, so their edits were made on it. Since then they develop at full resolution, and crops, spot removals and AI masks stayed at their thumbnail pixels: a crop became a corner of a few hundred pixels, and spots and masks sat near the top-left corner. These edits now open and export moved to the full frame. The sidecar is only rewritten when you save a change.
  - Measured with the CLI on five CC0 raw.pixls.us samples (Canon EOS-1D, Kodak DCS560C, Phase One P30, H 25 and IQ140) against the same edit made on the full frame:
    - Crop, crop with a 90° turn, and Straighten with crop export at the same size, with a mean luminance difference of 0.00 codes at the best shift of 0 px.
    - Spot removals land within 0.1 px (overlap 0.991–1.0).
    - Sky, Subject and People masks are computed again on the full-resolution image when the photo opens in Develop, keeping their Grow, Feather and Invert; a notice says on how many layers, and one undo brings back the earlier masks, stretched from the thumbnail, which hold no finer detail than it (a synthetic Subject selection stretched this way landed 1.5–21 px off, with overlap 0.94–0.996). Exports of such edits compute them the same way. Only models already installed are used, so opening such an edit downloads nothing; without the model, and for depth ranges, Object selections and People selections of one chosen person (whom the full image may number differently), the stretched mask stays. Measured with the CLI on the five samples: a migrated Subject and Sky mask exports byte for byte as the same selection detected fresh on the full frame (overlap 1.0, 0 px; the Develop path is covered by tests, not measured).
  - Brush, Linear and Radial masks, which record the size they were drawn on, and edits of all other files render exactly as before.
  - Rawtonic now saves the photo size each edit was made on with it.
  - Snapshots, Object selection prompts and Library thumbnails move with the edit.
  - No version number marks an edit as made before 0.1.0-alpha.17.1, so the edit itself decides:
    - It is left as it is when it has a drawn mask or AI mask of the full size, or a crop or spot reaching beyond the thumbnail's size.
    - It is moved when it has a drawn mask or AI mask of the thumbnail's size, or a crop of exactly the whole thumbnail, also when processing versions of a RAW were pasted onto it.
    - Otherwise RAW-only processing settings, which every edit begun on a RAW has and an ordinary TIFF's never had, mean the full frame, but processing versions pasted from a RAW add them too: when such an edit has a crop or spots, all within the thumbnail-sized top-left corner, it cannot tell, so it opens as it was saved and Develop offers to move it to the full image (one undo step; saving keeps your choice).
    - An edit without RAW-only settings and with everything within the thumbnail is moved. An older edit that got a new full-size spot within the thumbnail-sized corner during the two days 0.1.0-alpha.17.1 and alpha.18 were current looks exactly like one and is moved too.
  - Pasting, syncing or applying a preset to such an edit in the Library now moves it to the full frame first, so pasted processing versions no longer keep it on the thumbnail.
- **JPEG-to-DNG and iPhone ProRAW photos with clipped highlights** render those highlights white where some pixels were near black before, also in saved edits: a wrapped lookup is a decoding defect, and decoder fixes apply to all photos. Measured with the CLI, before and after: in a synthetic JPEG-to-DNG exported as 8-bit JPEG (default settings), 3,987 of 12,482 pure-white source pixels had a channel below 200 of 255 before; after, none do (lowest channel 242 of 255, at the white area's JPEG edges). On the public iPhone 12 Pro ProRAW sample from raw.pixls.us, 2 samples decoded as 0 instead of 65535; in its 16-bit TIFF export, 818 pixels change by at most 765 of 65535. Every other decoded value is unchanged. 17 other files whose decoders use these tables export byte for byte as before: Adobe DNG Converter lossy DNGs, Leica M8/M9, Sigma fp, Autel, Nikon curves including small NEF, Sony curves, Leaf. The method and results are in `third_party/rawler/RAWTONIC_PATCHES.md`.

- **Super Resolution on a graphics card under Windows and Linux** uses the half-precision model once its check has passed on that card, so such exports change slightly: on two Nikon photos (24 and 45 MP, RTX 3080) at most 1 code in 8 bits with CUDA and 2 with WebGPU against the full-precision model, no visible difference at 200 %. The CPU and Macs are unchanged.
- **DNGs whose lens correction has separate values per colour** (such as the DJI Osmo Action) render their lateral colour correction on the camera's colours when never edited (`renderVersion` 14). Edited photos export byte for byte as before.
- **A photo whose LUT file is missing** previews and exports without the LUT instead of black, and with it again once the file is back.

### Known gaps

These are measured shortfalls or open work; they stay open until they are closed by measurement.

- **In-app update, Windows:** a photo opened on its own from the command line, without a library, is not reopened after **Restart now** (its edit is saved; tested on a Windows 11 laptop); a photo opened from Explorer's Open with was not tested (the test build had no file association). A library or session reopens.
- **Upright Full** keeps less of the frame than Camera Raw: median share 0.754 against Camera Raw's estimated 0.859 on 25 photos; even the best solution chosen per photo against the reference reaches only 0.838 without stretching the photo, so nothing was changed.
- **High-ISO colour edges** stay slightly below the better of Camera Raw and Capture One: 0.956 of it at ISO 6400 and 0.958 at ISO 12800 and above (27 frames); no candidate reached it without more colour specks.
- **Library thumbnails:** scrolling through 1,000 new RAWs takes 37 s; Lightroom Classic builds its Minimal previews of the same folder in 24–29 s.
- **AI Denoise** at equal flat-area noise keeps a 4–6 % higher lightness error than Camera Raw's Denoise, and on an M4 Pro its network alone takes an estimated 28 s per 24 MP photo on first use (1.15 s per tile on the GPU), against Camera Raw's ≈ 10 s for the whole step.
- **Landscape mask:** no Snow part yet (Lightroom masks with Snow are left out on import); snow on a mountain top in cloud can count as sky.
- **Eyes and Teeth:** the glow around Iris and Sclera is larger than Capture One's; deeply shadowed teeth and narrowed or hair-covered eyes are still missed in places.
- **Colour targets:** a colour far beyond sRGB may stay short of its target (a blue toy robot: ΔE00 1.13 left, Capture One 1.37); the readout says so.
- **HDR edits from Lightroom** keep their settings but are shown and exported as SDR.
- **Radeon 680M:** in a render probe on synthetic 1024 × 682 frames (Vulkan), Glow, Halation and film grain together still add +1.27 ms per drag frame, over the +1 ms goal; their cost in the app's own drags was not measured.
- The known gaps of alpha.18 and earlier that this release does not close remain (closed in this release, for example: photos with AI selections pasted, synced or from a preset now export without being opened in Develop first).

## 0.1.0-alpha.18 — 2026-11-19

The eighteenth alpha. Rawtonic can now install its own updates after you click **Install update** in the update notice — this is the first release that has the button, so reaching it takes one last manual update; from the next release on, updates install from the notice. The AI runtime is now Rawtonic's own build without Microsoft's usage reporting on Apple silicon Macs, Linux and Windows x64, and RAW decoding comes closer to the camera or to Adobe's DNG for ten Canon CR3 bodies, older Fujifilm kit zooms and bridge cameras, Phase One backs, early DNGs and Canon sRAW. Saved edits of the affected Canon, Phase One and DNG photos keep their settings but render differently. In our tests: Canon CR3 shadows up to 0.04 EV darker on 43 sample files, and Canon sRAW/mRAW up to 0.6 EV on test copies at other ISOs (real captures not yet checked); Phase One and early DNG files lose their colour and tile defects. Separately, Canon CR3 edits that use the older AgX tone mapper with Use Apple RAW 9 render 0.14–0.50 EV darker, depending on the camera, from an exposure correction applied before tone mapping (see Changes to existing edits).

### Highlights

- **Install update from the update notice.** When a newer release is out, the notice offers **Install update**: Rawtonic downloads that release's package for your Mac (app), Linux AppImage or Windows per-user setup, installs it only if it carries the signature of Rawtonic's own update key for exactly that version, and replaces the app when you choose **Restart now** — after it saved your edits, and after asking if exports or imports are still running. Nothing is downloaded before the click; the daily startup check sends nothing new. A package that fails the check is discarded and the app stays as it was. On Windows the setup runs without an administrator prompt, and Rawtonic removes the downloaded setups at its next start. Copies installed for all users (Windows MSI), the portable zip and releases without an update package keep the browser link; an all-users copy now gets the all-users installer there, and when a release's update information is unavailable the notice says so and links to the release page. Tested end to end with test builds and a local update server on macOS, the Linux AppImage and the Windows per-user setup (as a standard Windows user): edits made before the restart were there afterwards, the photos were unchanged, and a package with one changed byte, signed with another key or signed for another version was refused. **This release is the first that can be updated from**: getting to it is one last manual update, and the button is first used for the release after it.
- **Privacy: the AI runtime is now Rawtonic's own build without Microsoft's usage reporting** on Apple silicon Macs, Linux (x86_64 and arm64) and Windows x64. It is ONNX Runtime 1.30.0, built by Rawtonic from the same source with the reporting left out (no uploader; on Windows no event provider in Windows' telemetry group); on the CPU its results were identical to Microsoft's library bit for bit in the cases tested: one synthetic input per tested model on each platform (a different subset of models per platform; Linux on arm64 only under emulation), on a Mac two Super Resolution exports made with the command-line tool, and on a Windows laptop (Radeon 680M) a Super Resolution export from the app; other models and inputs were not compared. On that Windows laptop AI masks, AI Denoise and Super Resolution ran on it, and the app's only outside connections in the test were made while it downloaded the AI models from GitHub. The switches against reporting from 0.1.0-alpha.17.1 stay set as a second layer. Intel Macs and Windows on Arm keep Microsoft's library with those switches, and the GPU plugins (WebGPU, optional CUDA) stay Microsoft's. See [Privacy](PRIVACY.md).
- **Windows: the built-in web view starts with WebView2's background networking and component updates disabled** (`--disable-background-networking`, `--disable-component-update`). On a Windows laptop running 0.1.0-alpha.17.1, each of four test starts contacted `config.edge.skype.com` and Edge's component updater; with these two settings applied to that build for a test, the web view made no HTTPS request. This release's build itself has not been measured yet. Windows itself may still look up a proxy configuration (WPAD) on the local network.
- **Fujifilm kit-zoom wide end framed and corrected like the camera** (recipe `cameraGeometryVersion` 11, new edits and never-edited RAWs): on the X-T100, X-A5 and X-A10 the XC kit zoom's strong barrel correction at 15–16 mm came out 4 % wider than the camera's JPEG (24.5–25.8 px median), because those cameras keep the corrected picture inside the frame they recorded and read their correction data on the sensor's radius. Rawtonic now reads the data of the older Fujifilm bodies that way for barrel distortion: against the cameras' own JPEGs the X-T100 matches to 0.23 px, the X-A5 to 0.18 and the X-A10 to 0.22 (Camera Raw: 0.27, 0.24 and 25.6), the FinePix S1 and SL1000 bridge cameras 57.6 → 0.94 and 44.7 → 1.17, the X-A1 1.09 → 0.77 and the X-A2 0.75 → 0.65. Measured on 139 Fujifilm RAWs (raw.pixls.us, never edited, without 7 files whose embedded JPEG is no usable reference and the 4 shot in another aspect ratio, see the next entry), no photo's lens correction got worse by more than 0.04 px. The lens correction of current bodies, of pincushion data and of the fixed-lens compacts is unchanged, and edited photos keep their look.
- **Fujifilm photos that record their aspect ratio open in it.** A RAF whose header records the aspect ratio set in the camera (RawImageAspectRatio: an X-T200 shot in 16:9, an X-T50 in 5:4) keeps the whole 3:2 frame in its RAW (and in its embedded JPEG); a never-edited photo and a new edit now show it cropped to the camera's ratio, as with Canon's aspect ratios — exports, thumbnails and the camera-JPEG thumbnail included — an ordinary crop you can widen or reset. Edited photos keep their crop. RAFs without that tag (the X-M1) open uncropped as before.
- **Canon CR3 photos of ten bodies decode with the white level Adobe uses for them**, which fixes slightly too bright shadows (on four sample files checked against Rawtonic's render of Adobe's DNG of the same file: up to 0.05 EV brighter before, now within 0.003 EV), and for five bodies a decoder defect that read the wrong field of the camera's colour data. **Phase One backs, DNG 1.0 files and Canon sRAW/mRAW** decode correctly too: the flat-field correction of older Phase One backs no longer makes checker patterns, early lossless DNGs no longer have corrupted tiles, and sRAW/mRAW take their white level by ISO. Details and how saved edits change under Changes to existing edits.
- **Nikon High Efficiency NEFs** give a clear message that explains why they cannot be opened and how to convert them.
- **Windows is no longer labelled experimental**; it remains unsigned.
- **The tone curve's Luminance channel costs less than half as much per preview frame.** The preview reads the Luminance curve from a sampled table and no longer evaluates RGB, Red, Green and Blue curves that change nothing. Exports still evaluate the curve exactly, and their pixels are unchanged. On an M4 Pro, a 6 MP preview frame with a Luminance S-curve takes 0.38–0.42 ms longer than one without the curve, or 0.43–0.46 ms while you drag a curve point, compared with 0.93–0.97 ms before (four alternating runs). The preview stays within one 8-bit level of the export, as before. While the clipping warnings show, and for steep curves that a table would move by more than that on dark saturated colours, the preview evaluates the curve itself.

### Fixes

- Windows: paths in the library and export dialogs no longer mix `/` and `\`.
- **Windows setup can install Rawtonic for your account alongside an older all-users installation without administrator approval.** Setup explains how to remove the old copy later through Windows Settings with administrator approval. Both copies share your Rawtonic settings and app data.
- **Exports return freed CPU memory to the system sooner around the photo's GPU upload.** This applies wherever Rawtonic uses mimalloc, including macOS. Integrated GPUs also start with 8 MB graphics-memory blocks that can grow to 256 MB. Metal ignores that GPU hint.
  - In earlier app tests on a Windows laptop with a Radeon 680M, exporting seven 12–61 MP RAWs as both 16-bit TIFF and JPEG at quality 90 peaked at 5,964 → about 5,425 MB for the app and WebView2 with one export thread, and 4,494 → about 4,234 MB for the headless app with two jobs.
  - In the updated CLI tests on the same laptop, exporting the seven RAWs as 16-bit TIFF alone with two jobs took 39.2 s and peaked at 3,750 MB (medians of three runs), compared with 40.9 s and 4,900 MB in the earlier baseline. With one job, it took 45.6 s and peaked at 3,074 MB, compared with 48.4 s and 4,353 MB. Exporting just JPEG at quality 90 with two jobs took 47.4 s and peaked at 3,524 MB on the updated build. All seven outputs in each of those nine runs matched the earlier exports byte for byte.
  - On that laptop, Exposure drags at 100 % zoom produced a median 630 preview frames per run with the smaller starting blocks, compared with 632 using the previous GPU hint. In a separate earlier CLI comparison, all 13 16-bit TIFFs and all 13 JPEGs at quality 90 matched byte for byte across the benchmark builds.

### Changes to existing edits

- **Canon EOS R5, R5 Mark II, R6, R6 Mark II, R7, R8, R10, R50, R50 V and EOS-1D X Mark III CR3 photos** decode with the white level Adobe DNG Converter writes for them (16384; 1D X Mark III 16050; R6 Mark II its file's specular white 14008), instead of an exposure correction on top of a lower white level. For the R6 Mark II, R7, R8, R10 and R50 this also fixes a decoder defect that read the wrong field of the camera's colour data (the linearity margin, 12735, instead of the white level). Shadows render slightly darker: on the 43 raw.pixls.us samples of these bodies, average midtone lightness drops by 0.002–0.13 L\*, and the darkest tones by up to 0.04 EV. On four of these files, checked against Rawtonic's render of Adobe's DNG of the same file, average brightness per lightness band now matches within 0.003 EV; before, the shadows were up to 0.05 EV brighter. Highlights look the same: on a clipped sky, at default settings and at Exposure −1.5 with Highlights −100, cloud detail did not change, because values above the old white level were already kept. On the 43 files, fully white output pixels changed by at most 26 of 24 million, and magenta-tinted bright pixels by a few dozen to about 130 per file, in both directions. With **Use Apple RAW 9**, CR3 photos of these bodies keep their exposure: Rawtonic scales Apple's decode by the correction it used before. Small shadow differences of the size above are possible there; they were not measured. Exception: edits that use the older AgX tone mapper with Use Apple RAW 9 render darker by that correction (R5/R6 0.23 EV, R5 Mark II/R6 Mark II 0.14 EV, R7/R8/R10/R50 0.38–0.42 EV, R50 V 0.50 EV, 1D X Mark III 0.20 EV), because AgX never applied it before. Other cameras' files are unchanged. Saved edits of these photos change too: a wrong white level is a decoding defect, and decoder fixes apply to all photos.
- **Phase One H 25, P20+, P25, P25+, P30, P45 and P45+ photos** get their flat-field colour correction on the red and blue pixels; it went to green pixels before (wrong-coloured checker patterns on flat areas). The variation of the two green channels against each other on flat areas of the sample files dropped close to that of Adobe DNG Converter's DNGs of the same files (H 25 1.60 % → 0.41 %, Adobe 0.41 %; P20+ 4.11 → 0.79 %, Adobe 0.76 %; P25 4.97 → 0.63 %, Adobe 0.63 %; P25+ 4.16 → 2.07 %, Adobe 2.11 %; P45+ 1.41 → 0.49 %, Adobe 0.49 %). Saved edits of these photos change too. Of the other Phase One and Leaf files checked (every IIQ, Phase One TIF and MOS of raw.pixls.us), none changed.
- **DNGs of DNG version 1.0 with lossless compression** (e.g. XIAOYI action cameras) no longer have corrupted, too bright or tinted image tiles. The two affected files among the 262 raw.pixls.us DNGs now decode to the same raw samples as Adobe DNG Converter's DNGs of them (every sample equal). Saved edits of such photos change too.
- **Canon sRAW and mRAW photos** (EOS 7D, 7D Mark II, 40D, 50D, 60D, 70D, 5D Mark II/III/IV, 5DS, 5DS R, 6D, 6D Mark II, 1D Mark IV) take their white level from a table by ISO instead of one fixed value. The table holds the white level Adobe DNG Converter 18.7 writes for copies of one sample file per body with only the ISO tag changed; before, every ISO used the level of the sample's own ISO. On these copies (31 files of 16 bodies, six ISOs each, compared with Rawtonic's render of Adobe's DNG of each copy) the exposure difference varied with the ISO by a median 0.24 EV (up to 0.95 EV on the 5D Mark IV) and now by under 0.002 EV; the constant difference of each file (−0.17 to +0.33 EV against that reference, not against Camera Raw) is unchanged. Not yet checked on real captures at other ISOs. The 5D Mark IV no longer uses the file's specular white level, which Adobe ignores for it (checked on an edited copy), so 5D Mark IV sRAW/mRAW files whose specular level differs from the table can change even at ISO 100. The raw.pixls.us sample files decode unchanged. Saved sRAW/mRAW edits at the affected ISOs re-export brighter or darker, matching Adobe (on the probe copies: EOS 60D ISO 160 +0.17 EV, 50D ISO 200 −0.42 EV, up to +0.60 EV on the 5D Mark IV at ISO 1250): a wrong white level is a decoding defect, and decoder fixes apply to all photos.
- **Fujifilm: never-edited photos follow the new lens correction and aspect crop; edited ones keep their look.** Of 151 raw.pixls.us RAFs, the 14 covered by the new barrel-distortion reading or by the camera's aspect ratio render differently when never edited; the other 137 export byte for byte as before. Saved edits exported byte for byte as before (Rawtonic's 280 saved test edits on a Mac, 326 under Linux with an NVIDIA card; the Linux set holds no affected Fujifilm body).
- Super Resolution exports using the ONNX Runtime CPU path may round differently from earlier versions. In a 24 MP test, the largest difference was 0.00000232 on a 0–1 scale before rounding, which can change an 8-bit channel by at most 1/255.

### Known gaps

These are measured shortfalls or open work; they stay open until they are closed by measurement.

- **Windows** is unsigned, so SmartScreen and browsers may hold the download or first start back (README); GUI testing has covered one NVIDIA graphics card and one AMD Radeon 680M laptop.
- **In-app update:** first used for real by the release after this one; so far tested only with test builds and a local update server. On Windows the question before restarting while an export runs was tested only on macOS and Linux (the Windows test machine had no graphics card for exports). A photo opened on its own from Explorer or the command line, without a library, is not reopened after **Restart now**. Windows on Arm and Linux on arm64 copies are not offered Install update (no release builds a package for them yet) and keep the browser link.
- **Privacy, Windows:** the absence of the runtime's usage reports in Windows' event tracing was checked in the file, not measured with a live trace (that needs administrator rights).
- **Super Resolution on the tested Windows laptop with a Radeon 680M** (integrated graphics, Vulkan) ran on the processor: about 570 s for a 24 → 96 MP export.
- **AI Denoise is below Camera Raw at high ISO:** on 10 frames of 5 cameras at ISO 6400–51200, its luminance error is 1.71× that of Camera Raw's Denoise at the default 50, and still 5–10 % higher when both leave the same flat-area noise; colour noise is on par. Capture One 16.8's Enhanced Denoise has not been compared yet.
- **Canon:** the EOS R1, R5 C and R100 keep their file's white level until they are checked against Adobe's. With **Use Apple RAW 9**, some other Canon bodies render darker than with the built-in decoder at the white level Adobe uses (EOS R6 Mark III about 0.7 EV, M50 and 90D about 0.4 EV); this is not new in this release.
- **Fujifilm:** the X-A7's kit zoom at 45 mm stays further from the camera's JPEG than Camera Raw (0.65 px vs 0.19); the XF1's lens correction stays about 11 px further from its camera's JPEG than Camera Raw's; the X-M1 records no aspect ratio, so its 16:9 photos still open in 3:2; old FinePix bodies keep their decoded frame size.
- The known gaps of alpha.17.1 and earlier not named here remain.

## 0.1.0-alpha.17.1 — 2026-11-18

A privacy release. Earlier releases let the AI runtime (Microsoft's ONNX Runtime) report usage data to Microsoft; this release sets the runtime's own switches against it before the runtime starts (measured on macOS and Linux with an AI model running on the processor: no connection; on Linux also with the WebGPU and CUDA GPU plugins loaded; on Windows the switch is set but not yet measured) — the affected releases, what was sent and where its files are, below. Also: Panasonic DC-FZ83 files open, screen readers name more controls, Develop's scopes are in the View menu, and two small speed-ups. Updating from alpha.17: replace the app as usual; your libraries, sessions and edits stay as they are.

### Highlights

- **Privacy: the AI runtime reported usage data to Microsoft in earlier releases; this release switches it off.** Rawtonic's AI tools run on Microsoft's ONNX Runtime, which earlier releases bundled with Microsoft's usage reporting built in. Rawtonic's earlier attempt to switch it off (on the main branch only, never released) did not stop it.
  - **Linux (0.1.0-alpha.2 to 0.1.0-alpha.17, every AppImage) and Apple silicon Macs (0.1.0-alpha.12 to 0.1.0-alpha.17):** whenever the runtime started (using an AI tool, or AI work in the background such as content search indexing), it queued reports for Microsoft's event collector (`mobile.events.data.microsoft.com`) and uploaded them in the background while the app ran and a connection was available. Reports not uploaded before you quit stayed in a queue file and could be uploaded at a later start of the runtime. They held the runtime version, your operating system version, CPU model, number of cores, memory size, a hashed device ID and a session ID, and, for each AI model loaded, the model's file name, hashes and metadata, the processor or graphics card it ran on (vendor and device IDs), run counts and times, resource use and error messages (file paths removed). No photos, image content or photo file paths. Measured with a model running on the processor: on an Apple silicon Mac four HTTPS requests to Microsoft 9 s after the runtime started, all accepted; on Linux four connection attempts 8–9 s after start. The tests of the affected releases used the CPU. The runtime kept its device ID and queue in `~/Library/Application Support/Microsoft/DeveloperTools/.onnxruntime` (macOS) or `~/.cache/Microsoft/DeveloperTools/.onnxruntime` (Linux; under `$XDG_CACHE_HOME` when that is set). Other programs that use ONNX Runtime share that folder, so Rawtonic leaves it; you may delete it. Intel Macs (ONNX Runtime 1.23.2) had no reporting.
  - **Windows (0.1.0-alpha.10 to 0.1.0-alpha.17):** the runtime had no uploader of its own. It wrote its reports to Windows' event tracing as a Microsoft telemetry provider, so Windows' diagnostic data service could collect them and send them to Microsoft under your Windows diagnostic data setting (not measured). They held the runtime version, CPU model, number of cores, memory size, whether a debugger was attached and the process's service names, and, per AI model, the same model and hardware details as above plus graphics driver names and versions, provider options and the full path of the GPU plugin library, which can contain your Windows user name.
  - **This release** sets ONNX Runtime's own switches before the runtime is opened: `ORT_DISABLE_TELEMETRY` (macOS, Linux: the uploader is never created, so the queue is not sent either) and `ORT_RUNNING_UNIT_TESTS` (Windows: the event provider is never registered). Measured on macOS and Linux with a model running on the processor: no address lookup, no connection and, on Linux, no new files. On Linux, running a model on each of the WebGPU and CUDA GPU plugins showed no connection and no new ONNX Runtime reporting files. The Windows switch is taken from ONNX Runtime's source and not yet measured on Windows. A build of the runtime without any of this reporting (CPU outputs bit-identical on the 15 model files compared so far, macOS) is planned as a follow-up.

### Fixes

- **Raw TIFFs from Phase One backs, Canon EOS-1D and EOS-1Ds, and supported Kodak DCS cameras now open at full sensor resolution.** They previously opened as small embedded previews. Recheck earlier edits of these files, especially crops and masks, which were saved against the preview. The Kodak DCS520C reports an unsupported camera error.

- **Panasonic Lumix DC-FZ83 RAW files open.** They failed with "Unknown camera". Panasonic ships one firmware for the DC-FZ80, FZ81, FZ82 and FZ83, and the FZ83's RAW files have the FZ80's sensor layout and format, so they now develop with the FZ80's camera data, like the FZ82 and FZ85 already did. Checked on the four public FZ83 samples of raw.pixls.us (all aspect ratios); other cameras' files decode as before.



- **Screen readers name more controls in Preferences and the workspace.** Every drop-down and slider in Preferences is now named by its setting (before, they had no name or only their value), the icon buttons for lenses, AI tags and tagging shortcuts say what they do, the removable tag and shortcut chips are buttons the keyboard can reach (removing one moves the focus to the next chip, or to the field that adds them), each shortcut button names its action and key, an action without a key reads “Not assigned” instead of an empty key cap, the two side panels are told apart as “Develop tools” and “Library tools”, and the Export dialog's title bar no longer counts as a second page banner. The frontend tests now audit Library, Develop (each tool tab), Import, Export and Preferences with axe-core and fail on new problems of these kinds.

- Open Develop’s scopes from the View menu or search for them in the command palette. The Undo and Redo toolbar tooltips show your configured keys.

- **Entering the multi view shows every photo in its first frame.** On macOS and Windows (the native renderer), this applies when the live photo is shown at fit and the photos' thumbnails are still cached and were shown in the filmstrip recently (the app keeps up to 10,000 of them in mind). The thumbnails are then decoded while the cells are laid out, and the live photo is placed into its cell at once, so all of them appear in the layout's first frame. Before, they came one frame later. Any other cell gets its thumbnail as before, a frame after the layout. Mac mini M4 Pro, 60 fps screen recording, ⌘A to all 12 shown: 102–116 → 84–86 ms (median, n = 10 per block). Lightroom Classic's Survey took 80 ms on the same Mac.

- **A tool's Before at 100 % shows faster.** On macOS and Windows (the native renderer), holding a tool's reset with ⌥ when zoomed in now draws the visible part of the photo first and then the rest; Linux is unchanged. Holding Before (the whole photograph's peek) uses the same path but was not timed. On a 24 MP photo: 188 → 92 ms on press and 175 → 96 ms on release (Mac mini M4 Pro, 60 fps recording, n = 10). Lightroom Classic's panel switch took 104/118 ms and Capture One 252/243 ms. The visible part can differ from the finished frame by a few levels in about 2 % of its pixels until the rest arrives, about 0.1 s later.

### Known gaps

These are measured shortfalls or open work; they stay open until they are closed by measurement.

- **Export memory on a 14 GB Windows laptop (Radeon 680M)** (measured with alpha.17; this release does not change it): seven 50–61 MP RAWs as 16-bit TIFF and JPEG, two at a time, peaked at 7.5 GB, above the 6.6 GB target (6.1 GB one at a time).
- **The first AI Subject mask on a fresh Windows computer** (Radeon 680M laptop, AI on the processor) still takes about 24 s: about 9 s to prepare the model, 1.6 s to keep its optimized form and 15 s to compute the mask. Later masks were ready within a second in our test when the app had computed them ahead.
- **Scopes:** on Linux, with the RGB waveform open, an Exposure drag at 100 % zoom took 2 ms longer at the 95th percentile of the renderer's reply time (21 vs 19 ms, 95 % interval 0–3 ms) than with the scopes closed; on a Mac the measured difference was within the noise.
- **.cube LUTs with their own input range:** the check that saved edits export byte for byte as before is done on Apple silicon, not yet on NVIDIA under Linux.
- **Imports:** Lightroom's pre-2012 curves are an approximation and not yet compared with Lightroom's renders. Guided Upright from Lightroom catalogs is not yet imported for Sony RAWs set to another aspect ratio in the camera. Capture One's brush strokes and colour ranges come over as fixed masks that cannot be edited as strokes or ranges, and a Capture One linear gradient on a lens-corrected photo stays within 2.3 % (0.3 % on average) of a mask that follows the correction exactly, because a straight Rawtonic gradient cannot follow the correction's slight bow.
- **Multi view:** entering 12 photos now shows them in about 0.085 s; Lightroom shows them about 0.08 s on the same Mac.
- **Sidecars:** an XMP sidecar with more than 250,000 XML elements (far beyond normal brush work) is refused and treated as changed elsewhere.
- **Accessibility:** contrast is not yet checked automatically; a few summary headers and the photo tiles are excluded from the automatic check until they are restructured.
- **Windows** stays experimental: unsigned, tested on one NVIDIA card and one AMD Radeon 680M laptop.
- **Privacy (Windows):** the runtime's switch against usage reporting is set but not yet measured on Windows (see Highlights).
- The known gaps of alpha.17 and earlier not named here remain.

## 0.1.0-alpha.17 — 2026-11-18

The seventeenth alpha. Color Grading tints coloured tones closer to Lightroom, .cube LUTs with their own input range import, and Capture One imports bring local adjustment layers and the lens correction along, while Lightroom imports keep all eight Point Color ranges in a mask, Guided Upright and old-process curves. Exports of large RAWs need far less memory on a Mac, the multi view on Windows no longer hides its other photos while the next one opens, and on a Windows laptop whose AI runs on the processor, later AI Subject masks the app had prepared ahead were ready within a second of the click. On Linux the settled fit view is sent lossless and the 100 % view stays sharp with a mask tool open. Damage the RAW decoders can detect now gives an error instead of a crash. Many smaller fixes from three UX reviews (macOS, Windows, Linux) make culling, menus, keys and dialogs behave as a photographer expects.

### Highlights

- **Color Grading tints coloured tones closer to Lightroom** (new edits; saved ones keep their look). A wheel's tint now also depends on the colour it lands on, not only on its lightness: half of the tint is applied to each colour channel by that channel's own level, which brings coloured areas closer to how Lightroom renders the same wheel settings. Measured on 24 photographs the fit never saw, against Camera Raw's (Lightroom's engine) renders of 71 wheel, Luminance, Blending and Balance settings: 47 now match closely and 24 are near (before 39 and 30); the two settings that were clearly off there are now near — a strong green Highlights tint (hue 120°, saturation 100) and a strong blue Midtones tint (hue 210°, saturation 100). On the photographs it was fitted on, the strong magenta Midtones tint (hue 300°, saturation 100) is no longer clearly off either.
- **The Develop fit view shows the colour noise the export has, also at Color 0 (corrects a claim of alpha.15).** A moment after a change the fit view and the open photo's grid thumbnail now render from the export's own render input: the photo's lens correction, automatic chromatic aberration correction, edge-fringe treatment and colour noise reduction computed at full resolution, as the export computes them (about 0.25–0.36 s per 24 MP photo on a Mac mini, 0.4–0.9 s on an RTX 3080 under Linux; while a slider is dragged the view shows its quick version as before). Before, the view put its reduced copy through the edge-fringe treatment at the reduced size, where it leaves colour noise the export's full-resolution pass smooths on noisy photos: at Color 0 the view showed up to 1.9 times the export's colour noise (Nikon D5500 at ISO 6400 and 100). Measured on 4 RAWs against real exports scaled to the view, per frequency band: Color 25, 50 and 100 within 15 % in every band on Mac and Linux (before 30–40 % of the bands were outside), Color 0 within 16 %. Linux with a discrete graphics card now has it too. **Correction:** alpha.15 said the fit view's colour noise lay "within 15 % of the export scaled down in every cell" on Apple silicon and dedicated graphics cards; that was measured against a reference rendered without the export's lens warp, automatic chromatic aberration correction and edge-fringe treatment — against the real export 30–40 % of the bands were outside. Dragging a slider while the view prepares this no longer holds up the drag on a busy computer (before, a drag could get 0–2 frames). Integrated graphics other than Apple silicon keep the quick version. Exports are unchanged.
- **Linux: the preview keeps the photo's fine detail, at fit and at 100 %.** On Linux the Develop view reaches the page as an image. Its settled frame at fit, and a Before/After or multi-view frame beside it, used to be a JPEG at quality 94, which removed most of the faint fine colour noise of a high-ISO photo after colour noise reduction, so the view looked cleaner than the export. Settled reduced frames are now sent lossless, and a split's before is always encoded like the after it is compared with: in tests with four high-ISO photos at Color 0–100, the fit view's fine colour noise relative to Color 0 now stays within 15 % of the export's in every measured band (before, 21 of 60 bands were outside); in absolute terms 78 of 80 bands are within 15 %, the two exceptions at Color 0 (1.16 and 0.85), the same as on macOS and Windows (with the JPEG, 39 of 80 were outside, at as little as half the export's). In a 1536 × 1024 view the settled frame is decoded and ready about 25 ms later after a change; the time from a drag input to its preview reply is unchanged; drag frames and the 100 % view stay JPEG. Separately, while a mask tool or the white balance picker was active, their drawing layer made the webview show the 100 % view as the fit-size picture enlarged; the photograph is now drawn beside that layer: detail at 100 % (Laplacian variance of screen crops relative to a lossless export of the same edit, 24 MP NEF, one NVIDIA/KDE Wayland PC) went from 0.01× to 1.12–1.13×, Exposure drag timings within run-to-run noise. Zooming Before/After to 100 % keeps both sides in the same format (Before waits for the After frame), and cached previews keep their format when switching photos.
- **.cube LUTs with their own input range import and render as the format says.** A 3D .cube whose table spans another range than 0–1 (`DOMAIN_MIN`/`DOMAIN_MAX`, or `LUT_3D_INPUT_RANGE` before the table size) was refused on import. It now imports and reads each colour over the file's range, values outside it taking the table's edge, as the .cube specification describes. What reaches the table depends on the LUT's input: a display LUT reads the photo's display colours, which stay within 0–1, and a scene-referred LUT its V-Log-encoded colours (0.125 at black, above 1 for the brightest highlights), so a range wider than either uses only the part those colours reach. Tested on synthetic tables over 0–1.5, −0.1–1.1, per-channel ranges and an input range against an independent implementation of the specification, with a bound of 1/1023 on a 0–1 scale: the engine's largest difference was 0.0000004 (a 33³ grid of colours reaching beyond each range; curved tables compared at their grid points), the GPU's display LUT against the engine 0.00025 (three of these ranges, colours inside the display range). Edits made before keep rendering as they were.
- **Capture One imports bring the local adjustment layers and the lens correction.** Importing a Capture One session or catalog (now also `.cocatalog`) brought a photo's global edits but none of its layers. Layers with a linear or radial gradient, a filled mask or a Luma Range on a filled mask now become Rawtonic layers with the same geometry, opacity, on/off state and the settings a layer can hold (settings a layer cannot hold are named in the import report); on a test photo the gradients' transitions lie where Capture One's are. Layers painted with Capture One's brush, made from a colour range (Color Editor) or by its AI selection, and Luma Ranges on a painted mask, come over pixel for pixel as an imported mask on the layer, which moves with the photo when it is turned, flipped, straightened, cropped or its geometry corrected: on a 36 MP test photo with lens correction off in both editors they cover the same area as in Capture One's own export (overlap 99 % for a soft brush stroke and a colour range, 100 % for a hard brush; edges within 0.5 px). A photo with Capture One's lens distortion correction on (its default where it knows the lens) arrived uncorrected when Rawtonic has no default correction for that camera, for example a Nikon D800 with the 24–70 mm f/2.8G, up to 4 % of its radius away from Capture One's (about 180 px in the corners at 36 MP); the import now turns on Rawtonic's own profile for that lens with Capture One's Distortion and Light Falloff amounts (none where Capture One had both at 0), and the photo then sits within 1 % of the radius of Capture One's (Rawtonic frames the corrected photo about 1 % wider). Capture One draws gradients before the lens correction, so the import moves their points through the correction: on the 36 MP test photo with lens correction on, painted, colour range and rasterized gradient layers cover the same part of the photo as in Capture One (overlap 98 %, edges within 1.1 px).
- **Lightroom imports keep more: eight Point Color ranges per mask, Guided Upright, old-process curves and Exposure.** A mask held at most four Color Editor ranges, so a Lightroom catalog or preset with more Point Color swatches in a mask (Lightroom allows eight) lost the fifth to eighth without a word; masks now hold eight, as the photo does, and imports bring all of them over (a record with even more swatches, only possible outside Lightroom, is named in the import report). A catalog import also dropped each photo's Point Color swatches (Lightroom keeps them in a different form there than in XMP); they now become Color editor ranges as presets already did, and a mask's own swatch variance (`LocalColorVariance`) carries over. A photo corrected with Guided Upright arrived uncorrected from a catalog; its guide lines now become Rawtonic's Guided Upright lines on the same edges, also where Lightroom's lens profile was on, with Constrain crop unless Lightroom's own crop came along. From edits made before process version 2012, catalog and XMP imports keep Exposure and explicit non-default tone curves (the old default Medium Contrast curve is left out, as in Lightroom's newer process; imported legacy curves are an approximation and are named in the import warning), and report the legacy Brightness, Contrast, Fill Light, Highlight Recovery, Shadows and Clarity values you changed that cannot be converted yet (Lightroom's own defaults for those controls are not reported). Saved edits render as before.
- **Exports of large RAWs need far less memory on a Mac.** Each photo in an export now frees its copies as soon as they are no longer read (the developed photo once it is on the graphics card, the lens and geometry correction's full-size copies, the colour noise reduction's working layers); the colour noise reduction rebuilds its finest level in strips and an export renders in smaller pieces; and a TIFF is no longer built whole in memory: a full-size TIFF (no resize, border, output sharpening, watermark or Super Resolution) goes into its file strip by strip as the graphics card renders it, still under a temporary name until the file is complete, also when a JPEG or another format was asked for before it and both go to the same folder (a full-size TIFF is then saved before the other formats rendered with it; it never passes another TIFF, so every file keeps its name; with separate mask files, or recipes with different destinations, the asked order stays). Each step was measured against the build before it, on a Mac, in the same time and with byte-identical files (8- and 16-bit): a 60 MP 16-bit TIFF peaked 3.3 → 3.0 GB, then 3.1 → 2.6 GB, then 2.7 → 2.4 GB and finally 2.37 → 2.04 GB; a 24 MP one 1.78 → 1.42 GB and later 1.34 → 1.22 GB, a 45 MP one 1.83 → 1.56 GB; a folder of four 24–60 MP RAWs 8.0 → 5.9 GB; a 60 MP photo exported as JPEG then 16-bit TIFF 2.50 → 2.13 GB (as 16-bit TIFF and JPEG 2.46 → 2.12 GB); seven 46–61 MP RAWs as TIFF and JPEG two at a time 4.0 → 3.8 GB and as JPEG then TIFF 4.3 → 4.1 GB. The command-line export also reads the photo's metadata before developing it instead of beside the render.
- **The multi view no longer hides its other photos while the next one opens on Windows, and shows a new selection sooner.** Clicking another cell or stepping in the multi view made the app window opaque until the new primary photo's first frame arrived, which hid every other cell: on a Windows laptop with slow decodes the other photos vanished for 5–44 s at a time. The window now stays clear over the multi view's cells while the new primary opens; only the new primary's cell shows its preview meanwhile (Windows laptop with Radeon 680M, seven 39–61 MP RAWs zoomed, panned and stepped for 60 s: 0 empty cells instead of dozens; six 24 MP RAWs: 0). In one later Windows test with large RAWs a cell still stayed empty for 2.3 s (see Known gaps). Selecting several photos with the multi view on (⌘A on 12) showed the other photos one after another over about a third of a second; now each shows its stored preview one frame after the new layout appears and its developed version replaces it in place a moment later (12 photos on a Mac mini M4 Pro: all shown after 0.10 s instead of 0.34 s, from a screen recording). Cells also load missing exposure details even when the photo has not been opened as primary.
- **AI masks start faster after their first use, and Windows laptops with integrated AMD graphics can keep the AI models loaded during idle shader compiles in a helper process when memory allows.** When an AI model runs on the processor (AI on the CPU, the Windows laptops whose integrated graphics keep AI there, and Subject masks on a Mac without the Core ML files), the app now keeps the model's optimized form in its cache after the first use, so later sessions, also after a restart, skip that preparation: on a Mac mini M4 Pro the Subject model is ready in 0.64 s instead of 1.7 s and the first Subject mask of a session takes 4.3 s instead of 5.5 s; on a Windows laptop with Radeon 680M the model loads in 3.7 s instead of 10.6 s from the second start. Masks and fills compute the same bits as before (checked on the Mac's CPU and on x86 code under Rosetta; Subject 3.8 → 1.1 s there). The first use takes about 1 s longer once and keeps about 1 GB (Settings → Storage, "Prepared AI models", under the AI result limit); small models that prepare in under a second keep nothing. On Windows laptops with integrated AMD graphics, the app unloaded the AI models before each background shader compile, so the next Subject mask loaded them again (7–9 s on the Radeon 680M laptop; a later Subject mask took 21 s instead of 12 s). The models now stay loaded when, after the caches the compile frees anyway, the memory available beside the compile covers its expected peak (4.5 GB) with 2 GB to spare and the app is within its share; otherwise, under memory pressure and around exports they are unloaded as before (the app log gives the numbers of each decision). A long AI mask run also counts as recent use when it finishes, so the next compile no longer treats the models as idle because the mask took more than 20 seconds. On that laptop, in our test, Subject masks after the first were then ready 0.74–0.88 s after the click, without reloading the models, because the app had computed them ahead in the background; a click before that work has finished, or when memory is too short to start it, still waits for the mask to be computed.
- **Damaged RAW files give an error instead of crashing the app, where the decoder can detect the damage; sidecars, presets and dates are sturdier too.** A truncated, partly copied or otherwise damaged RAW file could crash the app while it was read (out-of-range reads, overflowing sizes, allocations of many gigabytes, endless loops in NEF, ORF, PEF, RAF, RW2, ARW, CR2, CR3, IIQ, KDC, MRW, SRW, CRW, X3F, DNG/TIFF and lossless JPEG data). Damage the decoders can detect (sizes, offsets or tables that don't fit the file, compressed data that ends early, invalid codes) now makes the file report that it can't be read. The older uncompressed formats that opened a file whose data ends inside its last row (packed 10- to 16-bit data in ARW, ORF, NEF, SRW, KDC and others) still do, with the missing part of that row black; DNGs whose strip runs past the end of the file still report an error, as before. In tests 95,200 damaged copies of 112 raw.pixls.us sample files (one to ten per format; the mirror's large ARQ, FFF, LRI and STI files were not among them) and 20,000 damaged copies of two generated DNGs and five small generated file headers were read without a crash. Of the 2,016 raw.pixls.us sample files, every one that read before reads exactly as before (pixels, levels, frame, metadata, preview). Reading or merging XMP sidecars and importing Lightroom presets or Capture One styles now limits packet size, nesting and XML structure before the file is parsed, so a damaged or hostile file is refused instead of using up memory; a sidecar over the limit counts as changed elsewhere and is left untouched, and a preset archive (`.zip`, `.costylepack`) gets a clear message to import the single files. Dates are now read, written and converted with the jiff library instead of chrono, which is no longer maintained (RUSTSEC-2026-0543); file names, stored dates, capture-time corrections and exported files are unchanged, and on Linux the system Trash keeps recording when an item was deleted. On Windows, export file times, capture-time tools and filename date tokens use the Windows time zone even when the app is launched with a `TZ` environment variable.

### Fixes

- **Less memory in the multi view with large RAWs.** Zoomed into several 40–60 MP photos, the multi view decoded and enlarged them several at a time and kept up to an eighth of the memory in decodes regardless of what else ran, so the app grew past 10 GB on a 14 GB laptop and 20 GB on a 24 GB Mac. It now starts such work only while the app stays within about 45 % of the memory and the system keeps memory free, one at a time when memory is short, and the background preparation of 1:1 previews waits while an export runs (Mac mini M4 Pro, seven 36–61 MP RAWs, pan or step every 3 s: 20.4 GB → 13.0–13.6 GB, regions as fast; 14 GB laptop with Radeon 680M: 10.4 → 7.8–8.8 GB, an export beside it 101 → 69 s).
- **Less memory while exporting and editing on laptops with little free memory.** A batch export counted every free gigabyte as room for one more photo in flight, although it keeps 2 GB free for the editor and other apps. On a 14 GB laptop with shared graphics memory (Radeon 680M) three 16-bit TIFFs were exported at once and the app reached 7.8 GB when a photo was opened in Develop meanwhile. The free memory the export keeps is now left out when it counts how many photos to export at once: two there, 1.25–1.5 GB less, for 0–8 % more export time. Computers with plenty of free memory still export four at once.
- **NVIDIA: the first edits right after the first start wait far less for graphics preparation.** On a computer whose graphics driver had not prepared Rawtonic yet (first start, driver update), a photo opened right away showed later and an early Highlights edit could wait about 8.5 s. Display frames now render in smaller pieces that the driver prepares sooner, and the variant every Basic-panel slider needs is prepared right after the first one. Measured on an NVIDIA RTX 3080 (Linux) with a fresh profile: the photo shows after 6.4–7.3 s instead of 10.7–11.1 s, and no first Exposure, Contrast, Highlights, Shadows, Whites, Blacks, Clarity, Dehaze, Vibrance or Saturation edit waits more than 1.1 s (before: 8.5 s). Opening Crop for the first time on such a computer could leave the photo without its frame and handles for 13–45 s, and every other change waited with it; Crop now waits no longer than any other change (0.5 s both right after the photo opened and a minute later; before: 14 s and 13 s). On AMD Radeon integrated graphics under Windows nothing changes yet: the very first edit there can still wait 6–9 s. Exports are unchanged.
- **Smoother slider drags with the histogram or scopes open.** Each scope update during a drag re-rendered the histogram, curve, Levels and scope panels; the new histogram or scope image is now written straight into the page. On a 2560×1440 Linux window at 100 %, an Exposure drag with the RGB waveform open waits 2 ms more for the preview renderer's reply at p95 than with the scopes panel closed (21 vs 19 ms; 95 % interval 0…+3 ms; measured up to that reply, before the WebView decodes and shows the image), and it has an estimated 3.11 missed WebView animation-frame callbacks per two-second drag against 2.33 with the panel closed (2.56 with analytics switched off; 95 % interval −0.06…+1.28 against the analytics-off baseline; each callback gap is rounded to whole 60 Hz periods and the periods beyond the first are summed). Clipping marks and exports are unchanged.
- **Thumbnails appear right after a fast scroll through a large folder.** In the Library grid, the filmstrip and its docked column, a fling or scrollbar drag through hundreds of photos revealed the thumbnails of every row it had passed before those of the tiles where it stopped. A screen's cached thumbnails now arrive together, are looked up apart from thumbnails still being made, and the tiles in view are shown first. Measured with cached thumbnails, release to every tile in view filled: Linux, 1,200 photos, median 245 → 60 ms; Windows laptop, about 1,160 photos, median 133 → 88 ms for scrollbar drags, wheel flings 103–131 ms after the fix.
- **Library thumbnails use less disk and survive engine updates.** Camera previews stay cached across RAW engine changes; saving an edit removes its superseded developed thumbnail after the replacement is stored. Existing cache leftovers still follow the storage limit.
- **A moved session shows its thumbnails at once.** After Move the Whole Session (and its Undo and Redo) the Library developed every thumbnail again because thumbnails were remembered by the photo's full path: 7.9 s until the first screen of a 100-photo session was back on Linux. Thumbnails of photos in a session or catalog are now remembered by the library and the photo's place in it, so the session is back with its thumbnails in under half a second; after this update each library's thumbnails are made once more.
- **Empty Trash keeps photos recoverable.** Sessions and catalogs move their Trash contents to the computer's Trash, with Undo for each moved entry. If a move fails, the remaining entries stay in the library Trash; permanent deletion requires a separate confirmation with Cancel focused.
- **A photo moved to the Trash leaves Previous Import, albums and searches in a catalog too.** In a catalog (not in a session) a deleted photo went to the catalog's Trash folder but stayed in Previous Import, in albums and in the grid, filmstrip and count, unmarked; it is now left out, as in a session, until it is restored. Delete on a photo, or a folder, that is already in the library's Trash folder now says that it goes on to the computer's Trash ("Move Out of the Library's Trash Folder?") instead of promising the Trash folder again; ⌘Z / Ctrl+Z still brings it back.
- **Culling while an import runs keeps your place.** When an import ended, the Library switched to Previous Import and emptied the selection, so every rating key pressed after that rated nothing, with no message. If you pressed a key, clicked or scrolled while the import ran, the view, selection and open photo now stay as they are, and the import's notice offers **Show** to open the new photos. A rating, flag or label key with no photo selected now says so.
- **Rating keys work with on-screen keyboards, remote desktop and automation tools.** Key presses that arrive without the key's physical code (Windows on-screen keyboard, remote desktop, AutoHotkey and other tools that send keys without a scan code) were ignored for the digits, arrows, Home/End, Delete and other named keys, so stars typed with 1–5 were never set while P and X worked. Such a key now acts by the key it typed.
- **Coming back to an edited photo adds no History step.** After editing a photo, opening another one and coming back, History showed an extra step nobody made — on a Fujifilm RAF named "Color grading, Color mixer +2" — because the edit read back from its save (with its colour label, its lens stamp and, for a camera's own lens data, numbers rounded in their last digit) counted as a change. History now shows only the steps made, and a step read back from its save is named for what changed (e.g. "Shadows +100").
- **Opening the Crop tab is no edit.** On a photo without a crop, opening Crop and leaving it again added a "Crop" step to History, enabled Undo, marked the photo as edited and wrote its sidecar. Nothing is written now until the frame, angle or ratio is changed, as in Lightroom and Capture One.
- **Move to Trash in the Library loupe stays in the loupe.** The loupe (and the Library's full screen) now goes on to the next photo, or the previous one at the end of the list, as Develop already did; before, it dropped back to the grid. Moving the shown photo to another folder or to Selects (⌘J / Ctrl+J) goes on to the next photo the same way in the loupe, full screen and Develop, instead of returning to the grid.
- **New Virtual Copy shows the copy.** The new copy is selected beside its photo (in Develop and the loupe it opens), and a message names it ("Made virtual copy 2 of A.CR3"); Previous Import lists virtual copies beside their photos, and a view that does not show the copy says where it is. Before, nothing showed that a copy was made, so it was easy to make several.
- **Deleting an export recipe asks first.** The × beside a recipe asks "Delete the recipe …?" in its row, with Cancel focused, as deleting a preset does; before, one click deleted it.
- **Quitting while exports or imports run asks first.** ⌘Q, ⌘W, Alt+F4 and the window's close button ask "Exports or Imports Are Running" with Keep Working as the default when an export is running or waiting in the queue or an ingest, catalog import or Add Folder is running; Quit Anyway quits as before (an interrupted export can be resumed from Activity). A hot folder needs no question: it resumes at the next start and takes the files it has not copied yet.
- **An export interrupted at the last quit is named in your language.** After a relaunch the activity entry read "Export interrupted" with a "Resume" button in English while the rest of the window was German (or another language); it now follows the interface language.
- **Edit With: Try again after a failed render, and a better program picker on Windows.** When some photos of an Edit With could not be rendered (for example because their folder was locked), the notice now has Try again, which renders only those photos (the finished renders are not made a second time) once the session or catalog you started in is open again. On Windows, Browse for a program… opens on top of the app window, starts in Program Files, lists program files only, no longer opens the picked file to check it (which refused the app shortcuts in `WindowsApps`, such as Paint's), and says why when it fails. Edit With writes each render, its library and the hand-off to the app log.
- **Change brush size and feather right on the photo.** With a mask brush, the eraser or Heal, Clone and Remove (including the object brush), a right-click (two-finger click, Ctrl+click) opens the tool's Size, Feather/Hardness, Opacity and Flow at the pointer; the ring stays where you clicked and shows each change. Enter applies a typed value and closes, Esc or a click outside closes and keeps what you set, and a Photo menu… row still opens the photo's own menu (Undo, Copy, Rating).
- **See the brush size while retouching.** Heal, Clone and Remove now show a ring on the photo, including the object brush. It follows zoom and size changes from the panel slider or [ and ] keys. Moving or resizing an existing spot keeps its own cursor.
- **[ and ] size the brush on every keyboard, never turning the photo.** On Swiss and German keyboards the keys right of P (Ü and ¨, or Ü and +), which carry Rotate, did nothing for the brush or turned the photograph and added a History step while a brush, Heal, Red eye or face brush was active; they now change its size (with Shift the hardness) like typed [ and ] (AltGr or Option), with no History step, as the panel's sliders do. The face brush's hint names those keys as your keyboard types them.
- **Every menu shows each command's key, named for your keyboard.** The menu bar (macOS), the ⋯ menu (Windows, Linux) and the right-click menus show the key of each command at the row's right — single keys too (ratings, flags, views), and your own keys when you changed them. Keys are named as your keyboard layout types them: on a Swiss or German Mac, New Virtual Copy shows ⌘Ä and Rotate shows Ü, the keys that run them (before, the menu showed ⌘^, which zoomed instead); ⌘+ and ⌘- zoom on every layout. Preferences and tooltips write keys the platform's way (⇧⌘E on a Mac, Ctrl+Shift+E elsewhere).
- **F10 or Alt opens the menu on Windows and Linux**, and the arrow keys, Enter, Esc and a row's first letter work in every menu, as in other Windows and KDE applications.
- **The bars above the grid and the photo stay one row.** On a 13-inch laptop, at 1280 × 800 or with the filmstrip docked beside the photo, the Library's filter bar and Develop's viewer bar no longer wrap to two or three rows: words beside icons give way first (their names stay as tooltips), then field widths; the viewer bar's Camera preview, Proof margin, zoom presets and finally Fit and 100 % move into a ⋯ menu. The eyes filter shows once Eye check is on (Preferences ▸ General).
- **One name for each thing.** Bringing photos in is "Import" everywhere; the library kinds are "session" and "catalog" (no more "project"), and texts in a catalog say catalog (its Trash folder, Output folder, Import, Hot folder and Tether tooltips); the settings window is "Settings" everywhere, and what you copy, paste, sync or reset between photos is "Adjustments".
- **Tab moves from value field to value field in Develop.** Typing a number in a slider's field and pressing Tab (Shift+Tab back) now commits it and selects the next tool's value (the next open one), so a column of numbers can be typed without the mouse, as in Lightroom; Enter commits, Esc reverts. Before, Tab went to the next slider and the typed digits did not reach a field.
