- **Home and End go to the first and last photo** in the Library grid, the loupe, full screen and Develop's filmstrip (in a multi view, of the selection).
- **Quieter activity warnings and predictable slider resets.** Warnings and failures stay as a dot and count in the top bar; double-clicking a Develop slider resets it in one undo step.
- **Two-finger scrolling pans the photo on a Mac.** A trackpad or Magic Mouse scroll over the zoomed photo now moves it, in both directions, and a pinch zooms; a mouse wheel zooms as before (Shift or Option + wheel pans). Before, a two-finger swipe zoomed — a sideways one too — unless Preferences ▸ Controls was set to Touchpad. macOS tells the two apart by itself, so that preference is gone there; on Windows and Linux it stays, and a touchpad pinch zooms. After the page had lost the app's note of which device was scrolling (for example after a reload), a trackpad scroll zoomed back to fit and a sideways scroll did nothing until a mouse wheel was used; the app now repeats the note at the start of every trackpad gesture.
- **The window comes back at its own size on a Retina Mac.** The window's size and place were restored as twice their size after every relaunch on a Retina or scaled display; they now come back as they were left.
- **The interface starts in the system's language.** On a first start, and whenever no language is chosen in Preferences, Rawtonic shows its interface in the system's language when it is one of the 13 it ships (de-CH → German, pt-BR → Portuguese, zh-Hant → Traditional Chinese), else in English. Preferences ▸ General lists "System language" first; a language chosen there wins. A language that was already saved stays as it is.
- **Linux: file and folder dialogs are the desktop's own.** They go through the XDG desktop portal, so KDE Plasma shows its dialog with the user's places and bookmarks (GNOME its own); GTK's chooser remains when no portal answers. Every dialog says in its title what is chosen ("Choose where the new library is created") and opens where the last dialog for the same purpose was answered, else in the library's folder or Pictures — never in "Recently used". Setting `GTK_USE_PORTAL=0` keeps GTK's chooser.
- **Linux: the window's edge shows and its title bar has the window menu.** Without the system title bar, a 1 px light outline marks the window's edge while it is not maximized, and a right-click on the empty part of the top bar opens the desktop's window menu (Keep above, Move to desktop or screen, …). The system title bar in Preferences still gives the desktop's own frame and shadow. Linux uses the system interface font, and packaged desktop entries offer supported photos in Open With.
- **Dark native controls and tidier Import actions.** Native select menus and number steppers follow the dark interface, and Import's Maximize button sits beside Close.
- **XMP titles, captions and rights with translations save reliably.** Sidecar edits preserve title, caption, rights and IPTC language alternatives, handle stale duplicate properties and quoted `>` qualifiers while preserving nested foreign content, and clearing the displayed default keeps the translations.
- Values that round to zero no longer show a minus sign in sliders, Series Match or merge readouts.
- Updated frontend libraries (minor versions).

### Changes to existing edits

- **Color Grading and .cube LUTs change only new edits.** New Color Grading edits use `colorGradingVersion` 4 (part of the tint per colour channel); a .cube with its own input range is read over that range (`lutVersion` 2). Saved edits keep their recorded versions, so photos you have already edited keep their look. Byte-for-byte comparisons of saved edits against the build before: on Apple silicon 280–326 test exports (16-bit TIFF, command line and app) after each change of this release that touches the render, identical; on NVIDIA under Linux 326 16-bit TIFF exports (command line and app), identical, on main at `206572e4` against `65ed343b` (from before the export memory changes) and for the eight-range mask change on its own (that set holds no .cube edits; saved .cube edits were compared on Apple silicon only: every recipe without a LUT version or with version 1 identical, on two RAWs × 24 synthetic LUT recipes); Color Grading versions 2 and 3 also on 96 exports of their own on Apple silicon and 64 under Linux. Windows was not compared.
- **JPEG XL exports of opaque photos have no alpha channel.** They were written with an opaque alpha channel; they now use three RGB channels, so their bytes differ from earlier JPEG XL exports. A lossless JPEG XL decodes to the same pixels as before; a lossy one is encoded without the alpha channel and was not compared pixel for pixel with an earlier export. If the rendered image contains transparency, JPEG XL keeps its alpha channel. Other formats are unchanged.
- **Imports bring more over.** Importing the same Capture One or Lightroom photo again now gives local layers, the lens correction, all Point Color ranges, Guided Upright and old-process curves and Exposure where the earlier import left them out (see Highlights). Photos already imported are not changed.
- **Each library's thumbnails are made once more** after this update, because they are now remembered by the library and the photo's place in it; edits are not touched.

### Known gaps

These are measured shortfalls or open work; they stay open until they are closed by measurement.

- **Export memory on a 14 GB Windows laptop (Radeon 680M):** seven 50–61 MP RAWs as 16-bit TIFF and JPEG, two at a time, still peak at 7.5 GB, above the 6.6 GB target (6.1 GB one at a time); this release's TIFF streaming did not lower it there. The multi view with large RAWs still reaches 7.8–8.8 GB on that laptop, and in one Windows test after the fix a cell stayed empty for 2.3 s.
- **The first AI Subject mask on a fresh Windows computer** (Radeon 680M laptop, AI on the processor) still takes about 24 s: about 9 s to prepare the model, 1.6 s to keep its optimized form and 15 s to compute the mask. Later masks were ready within a second in our test when the app had computed them ahead.
- **The scopes panel opens only with the A key** for now; a View menu and command palette entry is in work. On Linux an open scope still costs a few milliseconds per frame in the fit view while dragging; on a Mac the measured cost is within the noise.
- **.cube LUTs with their own input range:** the check that saved edits export byte for byte as before is done on Apple silicon, not yet on NVIDIA under Linux.
- **Imports:** Lightroom's pre-2012 curves are an approximation and not yet compared with Lightroom's renders. Guided Upright from Lightroom catalogs is not yet imported for Sony RAWs set to another aspect ratio in the camera. Capture One's brush strokes and colour ranges come over as fixed masks that cannot be edited as strokes or ranges, and a Capture One linear gradient on a lens-corrected photo stays within 2.3 % (0.3 % on average) of a mask that follows the correction exactly, because a straight Rawtonic gradient cannot follow the correction's slight bow.
- **Multi view:** entering 12 photos shows them all after 0.10 s; Lightroom shows them about one frame sooner on the same Mac.
- **Sidecars:** an XMP sidecar with more than 250,000 XML elements (far beyond normal brush work) is refused and treated as changed elsewhere.
- **Accessibility:** the Undo and Redo buttons in the toolbar have no accessible name for screen readers.
- **Windows** stays experimental: unsigned, tested on one NVIDIA card and one AMD Radeon 680M laptop.
- The known gaps of alpha.16 and earlier not named here remain.

## 0.1.0-alpha.16 — 2026-11-16

The sixteenth alpha. Develop's filmstrip can be docked beside the photo as a vertical grid, and unedited photos can start from develop defaults of your own — globally, per camera model or per camera body. For new edits, raised Shadows no longer leaves a light rim beside strong edges and radial and linear gradients fade as Camera Raw's do. On Windows with AMD graphics the first edits on a new computer wait at most about 9 s instead of 37 s, exports of new edits with noise reduction or sharpening are about a tenth faster, and the import view keeps the photograph on screen while zooming and stepping.

### Highlights

- **Develop's filmstrip can sit beside the photo as a vertical grid.** View ▸ Panels (or the buttons in its header) puts it under the photo as before, or docks it in a column on the left or right; dragging its header to a window edge docks it there, with a preview of the place. The column has its own header with the count, a quick filter (rating, pick/reject flag, colour label — the library's filter, so the arrow keys step through what it shows) and a file-name switch; Ctrl/⌘ + wheel sets the thumbnail size and the column's width can be dragged. It uses the same thumbnails, selection and keys as the strip, mounts only the photos in view, and is remembered with the layout. The strip under the photo is unchanged.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: the first edits on a new computer no longer wait half a minute.** On graphics drivers that compile slowly (AMD on Windows), the develop view is now prepared in three smaller parts — before the tone sliders, the tone sliders with Highlights, and the rest — which that driver compiles far faster than the one large view: on a Radeon 680M laptop with a fresh profile, the longest wait in the first minute of editing (Basic sliders, Glow, a brush, before/after, a JPEG and a 16-bit TIFF export) fell from 36.7 s to 8.7 s, and the helper that prepares views in the background used 1.1 GB instead of 4.5 GB. Dragging got slightly faster there too (1.7–3 ms less per 2560×1440 frame). Photos look and export exactly as before. In side by side, the before pane now shows the edited photo while its before is prepared instead of staying empty. Other graphics cards are not affected.
- **Raised Shadows leaves no light rim beside strong edges** (new edits; saved ones keep their look). Next to a dark area, the bright side of a strong edge (a sky above a skyline, a window frame) was lifted as if it were shadow, a light band a few pixels wide: 0.94 L* at Shadows +100 in the median over 48 test photographs, where Camera Raw's is 0.36–0.43. It is now 0.42 L*, and on 16 skyline edges 1.55 → 0.47 L*. Broad shadows lift as before (the darkest quarter 14.37 → 14.35 L* in the median, up to 0.7 L* less where textured shadows meet bright areas) and keep their fine detail; medium-scale texture in lifted shadows is a little flatter at +100 (now 0.08 below Camera Raw's in our measure, before 0.02 above).
- **The measurements of a full RAW decode take 86–96 ms less per 24 MP photo.** Every full decode (import dialog 100 % view, opening in the editor, export) measures the photo's noise level, tonal key and haze level, which noise reduction, Shadows/Highlights and Dehaze adapt to; these now run on all processor cores and read the photo once instead of twice: 120 → 34 ms on a 6-core/12-thread Ryzen 5 3600X (Sony A6400 ARW, Fujifilm X-T5 RAF, Canon CR3; at 12 and 6 threads). That stage alone was timed, not the whole open, import view or export. The measured values, and so every render and export, are bit for bit what they were.
- **Faster exports of new edits with noise reduction or sharpening** (`renderVersion` 13, new edits and never-edited photos): the export computes noise reduction and sharpening in a pass of its own and the rest of the develop in a smaller one, as the editor's display already does. A batch of 20 NEF photos (16–36 MP, noise reduction and sharpening on) exported as JPEGs in 16.1 s instead of 18.1 s on a Mac mini M4 Pro. The result looks the same; the GPU rounds a few values differently: in our 280 test exports (10 cameras) by at most one level of 65,535 in a 16-bit TIFF on at most 0.16 % of the pixels, in GPU tests on generated noise by up to 5 levels of 65,535. Saved edits export byte for byte as before.
- **Develop defaults: what unedited photos start with, per camera and per camera body.** Preferences → Develop defaults sets a global default preset or style, and lists the defaults of camera models and single camera bodies (by serial number), set in Develop by right-clicking a preset or the photo (“Set as default for <camera>”) or from a tool's menu (“Save tool settings as default for <camera>”, one tool at a time). A body's default wins over its model's, a model's over the global one; masks, crop, transform and retouching never become defaults, and by default only RAW files get them. Photos without an edit start with them at import and when opened, and follow when the defaults change; edited photos never change. Reset goes back to the photo's defaults, the new Reset to Neutral (Edit menu, context menus) to the neutral start, and Before shows the photo with its defaults. Nothing changes until a default is set.
- **Radial and linear gradients fade as in Lightroom** (new gradients; saved ones keep their look). A radial gradient's feather was a straight ramp that ended exactly on the ellipse, so at the default feather it lost its effect early and at feather 100 dropped from the very centre. It now holds the full effect longer and fades out smoothly just beyond the ellipse, as Camera Raw's does: at feathers 10, 25, 50, 75 and 100 the points where 90 %, 50 % and 10 % of the effect are left (medians over 12 photographs, Exposure +1; the curve fitted on 8, checked on the other 4) lie within 3.5 % of the radius of Camera Raw's medians, the 50 % point within 1 %. A linear gradient now eases in after its 100 % line and out before its 0 % line instead of changing at one even rate; its 50 % line stays where it was. The canvas draws a new radial's inner, central and outer lines where 98 %, 50 % and 2 % of the effect are left. Lightroom imports get the new fade too; edits made before keep theirs.

### Fixes

- **The app starts through a symlinked folder or link on macOS** (for example a link on an external drive or in `/usr/local/bin`); before, it aborted with "failed to resolve resource directory". Linux and Windows were not affected. The error for truly missing resources now names the executable.
- **Windows: no crash when the window needs another graphics setup than the one the app started with.** When the first graphics device (Vulkan) cannot show the window — on computers with two graphics cards, or when the window needs DirectX 12 — the app sets up its graphics a second time; shader compiles of the first setup still running then could pick the new setup's pipeline cache and crash the app. Each setup now uses only its own cache.
- **Import: Z and the arrow keys no longer show an empty frame on Windows.** In the import dialog's large view, Z (or a click) to 100 % and back, and stepping to the next photograph, showed nothing for a moment (65–98 ms measured on a Windows laptop after Z) before the enlarged preview or the real pixels appeared, because the webview draws a newly made image only once it has decoded it again. The photograph now stays on screen: the shown preview itself grows to the 100 % size, the full-size pixels replace it only once they are ready to draw, and when stepping the previous photograph stays up until the next one can be drawn, as in Lightroom. Measured in Chromium (the engine of Windows' webview) on Linux with the real view: 0 empty frames in 18 zooms and 6 steps (before: an empty frame after each). "100 %" now appears when its pixels are on screen.


### Changes to existing edits

- **Shadows, gradients and the export pass change only new edits.** New edits use tone response 19 (`toneVersion` 19: raised Shadows without the light rim), new radial and linear gradients use `gradientVersion` 2 (Camera Raw-like fade; Lightroom imports get it too), and new edits and never-edited photos export with `renderVersion` 13 (noise reduction and sharpening in their own pass: at most one level of 65,535 in a 16-bit TIFF on at most 0.16 % of the pixels in our 280 test exports). Photos you have already edited keep their look and export byte for byte as before (checked on 280 exports on Apple silicon and 326 on NVIDIA under Linux).
- **Develop defaults change nothing until one is set.** Once a default is set, photos without an edit (RAW files only, by default) start with it and follow when it changes; edited photos never change.

### Known gaps

These are measured shortfalls or open work; they stay open until they are closed by measurement.

- **Docked filmstrip:** after a very fast scroll through a large folder, tiles stay blank for about 3.5–4 s before their thumbnails appear.
- **Windows with AMD graphics (Radeon 680M):** importing a 24 MP Sony ARW, the 100 % view's real pixels take 2.8–3.1 s; raised Shadows costs about 1.5 ms more per frame while dragging; the first use of Halation or Grain on top of Glow still prepares a larger view once per computer.
- **Masks:** a mask's Shadows and Clarity act as the global sliders do, which is stronger than Camera Raw's local ones in places (a deliberate choice for now).
- **Export speed** is still below Capture One's (the export is limited by the graphics card).
- **Windows** stays experimental: unsigned, tested on one NVIDIA card and one AMD Radeon 680M laptop.
- The known gaps of alpha.15 and earlier not named here remain.

## 0.1.0-alpha.15 — 2026-11-15

The fifteenth alpha. Photos can be filed in keyword hierarchies (Animals > Dogs) with a Keyword List, Undo for keyword changes and drag-and-drop of photos onto a keyword. For new edits the colour mixer follows Camera Raw more closely for Blues and Purples and Color Grading tints where Camera Raw puts them. The Develop fit view and the grid thumbnail now show the export's colour noise reduction on Apple silicon and discrete graphics cards, the Before of a RAW includes the sharpening and noise reduction it gets at import, and each before/after mode has a key of its own. Linux gets Open With and Edit With and a 100 % view as sharp as the export, lossless JPEG XL exports reopen and encode far faster, and the CLI exports several photos at once. On Windows with AMD graphics the first start and the first edits prepare their views without piling up memory, and exports, AI masks and view preparation take turns. A library opened by its path, one duplicate rule everywhere and a rating that survives a catalog that had registered a file twice fix several ways of losing track of photos.

### Highlights

- **Exports use less processor time, and the command line exports several photos at once.** The RAW steps before the graphics card renders a photo (demosaicing, hot-pixel repair, the automatic chromatic aberration measurement and the shadow black-level restore) do the same arithmetic in fewer processor steps, so every exported file is byte for byte what it was. Measured on a Mac mini M4 Pro shared with other work (its graphics card was also busy with other programs during the runs), 20 and 48 Nikon NEFs exported full size as JPEG with a current edit: 16 % less processor time (73 → 61 and 157 → 134 CPU-seconds), wall time 13.2 → 12.9 s and 30.0 → 29.7 s; Capture One's speed is not reached. `rawtonic export` of a folder now renders up to four photos at once in the app's export workers instead of one after the other: 27.6 → 14.8 s for the 20 NEFs (the app: 12.9 s).
- **Colour mixer: Blues and Purples follow Camera Raw more closely, and dragging a mixer slider is smoother** (new edits; saved edits keep their look). Blues Hue, Saturation and Luminance and Purples Saturation were measured again on 60 photographs of skies, water and violet flowers (30 to fit, 30 to check): darkening a sky with Blues Luminance now reaches the paler blue towards the horizon as Camera Raw's does, Blues Saturation +100 and Purples Saturation +100 raise the colours Camera Raw raises (Purples Saturation +100 was far too weak on magenta-purples), and turning Blues Hue no longer brightens or darkens the sky beside it. A mixer drag costs about a fifth of the extra time per frame it cost before (measured on a Mac mini M4 Pro, 2304×1728 view: +0.7 ms over the first mixer instead of +4.0 ms); edits made with the previous mixer response get faster too (+2.0 ms) and export byte-identically.
- **Hierarchical keywords and a Keyword List.** Typing `Animals > Dogs` (Lightroom's form), `Animals|Dogs` (Capture One's) or `Dogs < Animals` in the Keywords field now files Dogs under Animals instead of making one keyword with `>` in its name; a single name the library has exactly once in its tree (`dogs`) is that keyword, and the levels of a path take the spelling the library already has. Keywords show their path, and autocomplete offers paths. The new Keyword List tool in the Library shows every keyword of the library as a tree with how many photos hold it or a keyword under it: a click shows those photos (a parent finds its children's photos too, also in the Library filter's keyword column and in smart albums), dragging a keyword onto another moves it there, and its menu renames (a name the library has merges the two), moves to the top level and converts a keyword typed as `a > b` before this change; each change says how many photos it rewrites and waits for a confirmation. XMP keeps writing every level to `dc:subject` and the path to `lr:hierarchicalSubject`, as Lightroom does, in sidecars and exported JPEG and TIFF files; hierarchies read from Lightroom, Capture One and Bridge sidecars and imported Lightroom catalogs and Capture One sessions arrive in the tree. Keywords you have keep their form.
- **Develop (Mac with Apple silicon, Windows with a dedicated graphics card): colour noise reduction in the fit view looks as the export does.** The fit view is rendered from a reduced copy of the photo, and the colour noise filter run on that copy left 1.5–3 times the colour noise of the export at the same size (and less on some cameras), so the fit view looked noisier or cleaner than the file you export. After a colour noise setting changes (or a photo opens), the filter now runs once on the full-resolution photo in the background and the fit view and the photo's grid thumbnail show its result; while a slider is dragged the view shows the quick filter as before. Measured on 7 RAWs at Color 25/50/100 (MacBook Pro M5 Pro): the fit view's colour noise per frequency band now lies within 15 % of the export scaled down in every cell of the 4 main test RAWs (before: 60–68 % of cells outside), with the corrected view shown about 0.8 s after a change (0.3 s pause, 0.4 s for the full-resolution filter, the frame) and about 50–90 MB kept per open photo. On Windows and Intel Macs with a dedicated graphics card the full-resolution filter is the export's own GPU filter, about 0.3 s per change for a 24 MP photo (RTX 3080, measured under Linux; the Windows app's timing on such a card is not measured yet), with the same result (on Linux, measured there: within 15 % of the export in every cell of the 4 main test RAWs). *(Corrected in the next release: these figures were measured against a reference without the export's lens warp, automatic chromatic aberration correction and edge-fringe treatment; against the real export 30–40 % of the cells were outside.)* The Library loupe of other photos still uses the quick filter, and so does the fit view on integrated graphics (a Radeon 680M laptop took 0.9–2.9 s and 1–1.7 GB of memory per change) and on Linux, whose previews have no way yet to show a frame the editor did not ask for.
- **Color Grading tints as Lightroom's does** (new edits): the Shadows and Highlights wheels now put a colour where Camera Raw puts it — the direction of each hue, how far the tint reaches into the midtones and how strong it is in the light tones were off (a green highlight tint was up to 28 % too strong at the bright end, a warm shadow tint sat 12° off). Balance moves the ranges closer to how Camera Raw's does (−50 / +50 used to move them too far), and lowering the Highlights luminance no longer darkens near-white tones more than the lights below them. Fitted to Camera Raw on 24 photographs and checked on 24 others; wheels at saturation 100 and a green shadow tint in near-black tones still differ. Photos you have already graded keep their look; Lightroom presets with Color Grading or Split Toning import with the new response.
- **Before shows a RAW as imported.** The whole-photo before (toggle, split, side by side, second window) now includes the capture sharpening and noise reduction a RAW gets at import, like Lightroom and Capture One, instead of a zero-detail render; before and after differ by your edit only. A compared snapshot still wins; a tool's own before is unchanged.
- **Color Grading: strong tints, Luminance, Blending and Balance as in Lightroom** (new edits): four things Camera Raw does that a plain colour shift missed. A strong tint of a very dark or very light tone is now limited per colour channel, as Lightroom limits it, instead of being pulled back along its hue at the output — a green Shadows tint at saturation 50 kept only about 90 % of its strength and half of its yellow in near-black tones. Shadows and Highlights wheels above saturation 50 change as Lightroom's do: the tint stops growing in proportion and the tone gets lighter or darker with the hue (an orange Shadows tint at 100 lightens the shadows by about 4 L\*, a blue Highlights tint darkens the lights by about 4). The Luminance sliders of all four wheels now work like a tone curve on each channel, so they change saturated colours the way Lightroom's do instead of only their lightness. The Midtones and Global wheels follow Lightroom's tint directions through the tonal range, and Blending 100 and Balance ±100 place the two ranges closer to Lightroom's. Checked against Camera Raw on 48 photographs (24 of them not used for fitting). Photos you have already graded keep their look.
- **Before/after: a key for each mode, and the before pane pans.** ⌥Y (Alt+Y) shows the split top/bottom, ⇧⌥Y side by side and ⇧⌘Y (Shift+Ctrl+Y) the split left/right, each in one press (pressing it again turns before/after off), beside Y (the last mode) and ⇧Y (the cycle); the View menu lists them, and every key can be changed in Preferences. Shortcuts with ⌥/Alt and a letter now follow the letter the key types, so ⌥Y is the Y key on German and Swiss keyboards too. In side by side, dragging in the before pane pans both photos, and a click there zooms both, as in the after pane.
- **Linux: Open With and Edit With list the programs your desktop offers.** The submenus showed "Not available on Linux yet". They now list the applications whose `.desktop` entry handles the file type (system, user, Flatpak and Snap), your default first, then recent ones, plus *Browse for a program…* for any `.desktop` file or executable. Edit With renders beside the original, stacks it and refreshes the thumbnail when the program saves, as on macOS and Windows.

### Fixes

- **Keywords: undo, drag photos onto a keyword, keywords without photos.** Adding or removing a keyword on photos and every Keyword List change (rename, merge, move, convert, delete) is now one step in Undo and Redo, named in the Edit menu (*Undo Add Keyword “Animals > Dogs”*, *Undo Merge Keywords*); an undo puts back only what the step changed, so a keyword added to a photo since stays. Dragging selected photos from the grid onto a keyword in the Keyword List adds it to all of them (virtual copies only when they are selected). *New Keyword…* in the Keyword List (also inside a keyword, from its menu) makes a keyword before any photo has it; it is listed with 0 photos and stays until deleted, as in Lightroom. A keyword's menu now offers *Delete Keyword…*: photos lose it and the keywords under it move up one level; *Delete Unused Keywords…* (from the + button) removes every keyword no photo has. Both ask first and say how many photos they change.
- **Library grid: Shift+arrow extends the selection.** In the Library grid ⇧← ⇧→ ⇧↑ ⇧↓ moved the active photo and selected only it. They now select every photo from the one you started on to the new one, and the range shrinks again when you go back, as in Lightroom and Capture One. A plain arrow selects one photo again and starts a new range from there. A Shift-click keeps the start of the range too, so a following Shift-click or Shift+arrow moves the far end of the same range, and a range stops at the first and last photo instead of wrapping round.
- **Edit all selected: the other photos follow about twice as fast.** In the multi view, a value typed for all selected photos reached the first of the others only after about a quarter of a second; they now follow right after the primary. Measured on a MacBook Pro M5 Pro with 12 selected photos (typed Exposure +1, from the key to the last changed photo on screen): 0.20–0.26 s, before 0.44–0.46 s (Lightroom Classic's Quick Develop on the same 12 photos: 0.79 s).
- **Import: 100 % shows at once, and the real pixels of the part in view come first.** When Z or a click asks for 100 % in the import dialog's large view while a RAW's full-size pixels are still being made (cameras whose own JPEG is small: Fujifilm, Sony, Panasonic, Olympus), the view now shows the camera's preview enlarged to 100 % straight away, in the same place and framing (as Capture One does), then the real pixels of the part in view as soon as the render has them, then the whole photograph; each replaces the one before in place. The part in view has exactly the pixels of the whole render. Measured on a Mac mini M4 Pro shared with other builds: the enlarged preview within about 0.05–0.25 s, real pixels in view 0.5–1.5 s after Z on 24–40 MP RAWs shown a second before (whole view before: 1.0–2.0 s) and 3.2 s on an 81 MP ORF (before: 5.1 s); a Z right as a RAW appears still waits for its decode.
- **XMP conflicts: the choice for the selected photos.** While XMP files differ from the library, the grid's right-click menu offers *Keep library* and *Use XMP* for the selected photos, like the panel does for one photo or all.
- **A newer edit or another photo no longer waits behind a view that is being prepared.** Where views are compiled once per computer (AMD integrated graphics on Windows), a frame that waited for its view kept the editor busy until the compile ended: turning Halation off again right after its first use on a Radeon 680M, another slider move or another photo showed only after about 50 s. The waiting frame now gives way to the newest request, which shows as soon as its own view is ready (at once when it is already prepared, as with the effect turned off again); the compile goes on in the background and serves the effect's next use. Exports never give way. Measured on a Radeon 680M laptop on a first session: Halation turned back to 0 shows in about 0.1 s (before: after the 50 s compile), and a step to another photo while a Contrast edit waits for its view shows the next photo developed 0.8 s after the key (before: 27 s).
- **Smart album: Return in the name field creates the album.** Typing a name and pressing Return in the *Save as smart album* dialog did nothing; you had to click Create. Return now creates it when the name and rules are complete.
- **Linux: 100 % is as sharp as the export, and before and after match.** At 100 % the photo was shown from a fit-sized raster magnified about four times (the viewer's zoom layer was composited all the time, and WebKitGTK scales a composited layer's bitmap), then from the full-size frame shifted by half a pixel and smoothed; the split's and side by side's before was developed from a quarter-size decode and stretched, and sat 2 px off the after pane. Now the viewer is composited only while it moves, at 100 % the frame's pixels land on whole screen pixels and are drawn unsmoothed, frames at the photo's own size are sent at JPEG quality 99, and the before is developed from the same full-size decode and drawn the same way, in the single view, split and side by side (the second window's 100 % view is placed the same way). Measured on a 24 MP NEF at ISO 1600 (RTX 3080, KDE Plasma Wayland): the screen at 100 % differs from the export by 0.4–0.7 codes on average (was a soft view with a fifth to a fiftieth of the export's fine detail), the before by 0.6–1.0 codes against its own export, and both panes show the same pixels in the same place. Slider drags take as long as before (input to preview median 8–9 ms at 100 %, 21–22 ms at fit); the sharp 100 % frame after an edit arrives about 0.25 s later (13 MB instead of 5 MB per 24 MP frame).
- **A library opened by its path, or by a file of it, opens as that library.** Passing a library's folder (or its catalog file) when starting Rawtonic, or opening it from another program, now opens it like the recent list does, including the one-time repair of photos registered twice, instead of showing it as a plain folder. The notice about merged photos names every photo, and the import view no longer keeps a 100 % preview in memory after it closes.
- **Upright Level levels by the camera's roll and leaves a photo alone without clear lines.** Level took the median tilt of all near-straight lines, including receding floors and converging walls, and turned 8 of 25 test photos further off level than they were. It now uses Auto straighten's measurement (a horizon, or the direction in which the verticals meet, seen from the centre) and changes nothing when the photo has too little support for an answer, saying so as before. On 20 photos with hand-checked level (raw.pixls.us CC0 frames): median error 0.06° (was 0.51°), errors over 1° on 2 photos (was 8), made worse than untouched on 2 (was 8); Camera Raw's Upright Level on the same photos: 0.31°, 3, 4. Auto uses Level when it finds no converging verticals, so there it too now leaves a photo with too few lines unchanged instead of turning it by a guess (on the 25 test photos Auto always found verticals and gave the same results as before). Vertical and Full are unchanged; saved edits render as before.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: Glow, Halation and Grain no longer need more memory to prepare.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver each view of the editor is compiled once per computer, and a view with Glow, Halation and Grain was almost twice the size of the one behind the Basic panel: preparing it took 56 s and 6.0 GB of memory on a Radeon 680M laptop, against 37 s and 4.3 GB without effects. There the three effects now render in small steps of their own around the main one, so every combination uses the Basic panel's view (37 s, 4.3 GB, prepared once from the app's start) plus steps of about 2 s and 0.5 GB, and turning an effect on no longer prepares another view; 16-bit exports use that same view instead of preparing one of their own. Other graphics drivers are unchanged.
- **Windows with AMD integrated graphics: AI masks, exports and view preparation no longer pile up in memory, and an effect's first use is prepared outside the app.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver a view is compiled once per computer in a helper process (about 4.4 GB on a Radeon 680M), and on integrated graphics the AI masks run on the processor (2–2.5 GB while a Subject mask is computed). The mask the editor computes ahead when a photo opens could run beside such a compile or beside an export: the app and its helper reached 8.3–8.5 GB together, the app alone 7.6 GB during a JPEG export. Now these take turns: a mask computed ahead waits for the compile and stops for an export; a mask you click goes first and the compile nobody waits for starts again afterwards (a compile your edit or export waits for finishes first, and a clicked mask waits for a running export); the AI models are unloaded while a view compiles for the first time (the next mask loads them again, about 7 s). And the first use of an effect that is in no prepared view (Halation, Grain) compiled in the app itself, 51 s at 6.7 GB with the photo unchanged; it now compiles in the helper too, with "Preparing graphics for this computer" shown; the photo still waits for it, once per computer. Measured on a Radeon 680M laptop: no compile in the app above 1.5 s, a curve point 0.2 s, and a first session with a photo opened at once 6.3 GB for the app and its helper together (8.5 GB before). Still above 6.6 GB: the first use of Halation or Grain on top of Glow, whose larger view takes about 5.1 GB in the helper (7.0 GB together), once per computer.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: on a computer's first session the editor prepares its views from the start.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver the views behind the Basic panel are compiled once per computer, in a helper process. That began only a few seconds after the first photo was shown and only while nothing was edited, with a smaller view for the tone sliders first and the one for Glow last, so the time spent in the Library before it (an import, culling) was lost: on a Radeon 680M the first Contrast and Shadows edits after opening a photo waited 15–21 s and 44 s, and Glow turned on while editing compiled its own view. The preparation now starts with the app and goes on while you edit, with one view for the whole Basic panel and Glow first (ready about 46 s after the start there); opening a photo meanwhile neither waits for it nor interrupts it. After a minute in the Library the first photo shows in about 0.5 s and every Basic-panel edit and Glow respond in 0.1–0.8 s; a photo opened and edited right at the start still waits once for that view (up to about 40 s). An export of the photo on screen whose view is not prepared yet (a 16-bit TIFF exported right after choosing it, or shortly after another export) now has it prepared first, before the export takes its graphics memory, instead of compiling it beside that memory (7.2 GB of the app's memory before; app and helper together 6.6 GB now); the first such export per computer takes about 40 s, the next about 4 s. Exported files are unchanged.
- **A rating, label, flag or keyword is no longer lost when a catalog had registered one file twice.** When a catalog folder's record pointed at a folder under another spelling than the one it was made for (a copied or moved catalog, a relinked folder, a trailing separator, or the Windows spelling change of earlier versions), adding or importing the same file again gave it a second row: ratings and labels were then saved to one row and read back from the other, so the next Develop edit, or its Undo, wrote the old rating over the new one, and Import from Catalog could add the file once more. A folder is now found by its path whatever its record's id, Import from Catalog never adds a file the catalog already holds, and a catalog that holds such rows merges them into one photo when it opens: the row with the latest ratings, labels and edits is kept, a field it has no value for takes the other row's, the other row's different edit is kept as the snapshot "Before duplicate repair", albums and virtual copies come along, a copy of the catalog database as it was is saved beside it first, and the activity panel says so once. Photo files are not touched.
- **The scan at start reports skipped duplicates reliably; folder counts follow the catalog; the duplicate messages name the skipped file; XMP differences read plainly.** (1) A library reopened as the window starts could scan before the activity panel was listening, so the "duplicate not added" entry never appeared; the panel now asks for the report it missed. (2) A catalog's Folders panel counted the files lying in a folder, so a copy left out as a duplicate made a folder say 3 photos while 2 were listed; it now counts the photos the catalog holds. (3) The import and Add Folder summaries name the file left out ("Canon_R6_copy.CR3 skipped as a duplicate — already in the catalog as Canon_R6.CR3"; for several, a count with an example). (4) The XMP review of a sidecar that differs from the library (including one Rawtonic never read, as after copying a library) no longer says another program changed it; it explains the two choices, Keep library (writes the library's settings into the file) and Use XMP (reads the file's), per photo or for all, and writes nothing until you click.
- **A photo whose sidecar cannot be read no longer hides its identical copy at start.** When two byte-identical new files arrived in a catalog's folder and the first had an unreadable sidecar, both stayed out of the catalog. Now only the file with the unreadable sidecar is left out (and reported as before); the second is added, since it is a duplicate only of a photo that was added.
- **Windows: Maximize works at interface size 125 %, and the Preferences button shows its tooltip there.** Windows 11 placed its snap-layouts flyout as if the window had a title bar of the system's height, so from interface size 125 % it opened over the lower half of the taller maximize button and over the Preferences button: a click on Maximize after resting the pointer on it for a second went to the flyout and did nothing, and Preferences showed no tooltip. The window now tells Windows where its maximize button is drawn, and the flyout opens below it (checked on a 2560×1600 laptop at 200 %, interface sizes 100, 112 and 125 %).
- **A photo opened from outside the library stays out of the Library view.** With a project or catalog open, a loose photo opened in Develop (Open With, a double-click in the file manager) kept showing in the Library's Navigator after switching to the Library, and Metadata said that some selected photos are not part of the project. Going back to the Library now closes the loose photo (its edits are saved) and selects what was selected before it opened; Develop then opens the Library's active photo.
- **A library whose folders are all missing opens on Missing Photos.** When the folder the Library was closed on and every catalog folder were gone from disk (drive away, folders renamed), the Library opened on an empty view with nothing highlighted. It now opens Missing Photos, highlighted, which lists what you are looking for; the folder you closed on stays your saved selection and is restored once it is back.
- **An export that had finished before a quit is no longer reported as interrupted.** When the app was quit just after the last photo of an export was written, the next start logged "was interrupted: 1 of 1 photos exported" although the file was complete. Such an export is now recorded as finished; one with missing photos stays interrupted and can be resumed.
- **One duplicate rule for import, Add Folder and the scan at start.** A file whose content equals a photo the catalog already holds is a duplicate and is not added, whatever its name. Before, a byte-identical copy made while the app was closed was added as a new photo when the catalog opened, while the same copy was skipped by Add Folder and import. Now all three leave it out and say so: the import summary and Add Folder name the photo it copies ("1 duplicate skipped — already in the library as Canon_R6.CR3"), and the scan at start reports it once in the activity panel with Show photos (Synchronize Folder still offers such a file, unticked). A file at its registered place keeps the wording "already in the catalog"; the same name with other content is a photo of its own. Projects keep every file of their folder.
- **Reopening a library returns to the source it was closed on.** The Library used to open on the library's own folder every time, which showed "No photos to display" when the photos lived in an added folder (reported on Windows after quitting on a folder). The app now reopens the folder, collection or Previous Import that was shown when the library was last open, with the same filter and sort; if that source is gone, the first folder of the catalog that is on disk is shown (a catalog whose own folder holds no photographs, because they stay in added folders, no longer opens on an empty view), else the library's own folder.
- **Windows and Linux: the Edit menu names what Undo and Redo will do.** The ⋯ menu showed a plain "Undo" and "Redo" (for example after Import from Catalog); like the macOS menu bar it now reads "Undo Import from Catalog", "Redo Set Rating" and so on, in every language, and follows each change of the history.
- **The second window's toolbar stays on one line and its tooltips stay inside the window.** In a narrow second window "100 %" wrapped onto two lines and the Lock tooltip was cut off at the window's edge. Whenever the window is narrower than the toolbar needs (and only then; widening brings it back), the file name goes and Lock shows its icon only; tooltips now use the app's own tooltip, which wraps within the window.
- **AI masks on the processor use less memory.** When the AI models run on the processor (Windows with AMD graphics), opening Develop now loads only the Subject model ahead; the Sky model loads when it is first needed (about 0.1–0.4 s), and a background mask run first hands back memory the editor can rebuild. On a 24-megapixel photo the peak of the Subject mask dropped by about 0.25–0.4 GB (Mac mini, processor path), with identical masks.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: views prepared in idle time stay prepared when the driver's cache is cleared.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver the views the editor prepares in idle time (in a helper process) were kept only in the driver's own cache; when that cache was cleared (by a cleanup tool or a cache reset) every such view was compiled again, 39–45 s each on a Radeon 680M. They now go into the app's own cache file too, so the next start with the same driver finds them there (a driver update still starts a new cache). Such a helper now also ends at once when the app ends or is closed from the Task Manager, instead of running on for up to 15 minutes. Exports are unchanged.
- **Windows: Open With and Edit With offer the apps Windows itself offers, including Store apps such as Photos.** The submenus listed only the programs Explorer recommends for the file type (Paint, Snipping Tool, Windows Photo Viewer for a TIFF), so the Photos app was missing. They now list every app Windows offers for the type, the recommended ones first and, after a separator, the others from Explorer's *Choose another app* list, with their names and icons. A Store app opens the file through its own registration, as from Explorer, and Edit With picks up its saves as for any program (the render's file is watched, not the program). *Other…* is now *Browse for a program…*, the program file picker; programs used or picked before keep working.
- **Lossless JPEG XL exports reopen in Rawtonic, encode far faster with much less memory, and declare their colour space.** Lossless JPEG XL exports (Quality 100) larger than 256 × 256 pixels could not be opened again in Rawtonic ("Failed to decode image"), although other programs read them; every such export since 0.1.0-alpha.1 opens now, with its exact pixels. Display P3, Adobe RGB and ProPhoto lossless exports were labelled sRGB in the file, so other programs showed their colours wrong; they now declare their space, as the lossy ones do. Lossless JPEG XL now uses libjxl's fast lossless method (zune-jpegxl). Encoding a 36 MP frame takes about 0.1–0.25 s instead of 2.4–4.5 s and peaks at 0.62 GB for the export's 8-bit render (2.96 GB before; writing a TIFF of it peaks at 0.37 GB); four at once peaked at 2.0 GB. A whole 24–26 MP RAW export from the command line (decode, render, encode) peaked at 1.3–1.5 GB, about as much as its 8-bit TIFF export (was 2.5–2.9 GB), in 1.7–1.8 s, and its file is 2–10 % smaller with identical pixels. Lossy JPEG XL and the other formats are unchanged, byte for byte.
- **A Canon CR3 with a few stray bytes after its last block opens.** A CR3 with fewer than 8 bytes after the last block failed with "No decoder found"; such files now open, while a truncated image block still fails with a clear error.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: Glow, Halation and Grain add little to a frame.** With all three effects on at once a frame costs about 1.8 ms more on a Radeon 680M (Grain +0.6 ms, Glow and Halation +1.0 ms, one effect alone less); other graphics cards and drivers are unchanged.

### Changes to existing edits

- **The colour mixer and Color Grading change only new edits.** New edits use mixer response 3 (`hslVersion` 3: Blues and Purples refitted) and Color Grading response 3 (`colorGradingVersion` 3: per-channel tint limit, Luminance per channel, Camera Raw's response above saturation 50); photos you have already edited keep their mixer and Color Grading look and export exactly as before. Lightroom presets with Color Grading or Split Toning import with the new response.
- **Exports of saved photos are unchanged.** The CPU speed-ups of the RAW steps, the lossless JPEG XL encoder (same pixels, the file is 2–10 % smaller) and the AMD effect passes change no exported pixel on Apple silicon, NVIDIA or other graphics drivers; on AMD's Windows Vulkan driver the alpha.14 note on rare one-level differences still applies.
- **Before and thumbnails look different, exports do not:** the whole-photo Before of a RAW now includes import sharpening and noise reduction, and the fit view and grid thumbnail show the export's colour noise reduction on Apple silicon and discrete graphics cards.
- **Upright Level** now levels by the camera's roll and leaves photos without clear lines alone; saved edits render as before.

### Known gaps

These are measured shortfalls or open work; they stay open until they are closed by measurement.

- **Windows with AMD graphics (Radeon 680M):** a photo opened and edited right at the first start of a new computer still waits once for its view, up to about 38 s in the worst case (goal 10 s); the first use of Halation or Grain on top of Glow takes the biggest view (about 5.1 GB in the helper, 7.0 GB together with the app), once per computer; all three effects at once cost about 1.8 ms more per frame (goal 1 ms).
- **Export speed** is still below Capture One's (the export is limited by the graphics card); the CPU speed-ups cut processor time by 16 % but wall time only by about 2 %.
- **Fit view colour noise reduction** matches the export only on Apple silicon and discrete graphics cards; on integrated graphics (a Radeon 680M took 0.9–2.9 s and 1–1.7 GB per change) and on Linux it still uses the quick filter, as does the Library loupe. The Windows timing on a discrete card is not measured yet.
- **Color Grading** still differs from Camera Raw in a few cells (wheels at saturation 100, a green shadow tint in near-black tones); the colour mixer's Blues Saturation +100 and Luminance −100 stay further from Camera Raw than the rest.
- **Windows** stays experimental: unsigned, tested on one NVIDIA card and one AMD Radeon 680M laptop.
- The known gaps of alpha.14 and earlier not named here remain.

## 0.1.0-alpha.14 — 2026-11-14

The fourteenth alpha. Windows on AMD integrated graphics (a Radeon 680M with AMD's Vulkan driver) now works properly: exports of edited photos no longer crash the app, edits respond in about 0.1–0.4 s once their first views are prepared, a JPEG export after editing takes about 5 s instead of about 2 minutes and 16-bit TIFF exports no longer compile a view of their own, and Glow is ready after a few idle minutes. Windows also gets Open With and Edit With, window controls in the top bar with Windows 11's snap layouts, and a Maximize that fills the screen again. Thumbnails, the Library loupe and the Develop fit view now show sharpening and noise reduction closer to the export. For new edits, Dehaze adapts its veil to the photo, Blacks −100 darkens as far as Capture One's, and Lensfun lens profiles correct as Lensfun measured them. Super Resolution on Windows and Linux moves to the graphics card in the same session. Saved edits keep their look (see *Changes to existing edits*).

### Highlights

- **Windows with AMD graphics: exports of edited photos no longer crash the app, and take seconds after editing.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver a full-size export of a photo with many edits (Basic panel, Clarity, Structure, Dehaze, Vibrance, a curve point, a colour-mixer hue, a brush layer) compiled its export view for over 7 minutes, used 52 GB of memory on a 15 GB laptop until the graphics chip stopped responding, and then the app closed without writing the file (Radeon 680M). There, the export view now leaves out the code the photo's edits cannot reach (effects it does not use, older tone versions): that edit's view compiled in 116 s at 9.3 GB instead of failing after more than 300 s, and an unedited RAW's in 6.5 s instead of 98 s (measured with the compile probe). JPEG (8-bit) exports, and likewise 16-bit TIFF exports, Edit With, exports with several recipes and photos whose curve has more than 16 points, no longer compile an export view of their own: they render with the view the editor prepares for the photo's edits (16-bit in full precision), so the export compiles nothing once that view is ready, also after an effect such as Glow is turned off again or another photo was shown, and effects such as Glow on top of many edits now export instead of failing as too complex. When a 16-bit TIFF recipe exists or a 16-bit export ran, the editor prepares that view in idle time after an edit. Measured on a Radeon 680M: the edited photo's JPEG export after editing took 4.8 s at 3.1 GB (was 112 s at 11 GB), the same edit with Glow exported in 60 s; a 16-bit export of an edited photo took about 2 minutes and 9 GB before, and with Glow it failed as too complex. Edits whose export view would still be too large for that driver fail with a clear message instead of minutes of compiling. On every system, a graphics chip that stops responding during an export now ends that export with a clear message (quit, reopen, export again) instead of closing the app.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: every Basic-panel edit is instant after five idle minutes, and edits no longer wait behind a view prepared in idle time.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver the editor prepares views in idle time, once per computer; moving Clarity, Whites or Blacks after Contrast and Highlights needed a view of all those edits at once, which the views prepared in idle time did not cover (Radeon 680M: 86, 97 and 220 s). Once a photo is shown the app now prepares, after the tone sliders, one view for every Basic-panel edit together (all tone sliders in both directions, curve, mask, Clarity, Structure, Dehaze, Vibrance, colour mixer); its tone code is no longer repeated per tone version, so that view compiles in 36 s at 4.3 GB (all tone directions were 131 s at 11 GB). After it the editor also prepares the view with Glow (turning Glow on after editing waited 66 s at 11.7 GB). One preparation takes 25–60 s and could not be stopped, so an edit that needed another view waited for it (17–39 s in the first 90 s after opening a photo); idle preparations now run in a separate helper process that is stopped the moment an edit or an export needs the graphics driver, and the finished view is taken over in about 0.1 s. A view that already serves an edit is no longer followed by compiles of narrower views, which held the GPU for minutes after editing. Idle preparation waits while an export runs (an export had waited 20 s behind one), while the app uses more than 60 % of the computer's memory, or while memory runs short. Measured on a Radeon 680M laptop (15 GB) after 5 minutes idle: every Basic edit in both directions, a curve point, the colour mixer and a brush stroke updated within 0.34 s, and Exposure drags ran at 135–182 frames per second, with no memory pressure; with the helper, edits respond in 0.1–0.4 s except while the view they need is still being prepared for the first time (one edit waited 11.7 s), Glow after idle shows in about 0.1 s, and a stopped preparation gives way within about 1 s. On other graphics drivers nothing changes; exports are unchanged.
- **Windows with an AMD graphics processor: the first start and the first photo are ready in seconds, without filling the memory.** On a Radeon 680M (Vulkan) the app's memory grew to 25–30 GB right after the first shader compile, as AMD's driver compiled several pipelines at once, and the whole PC slowed down; preparing the graphics took 70–97 s at up to 7–8 GB, and opening the first photo another 64–71 s at up to 8.8 GB. On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver Rawtonic now compiles one pipeline at a time (about 8 GB during the first compile and 2 GB after), and the preview compiles only the code a photo uses (no glow, halation or grain code for a photo without them, none of the switched-off tools): the start's graphics are prepared in about 7 s at under 1 GB, the first photo shows its preview after about 1.5 s and settles after about 11 s at 1.6 GB; on a warm start the graphics are ready in a fraction of a second. Closing the window during *Preparing graphics* is no longer taken for a graphics crash at the next start, *Graphics prepared … in N s* is shown when it ends, and when no graphics backend starts the activity centre opens with *Reset graphics settings and retry* in view. Exports are unchanged, byte for byte.
- **Windows: Open With and Edit With.** Both list the programs Windows offers for the file type (the recommended programs of Explorer's *Open with*, such as Photos and Paint, with their icons; the default first), plus *Other…* for any program file (`.exe`), which is remembered and offered from then on. Edit With renders the photos beside their originals as on the Mac, adds them to the library stacked with their originals, opens them in the program and picks up its saves. Paths with spaces and non-Latin letters work; a path of 260 characters or more is handed over in its short form where the volume has one.
- **Windows: the window controls sit in the toolbar.** Minimize, maximize/restore and close are now at the right end of the top bar (as on Linux) instead of in a separate "Rawtonic" strip above it, which freed a row; the empty bar drags the window and a double-click maximizes or restores it. The controls' labels are translated. Hovering over the maximize button opens Windows 11's snap layouts, as in other Windows apps. The window now always opens fully on screen: on a small or highly scaled display (a 1080p laptop at 125 %) it opened taller than the screen with the top bar out of reach; a window too large for the screen now shrinks to it, one placed on a monitor that is gone or partly off screen comes back, and it opens maximized when even its minimum size does not fit.
- **Thumbnails, the Library loupe and the Develop fit view look closer to the export.** They, and the Standard preview, are reduced from the photo with the export's Lanczos filter instead of an area average: unedited, they now carry 0.8–1.3× the fine detail of the export scaled to the same size (before 0.13–1.0×; the fit view looked softer than the export). In these reduced views, Luminance noise reduction and the noise gate of capture sharpening now work from the photo's full-resolution noise level; before, the views kept up to 3–7× the export's luminance noise at high ISO and showed up to 3× the export's sharpening at Radius 2.5. Capture sharpening at Radius up to 1 shows about as the export shows it scaled down (within about ±15 % per detail band); before, Radius 0.5–1 sharpening was missing from every reduced view (its blur fell below one preview pixel) and high Detail softened them instead. Larger radii now show in thumbnails too but are still stronger than in the scaled export (up to about 1.3× at Detail 25, 2× at Detail 100), and colour noise reduction in reduced views still differs from the export (open). Exports are unchanged.
- **Dehaze adapts to the photo, as Lightroom's does** (new edits): lowering Dehaze lifted every photo towards white, so a sunset or a pastel wall under a blue sky turned milky where Camera Raw lays a grey veil below white and even darkens the brightest areas; raising Dehaze darkened bright skies of photos without near-white areas more than Camera Raw. Dehaze now estimates the photo's airlight once from the whole decoded image (the brightest areas of its dark channel, the classic haze estimate), the same for the preview, every export, crop and virtual copy, and lowering veils towards that level while raising fades its darkening relative to it. On 64 photos compared with Camera Raw at the same softening (the 48 of the slider atlas and 16 fresh ones), the largest median difference in any 10-L* tone band went from 2.6 to 1.1 L* at −50 and from 3.3 to 1.4 L* at −100; on the 16 fresh photos from 4.4 to 0.9 and from 6.4 to 2.2 L*. The rule was fitted on all 64 photos; tested on held-out halves it stays within the atlas's amber bound at −50 in 92 % of 200 random splits (version 2: 47 %). Raised Dehaze moved closer to both references (+100: 2.2 to 1.5 L*; fresh photos +50: 4.7 to 3.5) but stays further from them on the fresh photos than on the atlas, and on the atlas's 48 photos +50 moved from 0.9 to 1.2 L*. Photos edited before keep their look (recipe `dehazeVersion`).
- **Blacks −100 darkens the shadows as far as Capture One's −100** (new edits, RAW files): the second half of the Blacks slider did little — Blacks −100 left the darkest tones grey where Capture One's Black −100 and Lightroom's Blacks −100 clearly deepen them (L* 10–30 darkened 9 / 7 L* against Capture One's 14 / 15). Lowered Blacks now moves at Capture One's pace: −50 is unchanged, the old −100 sits at about −72, and −100 deepens the black point twice as far (on the 48 photos of the slider atlas L* 10–30 within about 1 L* of Capture One's −100, the darkest tenth within 0.1; on the 24 not used for the fit within 1.2 / 0.3). Lightroom's −100, which darkens up to the midtones and crushes a quarter of the photo to black, stays deeper. Shadow texture and the share of pure black match Capture One's at the same darkening as before. Earlier edits keep their look; Blacks copied between older and newer edits (tool copy, presets, Edit all selected) keep the darkening they render. Raised Blacks, JPEG/TIFF photos and the inherited looks are unchanged.
- **Lens profiles correct as the Lensfun database measured them** (new lens choices and Auto lens correction): Rawtonic now applies a Lensfun lens profile the way Lensfun itself defines it. Before, the distortion was refitted and rescaled, the colour-fringe correction lost part of its shape, and vignetting took the nearest measured aperture and focus distance instead of blending between measurements. Against Lensfun's own correction on 18 lenses (13 used while building it, 5 checked afterwards), at settings both on and between the measured ones, the corrected geometry now agrees within 0.002 pixel at 24 MP (was up to 15 px with each program's own framing), colour fringes within 0.002 px (was 0.16 px) and the corner brightening within 0.15 % (was up to 49 % between measured apertures and distances). The corrected photo is framed by the largest crop it fills completely, checked at every edge pixel: no empty corners or edges (the old framing could leave a thin black sliver at the top of some wide-angle zooms), and up to 2.6 % more of the photo than a frame kept at Lensfun's scale. The vignetting correction keeps its measured strength (80 % of the profile). Saved edits keep their lens parameters and export exactly as before; the camera's own lens data is unchanged. Updated lens corrections from Lensfun add, among others, Nikkor Z 24-70mm f/2.8 S II, GF 63mm, Tamron 70-300mm, OM System EXIF names and the corrected Nikkor AF 80-400mm profile; already resolved lens corrections and saved diffraction settings keep their previous results.
- **Super Resolution on Windows and Linux moves to the graphics card in the same session** once the idle GPU check finds it faster: the first session on a machine used to stay on the processor until the next start, about 15 minutes for 24 MP on an RTX 3080; on the graphics card the enlargement alone of a 24 MP photo takes 22.6–30.9 s with the default WebGPU path (13.6–15.3 s with the optional CUDA path), plus rendering and writing the file (a complete export from the command line took 46.4 s). Measurement (8-bit output, RTX 3080, a 2048² crop compared with the processor: at most 1/255 difference) is in the maintainers' notes, SRL report 2026-11-12. The processor fallback is faster too: on the laptop, inference for the same 24 MP tile set took 461 → 146 seconds (ONNX Runtime CPU, 117 tiles); the complete export takes longer. Processor-only preparation downloads an additional 2.4 MB model conversion; GPU and Core ML keep their existing models, and older installed packs remain usable.

### Fixes

- **Windows with AMD graphics: Glow, 16-bit TIFF and the AI models no longer crowd a view preparation.** Picking 16-bit TIFF in the export dialog no longer compiles a view for the size estimate (39 s, 7.6 GB); the export's view is prepared in idle time instead. On an integrated AMD chip on AMD's Windows Vulkan driver the AI models now run on the processor without first testing the graphics chip (that test alone took a 15 GB laptop to 10.6 GB, and the chip was slower every time); on other AMD chips the test no longer runs beside a view preparation. Before a preparation, masking models unused for 20 seconds are unloaded (they load again on the next use) and image caches that rebuild on demand are freed. On a Radeon 680M laptop (15 GB) the app with its helper peaks at 6.4 GB while a view is prepared (at most 6.6 GB; the 6 GB goal is not met there). On other graphics drivers nothing changes; exports are unchanged everywhere.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: exporting while a view is prepared needs less memory.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver an export now waits for a running view preparation before it sets up its graphics memory, gives that memory back when the export ends, so it never adds to a later preparation, and the editor prepares nothing in the background for a minute after an export, so a second export does not wait for it (Radeon 680M: a Glow export right after turning Glow on peaked at 6.8 GB, the preparation alone at 5.8 GB). On other graphics drivers exports are unchanged.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: less memory while views are prepared.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver each view the editor prepares costs the graphics driver several gigabytes while it is compiled (Radeon 680M: the app peaked at 7.0 GB while idle and at 8.3 GB exporting a photo with Glow). The editor no longer prepares a view with every effect in idle time, a view with Glow, halation or grain now holds only the effects the photo uses, and an export waits for a running preparation before it allocates its own memory. The editor's views show the same pixels as before; exports on other graphics drivers are unchanged.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: the first Contrast, Highlights or curve edit no longer waits after a few idle minutes.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver the first use of such a slider waited 40–45 s on a Radeon 680M while the driver prepared that view (once per computer). Once a photo is shown, the app now prepares the likely next views (tone sliders, curve, mask, effects, raised Shadows) while nothing else happens, one at a time and always after anything you are waiting for, and shows nothing while it does. Measured on a Radeon 680M after 4 minutes idle (before the helper process above): Contrast, Highlights and a curve point were then instant; touching Contrast while such a preparation was still running waited 16.7 s; Exposure drags stayed at 30–76 frames per second.
- **Windows with AMD graphics: the first tone slider waits about half as long.** On AMD's Windows Vulkan driver the first use of Contrast, Highlights or Whites waited about a minute while the driver prepared that view (once per computer); the view's tone code is now shaped so the driver compiles it in about half the time and memory (Radeon 680M: 54 → 28 s; raised Shadows 174 → 58 s). Exports are unchanged.
- **Windows with an AMD graphics processor: the first tone edit waits half as long.** On a Radeon 680M (Vulkan) the preview of the first Contrast, Shadows, Whites or Blacks edit of a session (and of a Highlights, curve or mask edit after it) waited 95–135 s while the graphics driver prepared it, at up to 14 GB of memory. The preview now leaves out code a photo cannot reach (tone versions it does not use, among them the old tone response of edits from before tone version 6): those previews are prepared in 56–74 s at 5–6 GB, the start in about 7 s. Exports are unchanged, byte for byte. (The idle-time preparation in the Highlights shortens this further.)
- Windows with AMD graphics: switching to a photo with the same edits but other slider responses or another look now prepares that photo's idle-time views instead of keeping the previous photo's plan. The display's tone math is checked against exports for every tone version, slider and look (within one 8-bit level), and the preview no longer reuses a cached Clarity blur across RAW and non-RAW renders of one image.
- **A long graphics compile is shown instead of a panel that looks dead.** On some graphics drivers (a Radeon 680M under Windows: 65–90 s) a new kind of edit, a newly opened photo or an edited photo's thumbnail waits for a shader compile. Every compile that takes longer than a moment now shows *Preparing graphics for this computer* in the activity centre and over the photo until it ends, and its start is written to the log; a thumbnail that cannot be made says why in the log.
- **Windows: the installers finish in seconds, and the first start shows its graphics preparation.** The NSIS installer and the MSI no longer compile the GPU shaders after copying the files (on a Radeon 680M that ran over two minutes behind a busy cursor, then ended without a word). The first start compiles them instead and shows *Preparing graphics for this computer* in the activity centre while it takes longer than a moment, then *Graphics prepared … in N s* under Done.
- **Windows: no more "Could not lock adapter context" at start.** OpenGL is no longer used on Windows: wgpu's OpenGL backend there stops the app when a shader compile holds the graphics context for more than a second, which our processing shader does. A start that used to fall back to OpenGL now uses DirectX 12 or Vulkan; an OpenGL choice in Preferences becomes Auto, and the window says so once. The start guard never leaves out every graphics backend after crashes (only the newest crash's), and when no backend starts the window offers *Reset graphics settings and retry*. Headless runs (`rawtonic export`, `--warm-gpu-cache`) follow the guard but are never counted as a start crash when they are ended.
- **Crash reports from Windows name each frame by module and offset** (`rawtonic-app.exe+0x…`) instead of the misleading nearest export names (`onig_*`); each release's symbols are kept privately to resolve them.
- **Windows: Maximize at 125 % interface size on a small screen is a real maximize again.** Changing the window minimum while the window was maximized took it out of maximize at its maximized size, so on a 1280×752 work area at 125 % Maximize left a normal window hanging off the right edge (Close clipped) and Restore did nothing. The minimum now changes only in the normal window state (checked together with the change), the maximized window is the monitor's work area, and the minimum never exceeds it; if the top bar no longer fits, its actions become icons before the window controls are cut. Add to library… without a library offers to create or open one and then adds the photo; the Library Navigator follows the Library selection after a loose open or Show in library; an unsupported interface size in the settings file is written to app.log once at start. Native verification at 125 % on the reporting laptop pending.
- Windows restore placement is read from the native normal rectangle and retained when reopening maximized; maximize-button clicks capture their release across snap-flyout dismissal. Window restore bounds survive quitting maximized, and the maximize-button placement refreshes after compact-bar and interface-style changes. Session log lines are flushed individually without waiting on a console reader (checked on the reporting laptop).
- The Develop Adjust column now fills its panel through explicit flex sizing at every interface size, so every tool is reachable on Windows; wheel and scrollbar scrolling were checked on the reporting laptop.
- Windows and Linux show platform shortcut labels, scale the top bar with the interface, keep photo dimensions beside shortened filenames, and enforce a usable minimum window size. Plain Ctrl+C in Develop copies the remembered settings choice; Ctrl+Shift+C opens the picker. Catalog scans leave exports in Output out of the photo count, and thumbnail events update tiles listed through another Windows path spelling.
- Windows/Linux interactive minimum window dimensions follow the interface size (100/112/125 %), capped to the monitor work area. Windows programmatic window resizes respect the current interface size and are clamped below the existing Windows/Linux 1000×600 logical window floor (a 960-pixel half-screen tile at 100% DPI is smaller than this minimum). When enforcing the interface minimum makes the window grow past the screen, it is moved back inside the monitor work area; the minimum-size correction is deferred and skips maximized windows and superseded resizes.
- Windows/Linux shortcut tooltips include component-provided hints, Preferences accepts comma events without a physical key code, and shortcut hints preserve file and tool names containing Mac key symbols; Windows/Linux reference hints say Enter. Linux uses neutral file-manager wording, and thumbnail path aliases are cached per library list. The photo count follows the loaded grid; Windows/Linux footer space favors readable dimensions and the close button has darker pressed feedback.
- Preferences opens above the welcome screen, with a visible welcome entry, and an externally opened photo dismisses welcome; Windows snap-layout controls stay inactive behind modal dialogs. Activity indicators stay clickable in narrow headers, and the privacy acknowledgement survives replacement of settings.
- Edit With now groups renders with their originals in grid stacks, uses the render time for new files, and retries only unfinished photos after quitting. It offers recovery even when quitting during the final render or before handoff, and repairs completed renders’ stacks at restart. Cancelling individual Edit With renders is reported neutrally, separately from failed renders. Windows adds handling for app execution aliases in Other… (native verification pending) and uses program display names in rendering and interruption notices.
- The app keeps four previous session logs locally for support and crash diagnosis; crash reports still include only the scrubbed tail shown for review.
- Loose photos opened in Develop appear alone and highlighted in the filmstrip, with an explicit library status and an Add to library action that opens import for review; Develop retains the filmstrip count for externally opened photos. Returning to Library restores its photos. Adding an externally opened photo follows its library copy and refreshes library counts. The Navigator clears stale loose photos and retains a loose photo when there is no listed Library selection, and confirmed duplicates offer Show in library.
- Photos passed at startup open in Develop after the remembered library finishes restoring; launching with a folder opens it for browsing in the Library. Settings accept a UTF-8 BOM and snap interface sizes to the supported choices.
- **Windows: paths read as you know them, and long or busy files are handled.** Photo, folder and volume paths are no longer shown or stored as `\\?\C:\…` (a drive showed as `\\?\C:\`, and an import destination could become invalid with *The filename, directory name, or volume label syntax is incorrect*). Moves and renames deeper than 260 characters work; an XMP sidecar named `.XMP` keeps its spelling when Rawtonic updates it; a sidecar a virus scanner or the search indexer is reading is written once it lets go (or the write fails saying the file is in use, the old one untouched), and a read-only sidecar is refused instead of failing after the work; Import from Catalog no longer leaves its snapshot in the catalog's `.cache`. Your photo files were never affected.
- **Windows: picks and rejects are there again after a restart.** The library names its photos `\\?\C:\…` on Windows, the folder shown after a restart lists them as `C:\…`: the Pick and Reject counters read 0 and the flags were not shown, although they were saved. Flags are now found under either form, and a rating or label changed in such a view shows at once. Star ratings, edits and your photo files were never affected.
- **Windows: the AI tools no longer crash the app on PCs with an old Visual C++ runtime.** Choosing Brush or another AI masking tool could close Rawtonic within seconds (access violation in `MSVCP140.dll`) where Windows only had an older Microsoft C++ runtime: ONNX Runtime needs a current one. The installers and the portable zip now bring the current runtime beside ONNX Runtime, without administrator rights or a separate install, and if an older one is still in use the AI tools say the AI runtime is unavailable instead of crashing.
- **Windows: the AI tools can use the graphics card.** ONNX Runtime's GPU plugin needs Microsoft's DirectX Shader Compiler on Direct3D 12, which Windows does not include, so the AI tools always ran on the processor. The installers and the portable zip now bring it (`dxcompiler.dll`, `dxil.dll`, Microsoft's release 1.8.2502) beside ONNX Runtime; if it is missing, the AI tools still run on the processor and the log says why.
- **AI runs on the graphics card only where that is faster.** The one-time check of each AI model on the graphics card now also times it against the processor and keeps the faster one (on an integrated Radeon 680M, AI Subject took 14–19 s on the graphics card against 8.5 s on the processor). An AI Denoise tile that hangs on the graphics card no longer stops AI Denoise: after a time limit it goes on on the processor.
- **The first AI click no longer waits for that check.** The app used to time a model on the graphics card against the processor before its first use, so the first AI Subject click on a Radeon 680M took 110 s, 49 s of them the measurement. A model not yet checked now runs on the processor at once, and the check runs later in the background while nothing else happens: it stops the moment you edit, use an AI tool or export, never runs during an export, and is dropped when you quit. If the graphics card turns out clearly faster (at most 85 % of the processor's time, and a first run after starting the app at most 20 % slower), the model moves there; until then and everywhere else, the processor is used.
- **A corrected capture time now travels in the XMP sidecar.** *Shift Capture Time* writes the corrected time as `exif:DateTimeOriginal` and `photoshop:DateCreated` (with fraction of a second and UTC offset when known; no offset is invented) into the photo's XMP sidecar, so Lightroom, Capture One and other libraries see it, as with Lightroom's Edit Capture Time; *Back to the time in the file* and Undo remove exactly the dates Rawtonic wrote. The other way round, a sidecar whose capture time differs from the file's — for example after Lightroom's Edit Capture Time — becomes the photo's capture-time correction when it is imported or read with *Use XMP*; equal times change nothing.
